He came in the company of **Sultan Masud, son of Sultan Mahmud** — "یہ بزرگ سلطان مسعود سلطان محمود کے بیٹے کے ہمراہ لاہور مین آیا" — and "سنہ ۴۳۱ ہجری مین اس نے اپنے قدوم [OCR?] میمنت لزوم سے لاہور کو مشرف کیا اور سنہ ۴۶۵ مین مر گیا چونتیس برس برابر لاہور مین رہ کر ظاہری باطنی علم پہیلایا" — honoured Lahore with his arrival in **431 AH**, died in **465**, and spread outward and inward knowledge through **thirty-four years** in Lahore; **neither of these two year-numerals carries an `[OCR?]` flag** (p. 289, folio 292). His book: "کتاب کشف المحجوب بزبان فارسی تصوف کے علم مین اس نے ایسی لکہی ہے کہ اُسکا ثانی روئے زمین پر نہین ہے" — **Kashf al-Mahjub** in Persian on tasawwuf, with no equal on the face of the earth, and many books in **Arabic[OCR?] and Persian** (p. 291, folio 294).
- **Shrine / Darbar.** "یہ متبرک و قدیمی مقبرہ لاہور کے مشہور و مزین مزارون و مقبرون سے ہے دور دور سے مسلمان معتقد لوگ اس مزار کی زیارت کو آتے ہین" — among Lahore's famous and adorned mazars, with Muslim devotees coming from far for its ziyarat (p. 288, folio 291). Layout: the enclosure's door is **south**; inside, a stone-threshold gate to the west with a deorhi behind it; through it, from the eastern gate, a square courtyard with houses and dalans on all four sides; "جنوبی سمت مجاور رہتے ہین", the **mujawirs live on the southern side**; in the courtyard's middle an octagonal[OCR?] platform (ہشتمین [OCR?] چبوترہ) carrying the mazar — "چبوترہ سنگ مرمر کا ہے اوپر بہی فرش سنگ مرمر کا قبر بہی ایک ٹکڑہ سنگ مرمر کی بنی ہے", the platform of **marble**, the floor above it marble, **the grave made of a single piece of marble** — with a wooden lattice on all eight sides (pp. 289–290, folios 292–293).
- **A dated dome:** "پہلے اوپر مزار کے گنبد نہ تہا مگر سنہ ۱۲۴۷ [OCR?] ہجری مین مسمی نور محمد سماوہ [OCR?] نے یہ گنبد بنوایا تہا جو اب موجود ہے" — formerly there was **no dome**; in **1247[OCR?] AH** one **Nur Muhammad Samawa[OCR?]** had it built, round and very beautiful, with mirrors fitted below and above the lattice and the southern door kept (p. 290, folio 293).
- **A third date that does not agree with the other two, kept as printed.** A gilded inscription over the inner gate, "اندر کے دروازے کے سنہری یہ شعر لکہے ہین", three Persian couplets **every line of which is OCR-flagged**, beginning "خانقاہ علی ہجویری سے [OCR?]", under which stands "۳۷۶ [OCR?]" (p. 289, folio 292) — **a numeral that does not agree with the 431 and 465 printed on the same page, and is not reconciled**.
- **The mosque:** at the enclosure's south-west corner, "یہ مسجد حضرت نے خود بنوائی تہی مگر اب سنہ ۱۸۶۸ [OCR?] ع مین دوبارہ بنائی گئی ہے" — **Hazrat built it himself**, rebuilt in **1868[OCR?] AD** (p. 290, folio 293). Outside the enclosure to the south, many pakka mazars, a mosque, and "بہت سی نشستگاہ مین مجاورون کی", many **sitting-places of the mujawirs** (p. 291, folio 294).
- **Shaikh Imam-ud-Din's adjoining enclosure:** from the northern dalan a path leads to it, "دیوار بدیوار" wall-to-wall with Hazrat's enclosure on east and south, "نہایت مکلف بنا ہے"; "قبر نو اب [OCR?] شیخ امام الدین کی سنگ مرمر کی بنی ہے", the grave of Nawab[OCR?] Shaikh Imam-ud-Din of **marble**, with Persian couplets on its southern wall ("دریغا کہ بے ما بسے روزگار…"); built after his death by his son "نواب غلام محبوب [OCR?] سجہانی [OCR?]", and "احمد الدین بخش [OCR?] نام کا [illegible] نے اس قبر کو بنایا"; chronogram verses stand on the stone at the grave's chest[OCR?] ("چونکہ نواب شیخ امام الدین / شد ز دنیا و رو بخلد نہاد…") (p. 290, folio 293; p. 291, folio 294).
- **Shrine / Darbar — the contents locator, and the book's own classification.** "مزار داتا گنج بخش لاہوری[OCR?]" at printed page ۲۹۱ (p. 4, folio 3), inside the qism of buildings **outside** Lahore connected with Islam, begun at printed page ۲۳۶[OCR?] — so by the book's own classification this is an *outside-the-city* building (p. 4, folio 3). "داتا گنج بخش" is unflagged; "لاہوری[OCR?]" is flagged (p. 4, folio 3).
- **Practices & Events — royal Qurans kept at the mazar:** "بادشاہان سلف بہی کمال ادب اس مزار کا کرتے تہے چنانچہ سلطان ابراہیم غزنوی اور سلطان شمس الدین التمش وغیرہ بادشاہون کے ہاتہہ کے لکہے ہوئے قرآن شریف اس مزار پر موجود ہین" — Qurans **written by the hands of Sultan Ibrahim Ghaznavi and Sultan Shams-ud-Din Altamsh** and other kings are present (pp. 288–289, folios 291–292); they are kept on **rahls (رحلون)** in the courtyard's rooms, "اور لوگ اُنکو پڑہتے رہتے ہین" (p. 290, folio 293).
- **Seventy-plus mujawirs dividing offerings in equal shares:** "اس مزار کے مجاور بہت سے ہین گنتی ستر آدمیون سے زیادہ ہے آمدنی چڑہاوے کی وہ حصہ مساوی تقسیم کرتے ہین" (p. 291, folio 294).
- **Three fixed gatherings and a weekly vigil:** "اس مزار پر آٹہوین [OCR?] روز بروز جمعہ میلا ہوتا ہے اور خلقت کثیر جمع ہوتی ہے بڑا میلا یعنی عرس ماہ صفر کی ۱۸ [OCR?] تاریخ کو ہوتا ہے اور دوسرا میلہ آخری چہار شنبہ کے روز" — a mela on the **eighth[OCR?] day, on Friday**; the **great mela, i.e. the urs, on the 18th[OCR?] of Safar**; and a **second mela on the day of the Last Wednesday (آخری چہار شنبہ)**, both drawing people from far; and "ہر جمعرات کی رات کو بہی بہت بہت ہے لوگ شب بیدار رہتے ہین اور تمام رات عبادت مین مصروف رہتے ہین" — **every Thursday night** people keep vigil and remain in worship all night (p. 291, folio 294).
- **A Maharaja who paid inward and stripped outward:** "مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگہ اگرچہ ادب اس مزار کا بہت کرتا تہا اور [OCR?] ہزارون روپیہ اندرونے [OCR?] کے [OCR?] بہیجتا تہا مگر باہر کی عمارت اُس نے بہی ایک نہ چہوڑی سب کے پتہر اور دروازے انکی بنیادین [OCR?] زمین سے نکلوا دین صرف مزار کا مکان باقی رہ گیا" — he **sent thousands of rupees for the interior[OCR?]** yet **left not one of the outer buildings**, having their stones, doors and foundations[OCR?] taken out of the ground, only the mazar's own building remaining (p. 289, folio 292). Also in its vicinity: "روضہ و مسجد شاہ اشرف کی اسی کے جوار مین تہی مگر سب کی سب سکہون کی تعدی [OCR?] مین" — the **rauza and mosque of Shah Ashraf** stood nearby, but all fell in the Sikhs' oppression[OCR?] (p. 289, folio 292).
- **Legends & Miracles — chillas kept at the mazar by later saints:** "بہت سے بزرگون مثل خواجہ معین الدین چشتی اجمیری و خواجہ فرید پاک پٹنی وغیرہ نے یہان آکر چلے کاٹے اور فیض پایا تہا" (p. 289, folio 292). "اسکی لاکہون کرامتین کتابون مین درج ہین" — his lakhs of karamat are recorded in books; **none is narrated** (p. 289, folio 292).
- **Significance.** The author defers to books and then endorses them: "دارا شکوہ [OCR?] نے اپنی کتاب سفینۃ الاولیا اور مولانا جامی اپنی کتاب نفحات الانس مین اس بزرگ کی تعریف بحد کمال لکہتے ہین اور بیشک یہ بزرگ ایسا ہی ہوگا جیسی اسکی تعریف کتابون مین درج ہے" (p. 289, folio 292). The mazar is also used as a bearing for other sites: the Shia Imambara is placed "بیرون دروازہ بہاٹی … گنج بخش کے مزار پر انوار کے جنوب کی سمت کو" (p. 301, folio 304).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "پنجاب کا علاقہ", "پنجاب"; refers to: the time of "راجہ دیب چند" of Delhi, before Islam, period otherwise not stated; quoted source: "شیخ احمد زنجانی صاحب رسالہ تحفۃ الواصلین", which the book sets against "صاحب خلاصۃ التواریخ"): when Raja Deb Chand sat on the throne of Delhi "اُس نے پنجاب کا علاقہ [illegible] برادر [illegible] لوہار چند کی جاگیر میں دیا جب اُسکا کامل تسلط پنجاب پر ہو گیا تو اُس نے اس شہر کو دارالحکومت بنایا" (p. 14, folio 9).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: Sultan Mahmud's invasions and Malik Ayaz's government; the first attack on Lahore is dated "سنہ ۳۹۱[OCR?] ہجری" at p. 22, folio 17; book's historical prose): "سلطان محمود و سبکتگین غزنوی نے پنجاب پر حملہ کیا" when "راجہ جیپال برہمن پنجاب کا فرمان فرما لاہور میں صاحب تخت و تاج تھا" (p. 14, folio 9); Mahmud wished to oust Raja Jaipal from Lahore "اور پنجاب کا علاقہ اپنے ماتحت کر لے", and after the sack "ملک ایاز[OCR?] پنجاب کے انتظام کے لئے نامور ہوا" (p. 17, folio 12); restated as "جب ملک ایاز بادشاہ کے حکم سے پنجاب کا حاکم بنا" (p. 23, folio 18).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the last Ghaznavids, "خسرو شاہ" and his son "خسرو ملک", against "سلطان شہاب الدین غوری", date not stated; book's historical prose): when the Ghorids drove the dynasty from Ghazni "خسرو شاہ خسرو ملک کے باپ نے پنجاب میں اپنی حکومت قایم کر لی"; Khusrau Malik sat on the throne of Lahore "اور پنجاب سے تھا [illegible] اُسکی حکومت قایم ہو گئی"; with his surrender "سلاطین غوریہ کی سلطنت کا ہند میں آغاز ہوا اور غزنویہ حکومت با اختتام پہنچی" (p. 24, folio 19).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: Taj al-Din Yalduz after the death of "شہاب الدین", and again in the reign of "سلطان شمس الدین التمش", date not stated; book's historical prose): Yalduz seized Ghazni, "پھر پنجاب کو آیا" to take the throne of Hind from Qutb al-Din; in his second rising he took Multan, came to Lahore "اور اُس نے دل کھول کر پنجاب کو غارت کیا اور ستلج سے اُتر کر" advanced towards Delhi (p. 25, folio 20).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "سلطان جلال الدین خوارزمی" fleeing Chingiz Khan, and the later invasion by "چنگیز کے پوتے قلی خان", date not stated; quoted source: the book reports the author of "تحفتہ الواصلین" ("اسکا بیان ہے")): Jalal al-Din "سندہ کا دریا اتر کر پنجاب میں دخل ہوا"; "جب چنگیز کے پوتے قلی خان نے پنجاب پر فوج کشی کی تو مقام لاہور دہلی کی فوج اور مغلون میں لڑائی ہوئی" (p. 165, folio 168).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "تمام خطہ"; refers to: "سلطان جلال الدین فیروز شاہ خلجی کے وقت", the fourth blow; book's historical prose): a Mughal army sent by "[illegible] جغتائی خان [OCR?] حاکم ماوراء النہر" came into the Punjab; "اس وقت پنجاب میں قحط [OCR?] ہوا تہا [illegible] غارت و تاراج نے تمام خطہ کو برباد کر دیا [illegible] گاؤں اور قصبے ویران ہو گئے"; the sultan, named in the same passage as "شمس الدین التمش [OCR?]", was on the Gujarat campaign and "پنجاب کی امداد وہ کچھ نہ کر سکا" (p. 26, folio 29); after the victory on the Sutlej "سلطان نے پنجاب کی بستیوں کو جو محض غیر آباد و بے چراغ ہو گئی تھیں پھر آباد کیا"، distributed the Tatar loot "پنجاب کی غریب رعایا پر" "اور بڑی فوج سرحد پر مامور کی" (p. 27, folio 30).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "پنجاب کے حکام"; refers to: "سلطان محمد تغلق کے وقت", the fifth blow; book's historical prose): a Tatar army "غرب کی طرف سے آ کر پنجاب میں دخل ہو کے دیپالپور و لاہور دونو شہر غارت ہوئے"; "پنجاب کے حکام نے بہت کوشش کی" but fled to Delhi; when the army had left "بادشاہ پنجاب میں آیا رعایا کی آبادی میں بہت کوشش کی" and beheaded "بہرام [OCR?] نام حاکم ملتان" because the Tatar army had come into the Punjab at his signal (p. 27, folio 30).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "بعہد سلطنت فیروز شاہ [illegible] تغلق", the sixth blow; book's historical prose): the Tatar Mughal army "غارت و تاراج کے لئے پنجاب میں آ موجود ہوا"; the king, then besieging "ملتان [OCR?] سری نگر [OCR?] کا [illegible]", "پھر پنجاب میں آیا اور ایک خونخوار جنگ کے بعد تاتاری لشکر کو پنجاب سے نکالا" (pp. 27–28, folios 30–31).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "پنجاب کے دامن کوہ"; refers to: "سلطان محمد شاہ بن فیروز شاہ بارک [OCR?] کے عہد میں" and Amir Timur's invasion, the seventh blow; book's historical prose): "مسمی سیکھا [OCR?] قوم گھکڑ [OCR?]" came "ہزارہ [OCR?] کے متعلق علاقہ سے بڑے اجتماع کے ساتھ دخل [OCR?] پنجاب ہو کر شاہی املاک ملک سے نکال دیے ہزاروں بستیاں اجاڑ دیں"; Muhammad Shah sent "شاہزادہ ہمایوں" with an army "پنجاب کو"; "امیر تیمور پنجاب کے دامن کوہ سے گزر کر دہلی پر دخیل ہو گیا"; on Timur's return "[illegible] پنجاب نے سیکھا [OCR?] کے قتل و تاراج کا استغاثہ امیر کے حضور میں کیا" (p. 28, folio 31).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "سلطان مبارک شاہ بن خضر خان کے عہد میں", after "۸۲۰ [OCR?] ہجری", the eighth blow; book's historical prose): "جسرت [OCR?] سیکھا [OCR?] گھکڑ [OCR?] کے بھائی نے بھی پنجاب میں بہت فسدہ [OCR?] پا کیا [OCR?]"; the king ordered "سلطان شاہ لودی حاکم سرہند" "کہ اپنی فوج لے کر جسرت [OCR?] کو پنجاب سے نکال دیوے مگر" — the sentence breaks off at the page seam (p. 29, folio 32).
- (book's word: "پنجاب کا علاقہ", "پنجاب [OCR?] کے جاگیر دار", "پنجاب کی حکومت"; refers to: Humayun's reign, and Shah Jahan's reign, as the book states; book's historical prose): Humayun attached Langar Khan to "شہزادہ کامران اپنے بھائی کے جسکی جاگیر میں پنجاب کا علاقہ تھا" (p. 76, folio 79); in Shah Jahan's reign "شہزادہ دارا شکوہ پنجاب [OCR?] کے جاگیر دار" laid out the Chauk Dara Shikoh (p. 91, folio 94), and "بوقت سلطنت شاہجہان بادشاہ کے جب پنجاب کی حکومت دارہ شکوہ کو تفویض ہوئی تو اُس نے عمارتین بہت کین" (p. 139, folio 142).
- (book's word: "دیوان صوبہ پنجاب", "حکام پنجاب", "نائب صوبہ وسر و فتر[OCR?] خطہ پنجاب"; refers to: Shah Jahan's reign; the time of Zakariya Khan and Shahnawaz Khan, date not stated; Muhammad Shah's reign, "عہد میر معین الملک المشہور میر منو"; the unit is the Mughal suba, carried as the book words it; book's historical prose): "منشی محمد صالح دیوان صوبہ پنجاب [illegible]" built the mosque completed "سنہ ۱۰۵۸[OCR?]" (p. 152, folio 155); "نواب ذکریا خان بہادر و شہنواز خان وغیرہ حکام پنجاب" lived in Mughalpura (p. 90, folio 93); "میر سید بہکاری[OCR?] خان" was "محمد شاہ بادشاہ کے حکم سے بعہدہ نائب صوبہ وسر و فتر[OCR?] خطہ پنجاب کے ممتاز" (p. 146, folio 149).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the end of Muslim rule and the rise of the Sikhs, date not stated; book's historical prose): "جب سلطنت اسلام کی پنجاب سے جاتی رہی اور سکہیوں نے زور پکڑا تو سب سے پہلے سکہیوں نے اسی کو غارت کیا" — the butchers' quarter of Lahore (pp. 89–90, folios 92–93).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "سکھون کے وقت", the Sikh period; book's historical prose): "سکھون کے وقت چھاپے کی کوئی کتاب ہندوستان سے پنجاب مین نہین آتی تھی قلمی کتابین کاتب لوگ لکھکر فروخت کرتے تہے" (p. 42, folio 45).
- (book's word: "خطہ پنجاب", "والی پنجاب", "تمام خطہ پنجاب", "پنجاب"; refers to: Ranjit Singh's reign, death given as "۱۸۹۶ [OCR?] مہینہ ہگرمی [OCR?]", era not stated in the sentence; author's preface (p. 9) and book's historical prose): Ranjit Singh is "مہاراجہ فرماں فرما کے خطہ پنجاب" (p. 9, folio 4); under "مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگہ کی حکومت مین تمام خطہ پنجاب کا آ گیا" (p. 112, folio 115); "اس مہاراجہ نے چالیس سال تک پنجاب میں حکومت کی سوا اسکے پنجاب کے اور ملک کشمیر و دیرہ جات وغیرہ بھی اُسکے تصرف میں تہا" (p. 217, folio 220); the infantry of "مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگہ والی پنجاب", "جسکی تعداد و ستر ہزار سے کم نہ تہی", drew its pay through Bakhshi Bhagat Ram (p. 96, folio 99).
- (book's word: "تمام پنجاب"; refers to: Ranjit Singh's reign, the mosque dated "سنہ ١٢٢٣[OCR?] ہجری"; book's historical prose): Ranjit Singh struck coin in the name of Moran the courtesan, "تمام پنجاب میں اسکا سکہ جاری ہو گیا", and "مورانشاہی[OCR?]" rupees and paisa are still kept as keepsakes (p. 179, folio 182); the same is said at "روپیے پیسے پر بھی اس کی ضرب چلکتی [OCR?] تہی موران شاہی پیسے اور روپیے اب تک موجود مین" (p. 47, folio 50).
- (book's word: "پنجاب کی حکومت", "والی پنجاب", "شاہنشاہ خطہ پنجاب"; refers to: the reigns after Ranjit Singh's death, dates not stated; book's historical prose): "مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگہ کے مرنے کے بعد اُسکا حقیقی بیٹا مہاراجہ کہڑک سنگہ پنجاب کی حکومت کے تختہ پر بیٹھا" (p. 221, folio 224); "مہاراجہ شیر سنگہ والی پنجاب", of "اپنی اڑہائی سالہ سلطنت", is "اُس شاہنشاہ خطہ پنجاب" (p. 210, folio 213).
- (book's word: "تمام پنجاب"; refers to: Ranjit Singh's reign; reported speech of "فقیر صاحبان" to the Maharaja, as the book gives it): pleading for the mosque by the Baoli, the Fakirs said "تمام پنجاب کی مسجدون کے ملا کہبین[OCR?] بانگ بلند آواز سے نہین کہتے", and offered a bond that the mulla would not give the call (p. 148, folio 151).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "پنجاب کا ملک"; refers to: the end of Sikh rule, the British annexation, "سنہ ۱۸۴۷ع" given for John Lawrence as acting Resident; book's historical prose): "بعد [illegible] سلطنت سکھی کے جب صاحبان انگریز پنجاب کے مالک ہوئے" (p. 33, folio 36); Bakhshi Bhagat Ram's jagir had not yet been released "کہ پنجاب کا ملک سرکار نے ضبط کر لیا", after which "چیف کمشنر صاحب" fixed his pension (p. 68, folio 71); "جب مہاراجہ دلیپ سنگہ جلاوطن ہوئے اور کامل انگریزی حکومت پنجاب مین ہوگئی" the troops left the Badshahi Mosque (p. 141, folio 144).
- (book's word: "صوبہ پنجاب", "حاکم کلکٹر پنجاب", "ممالک پنجاب"; refers to: British rule in the author's own day; ethnographic present): Lahore is "صوبہ پنجاب کا دار الحکومت"; the court of the Lieutenant-Governor and his secretaries is there and "اس محکمہ کی حکومت تمام صوبہ پنجاب پر ہی۔ رئیس اور راجے مہاراجے کمشنر دیہٹی[OCR?] کمشنر وغیرہ سب ماتحت میں"; the second court is "فنانشل کمشنر بہا[در] اور حاکم کلکٹر پنجاب"; the Chief Court gives final decision in civil and criminal cases, "کمشنریان صوبہ پنجاب کا اپیل اسی محکمہ میں ہوتا ہی", and capital cases need its sanction (p. 60, folio 63); the book names "اجرٹن صاحب بہادر لفٹننٹ گورنر ممالک پنجاب" (p. 148, folio 151) and "مکلوڈ [OCR?] صاحب بہادر لفٹننٹ گورنر سابق پنجاب" (p. 213, folio 216).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "چند سال ہوئے", a few years before writing, date not stated; ethnographic present): "چند سال ہوئے کہ کشمیر میں قحط پر گیا۔ ہزار ہا قحط زدہ کشمیری پنجاب میں آئے", hundreds of them reaching Lahore itself (p. 75, folio 78).
- (book's word: "پنجاب کے امرا"; refers to: the author's own day, contrasted with "سکھوں کے وقت"; ethnographic present): silk drawstrings went out of use under English rule and everyone wears cotton, "البتہ پنجاب کے امرا اب بھی ریشمی ازار بند پہننے کے عادی ہین" (p. 47, folio 50).
- (book's word: "پنجاب کے ملک", "زبان پنجابی", "پنجابیوں"; refers to: the author's own day; author's preface): Heer and Ranjha is the story "جو پنجاب کے ملک میں نیک و بد خورد و کلاں امیر فقیر کی زبان پر ہے"; many books on it exist "بزبان پنجابی" "مگر سوائے[OCR?] پنجابیوں کے اور کوئی صاحب زبان اُس سے مستفید نہیں ہو سکتا تھا", which is why he wrote his version in Persian (p. 10, folio 5).


- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "سنہ ۶۱۵ ہجری", the Mongol pursuit of Sultan Jalal al-Din Khwarazmi — as the book states; book's historical prose continuing the account it introduces as what "مصنف حدیقتہ الاولیا بحوالہ تذکرہ حاکمیہ[OCR?] لکہتا ہے", in the entry "حال مزارات بی بی پاک دامناں"): "آخر جب سنہ ۶۱۵ ہجری میں کفار مغل نے بتعاقب سلطان جلال الدین خوارزمی کے پنجاب پر لشکر کشی کی اور لاہور کی رعایا بجرم مقابلہ و مجادلہ کے قتل ہو لی[OCR?]" — the setting for the Bibis' prayer to be taken into the earth (p. 305, folio 308).
- (book's word: "صوبہ پنجاب"; refers to: "شاہجہان بادشاہ کے عہد میں نواب وزیر خان کی نظامت کے وقت", the Mughal suba as the book names it; book's historical prose, entry "اندہی حویلی"): the haveli was built by "ایک ہند و امیر … جو صوبہ پنجاب کا دیوان تہا", and the builder's descendants held it "اخیر سلطنت چغتائی تک" (p. 336, folio 343).
- (book's word: "زکریا خان صوبہ پنجاب"; refers to: "در دور شاہ محمد شاہ", as the inscription states; quoted source — a "کانسی کار" tile inscription on the north mihrab of "مسجد نواب زکریا خان" west of Madho Lal Hussain's mazar, copied by the author): the verse names its builder "زبدۂ بارگاہ او نواب   زکریا خان صوبہ پنجاب"; the book's own prose heading the same entry calls him "نواب زکریا خان [margin] زکریا خان [OCR?] المعروف خان بہادر صوبہ لاہور" (pp. 262–263, folios 265–266).
- (book's word: "دیوان سلطنت پنجاب"; refers to: "نواب زکریا خان بہادر صوبہ لاہور کے وقت" — as the book states; book's historical prose, entry "حویلی دیوان لکہپت رائے و جسپت رائے"): the brothers "دیوان لکہپت رائے و جسپت رائے … دیوان سلطنت پنجاب تہے", and of their wealth: at his son's wedding Lakhpat Rai distributed in Lahore to every Hindu and Muslim "ایک ایک گنڈو[OCR?] ٹکا[OCR?] اٹہائی[OCR?] ٹہائی[OCR?] سیر وزن کہانڈ[OCR?] کا" (p. 337, folio 344).
- (book's word: "پنجاب کا انتظام", "افسر فوج پنجاب"; refers to: "محمد شاہ بادشاہ کے وقت" and after Ahmad Shah Durrani's defeat and return to Kabul — as the book states; book's historical prose, in the account of Jani Khan buried at this tomb): Jani Khan, with "اپنے بہائی میر معین الملک", defeated "احمد شاہ درانی" "اور وہ واپس کابل کو چلا گیا اسکے جانے کے بعد پنجاب کا انتظام میر معین الملک نے اپنے طور پر کیا اور جانی خان افسر فوج پنجاب مقرر رہا"; Jani Khan's death is printed "سنہ ۱۱۹۴ [OCR?]" (p. 270, folio 273).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "تمام خطہ پنجاب"; refers to: the coming of Sikh rule, "جب سکہی عمارت گری کا وقت آیا" / "جب زمانہ کے انقلاب نے اسلامیہ سلطنت کو زیر و زبر کر دیا اور سکہہ لاہور پر قابض ہوئے", date not stated; book's historical prose): a severe famine — "پنجاب میں سخت قحط پڑ گیا", after which the Andhi Haveli builder's descendants left Lahore (p. 336, folio 343); and "تمام خطہ پنجاب کا قحط کے سبب سے ویران ہو گیا", after which Mir Jawad's descendants left Lahore and "حکام" took the haveli (p. 342, folio 349).
- (book's word: "سلطنت پنجاب"; refers to: after "وفات مہاراجہ کہڑک سنگہ اور کنور نو نہال سنگہ", date not stated; book's historical prose, entry "مکان سمادہ رانی راجکوران المشہور رانی نکائین و رانی چند کنور"): "رانی چند کنور … کی مالک سلطنت پنجاب کی ہوئی اور سرداران سندہانوالیہ کی تجویز سے گدی پر بیٹہلا دی گئی", so that "مختار کار سلطنت کے سرداران سندہانوالیہ" became, and "راجہ دہیان سنگہ وزیر ناراض ہوکر جموں کو چلا گیا" (p. 443, folio 450).
- (book's word: "سکھی سلطنت پنجاب", "ملک پنجاب", "لفٹنٹ گورنر بہادر پنجاب"; refers to: the end of Sikh rule and the start of British rule, date not stated; book's historical prose): "آخر جب سکھی سلطنت پنجاب سے جاتی رہی اور ملک پنجاب زیر سایہ ابد پایہ سرکار انگریزی آ گیا", the octagonal kothi built over a tomb housed in turn "مسٹر بورنگ[OCR?] صاحب بہادر ڈپٹی کمشنر", "میجر میکلیگر[OCR?] صاحب ڈپٹی کمشنر" and "منٹ گمری صاحب کمشنر جو آخر کار لفٹنٹ گورنر بہادر پنجاب کے ہوئے" (p. 395, folio 402).
- (book's word: "لفٹنٹ گورنر ممالک پنجاب", "حاکم پنجاب", "پنجاب کا انتظام", "امرائے پنجاب"; refers to: British rule, Lawrence "مفسدہ دہلی کے وقت" — as the book states; author's present, entry "منٹگمری ہال و لارنس ہال"): Robert Montgomery, "لفٹنٹ گورنر ممالک پنجاب", "مدت مدید پنجاب کے حاکم رہے اور کمشنری کے عہدے سے لفٹنٹ گورنر ہوئے"; "کرنیل لارنس صاحب بہادر … جو مفسدہ دہلی کے وقت لفٹنٹ گورنر و اعلیٰ حاکم پنجاب کے تہے اور اُن کی کمال عرق ریزی [OCR?] و جانفشانی سے پنجاب کا انتظام قائم رہا اور فوج کثیر نوکر رکھکر دہلی کو روانہ کرتے رہے", for which he was made "گورنر جنرل و وسرائے [OCR?] ہند"; "امرائے پنجاب و حکام نے نہایت رضامندی سے چندہ دیا" for the two halls (p. 399, folio 406).
- (book's word: "صوبہ پنجاب کا خزانہ"; refers to: the author's present, "سرکار انگریزی", with the Sikh-period precedent; author's present): Ranjit Singh renamed the fort's Moti Masjid "موتی مندر" and kept "سلطنت کا خزانہ" there; "سرکار انگریزی بہی صوبہ پنجاب کا خزانہ اُسی میں رکہتی ہے" (p. 339, folio 346).
- (book's word: "خطہ پنجاب", "تمام اضلاع" of "پنجاب"; refers to: the author's present, British period; author's present): the Central Jail Lahore "ایک عام جیل تمام خطہ پنجاب کے قیدیوں کے واسطے بنایا گیا ہے"; "ہر ایک ضلع کے مقام پر ڈسٹرکٹ جیل" hold short-term prisoners, "مگر بڑی میعاد کے قیدی ہر ایک ضلع سے یہاں بہیج دیے [OCR?] جاتے" (p. 410, folio 417); of the women's prison: "اس بندی خانے میں پنجاب کے تمام اضلاع کی قیدی عورتیں آتی ہیں", built "سنہ ۱۸۶۸ء [OCR?]" under the author's supervision (p. 409, folio 416).
- (book's word: "عجائبات خطہ پنجاب", "ریاستوں پنجاب", "صنعت کاری پنجاب"; refers to: "سنہ ۱۸۶۴ء" and "جنوری سنہ ۱۸۸۲[OCR?]ء" — as printed; author's present): the Lahore museum building was put up "جب سرکار نے پہلے پہل عجائبات خطہ پنجاب کی نمایش نشو [OCR?] کی تہی", to which "بڑی بڑی ریاستوں پنجاب کے بیرون [OCR?] سرداروں جاگیرداروں" sent jewels, textiles, arms and manufactures (p. 402, folio 409); in January 1882 "جلسہ نمایش عجائبات صنعت کاری پنجاب" was held in the School of Art building under "مسٹر کپننگ[OCR?] صاحب بہادر پرنسپل مدرسہ صنعت کاری" (p. 387, folio 394).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "عہد شاہ جہان بادشاہ" — as the book states; book's historical prose, entry for the Garhi Shahu madrasa of Abul Khair): Abul Khair came from Baghdad touring countries, and "پنجاب کی آب و ہوا اسکو پسند آئی تو لاہور میں قیام پذیر ہوا" — a thin statement about the region's climate, kept only because it is about the region and not the city (pp. 362–363, folios 369–370).

*Added by `join_check.py apply` on 17 October 2026 under Rauf's rulings of 16 October 2026 (HANDOVER §9.357); decisions in `pipeline/book_queue/join_decisions.tsv`. Every proposed row named here stays its own row.*

*Related site (ruling 2) — proposed row `imambara-and-gami-shah`, Imambara and Gami Shah. Join-check basis follows; then its line(s) under this book, copied verbatim from `shrine_entries/imambara-and-gami-shah.md`.*

Join-check basis: Address landmark: outside Bhati Gate, south of Data Ganj Bakhsh. The data-darbar entry's Tahqiqat section has an Imambara '... ہجویری کے آگے واقع ہے' (p. 272) as a landmark, which may be this one (not asserted); Tareekh's line is not in the entry.

- **امام بارہ شیعہ امامیہ and گامی شاہ** — outside Bhati Gate south of Data Ganj Bakhsh; a domed rauza "بنام نہاد امام حسین صاحب کے" over a **sard-khana** with **replica graves** of Imam Hasan and Imam Husain carrying the taziya, and the grave of **Gami Shah**, "بانی مبانی اس مکان امام بارے کا"; patrons **Nawab Nawazish Ali Khan Qizilbash and Nawab Nasir Ali Khan**; "یہ مکان مربع قوم شیعہ امامیہ کا ہے"; the **Zuljanah procession and the 1849 riot** (pp. 301–302, folios 304–305); contents "امام بارہ شیعہ امامیہ ۳۰۴" (pp. 3–4, folios 2–3).

## From Zahur ul-Hasan Sharib, Tazkirah Awliya-e Pak o Hind

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/tazkirah_awliya_pak_o_hind.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- **Saint.** **The two ranges are grossly unequal and the reader must see it.** In pp. 1–109 the book treats him at chapter length: باب 1 is the book's first chapter, runs pp. 21–29 (folios 17–25), is complete within that range, and carries twenty numbered حواشی printed together at p. 29 (folio 25), supporting every substantive claim except two (p. 29, folio 25). In pp. 211–303 he is engaged **only** in chunk 012, pp. 250–251 (folios 246–247), inside باب 50, and there only as the shrine another saint is ordered to attend — a single episode, not a treatment (pp. 250–251, folios 246–247).
  - **Titles.** "سلطان الطریقت ہیں۔ گنج حقیقت ہیں۔ برہان الشریعت ہیں" — Sultan al-Tariqat, Ganj al-Haqiqat, Burhan al-Shariat (p. 21, folio 17).
  - **Descent.** "آپ سادات حسنی سے ہیں" — of the **Hasani sayyids**; the lineage runs "چند واسطوں سے", through a few intermediaries, to "حضرت سیدنا زید شہید رضی اللہ عنہ ابن امیر المومنین حضرت سیدنا امام حسن" — Sayyidna **Zaid Shahid** son of Amir al-Muminin **Imam Hasan** (p. 21, folio 17). The number of intermediaries is **(not stated)** (p. 21, folio 17).
  - **Place of origin and family.** "آپ کے آباؤ اجداد غزنی کے رہنے والے تھے" — his forefathers were residents of **غزنی**, Ghazni; his grandfather's name was "حضرت علی", Ali (p. 21, folio 17). Father: "آپ کے والد ماجد کا نام عثمان ہے" — **عثمان**, Usman (p. 21, folio 17, حاشیہ 1, citing کشف المحجوب اردو[OCR?] ترجمہ ص 2 and سفینۃ الاولیاء فارسی ص 164). The pp. 1–109 worker records that this agrees with the archive row's "Ali ibn Usman al-Hujwiri" (p. 21, folio 17, حاشیہ 1).
  - **Birth.** "آپ غزنی میں پیدا ہوئے" — born in **Ghazni** (p. 21, folio 17). Year of birth: **(not stated)** (p. 21, folio 17).
  - **What "Hujwiri" means, asserted without a source.** "ہجویر شہر کا نام نہیں ہے، بلکہ غزنی کے ایک محلے کا نام ہے۔ اس لئے آپ 'علی ہجویری' کہلاتے ہیں" — **Hujwir is not the name of a city but the name of a quarter (محلہ) of Ghazni**, and that is why he is called Ali Hujwiri (p. 21, folio 17). **Placement caution: the book asserts this without citing a source** — one of only two unfootnoted substantive claims in the chapter (p. 21, folio 17; p. 29, folio 25).
  - **Laqab, and its stated origin.** "آپ 'داتا گنج بخش' کے لقب سے پکارے جاتے ہیں" — he is called by the laqab **داتا گنج بخش**, Data Ganj Bakhsh (p. 21, folio 17); "بعض لوگ تو آپ کو 'گنج بخش' کے لقب سے پکارا کرتے ہیں", some call him simply Ganj Bakhsh (p. 22, folio 18). The book gives the laqab a **posthumous** origin, conferred at the tomb by another saint (pp. 21–22, folios 17–18, حاشیہ 3) — see Legends & Miracles.
  - **Training.** "آپ علوم ظاہری کی تحصیل سے فارغ ہو کر علوم باطنی کی طرف متوجہ ہوئے" — having finished the outward sciences he turned to the inward (p. 22, folio 18). Teacher (استاد): **شیخ ابو القاسم**, Shaikh Abu al-Qasim, whose saying on attendance upon a murshid is quoted: a murshid "وہ ہوتا ہے جو کہ غواص ہو نہ کہ متصل [OCR?]", is one who is a **diver** and not متصل[OCR?]; and one should take bai'at only "اگر اپنے میں قوت پائے", if one finds strength in oneself, "اور اپنے میں قوت نہ ہو تو دونوں خراب ہوں گے", and if not, both will be ruined (p. 22, folio 18, حاشیہ 4, citing نفحات الانس فارسی ص 291).
  - **Bai'at and khilafat, a three-link chain.** "آپ شیخ ابو الفضل بن حسن ختلی کے مرید ہیں" — murid of **شیخ ابو الفضل بن حسن ختلی**, Shaikh Abu al-Fazl bin Hasan Khuttali (حاشیہ 5, سفینۃ الاولیاء فارسی ص 164); "وہ مرید حضرت خضری [OCR?] کے، اور وہ مرید حضرت شیخ شبلی کے ہیں" — he the murid of **حضرت خضری**[OCR?], and he the murid of **حضرت شیخ شبلی**, Shaikh Shibli (p. 22, folio 18, حاشیہ 6, same source).
  - **Silsila: (not stated), in both ranges.** **No silsila is named as an order anywhere in باب 1** (pp. 21–29, folios 17–25), and the pp. 211–303 mention likewise records **silsila: (not stated)** (pp. 250–251, folios 246–247). The archive row's `silsila` field is empty and the book supplies nothing to fill it (pp. 21–29, folios 17–25; pp. 250–251, folios 246–247).
  - **Travels and other affiliations.** "آپ نے خراسان، ماوراء النہر، مرو، آذربائیجان وغیرہ کی سیر و سیاحت فرمائی" — **خراسان** Khurasan, **ماوراء النہر** Transoxiana, **مرو** Marw, **آذربائیجان** Azerbaijan and others; he met many darwishes and drew benefit from the spiritual graces of **حضرت شیخ ابو القاسم گرگانی**, **حضرت شیخ ابو سعید ابو الخیر** and **حضرت شیخ ابو القاسم قشیری** (p. 22, folio 18, حاشیہ 7, citing کشف الاسرار اردو ترجمہ ص 16).
  - **UNRECONCILED — two accounts of how he reached India, left standing side by side and adjudicated by neither the book nor this merge.** (a) "بعض لوگوں کا خیال ہے کہ آپ سلطان مسعود بن سلطان محمود غزنوی کے لشکر کے ساتھ ہندوستان تشریف لائے" — some think he came to Hindustan with the army of **سلطان مسعود بن سلطان محمود غزنوی**, Sultan Masud son of Sultan Mahmud Ghaznavi (pp. 22–23, folios 18–19, حاشیہ 8). (b) "اور بعض لوگ اس سے متفق نہیں، ان کا کہنا ہے کہ آپ شیخ احمد جماوی [OCR?] سرخسی اور شیخ ابو سعید ہجویری کے ہمراہ ہندوستان تشریف لائے" — and some do not agree, saying he came in the company of **شیخ احمد جماوی**[OCR?] **سرخسی** and **شیخ ابو سعید ہجویری** (pp. 22–23, folios 18–19, حاشیہ 8, citing فوائد الفوائد اردو ترجمہ ص 27). **The book adjudicates neither** (pp. 22–23, folios 18–19).
  - **Arrival at Lahore and the funeral he met.** He stayed outside the city because night had fallen; entering in the morning he saw a bier and on enquiry learned "کہ حضرت شیخ حسن زنجانی رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کا جنازہ ہے", that it was the janaza of **حضرت شیخ حسن زنجانی**, Shaikh **Hasan** Zanjani (p. 23, folio 19, حاشیہ 9, citing کشف الاسرار اردو ترجمہ ص 16). The pp. 1–109 input flags this against the archive under its Doubts D2, on Zanjani (p. 23, folio 19).
  - **Settlement.** "آپ نے لاہور میں مستقل سکونت اختیار فرمائی اور تعلیم و تلقین اور رشد و ہدایت میں مشغول ہوئے" — he took **permanent residence in لاہور, Lahore**, and occupied himself in teaching, talqin and guidance (p. 23, folio 19).
  - **UNRECONCILED — two death years printed side by side.** (a) "آپ نے **465ھ** میں اس جہاں فانی سے سفر دار الاخرت فرمایا" — he journeyed to the abode of the hereafter in **465ھ**, 465 AH (p. 23, folio 19, حاشیہ 10, citing سفینۃ الاولیاء فارسی ص 16). (b) "بعض کے نزدیک آپ کا وصال **456ھ** میں ہوا" — in the view of some his passing was in **456ھ**, 456 AH (p. 23, folio 19, حاشیہ 11, citing کشف الاسرار اردو ترجمہ ص 16). **The book takes no side and neither does this merge**; both years are printed in Western digits (p. 23, folio 19). **Day and month: (not stated). Age at death: (not stated)** (p. 23, folio 19). No Common-Era equivalent is printed and none is computed here; the pp. 1–109 input flags the relation to the archive row's `1072` under its Doubts D3 (p. 23, folio 19). The pp. 211–303 mention records **death year: (not stated)**, so that the row's "1072" is there "neither confirmed nor contradicted" (pp. 250–251, folios 246–247) — silence, not a third reading.
  - **Character.** "آپ قطب زمانہ تھے" — he was the **qutb of the age**; engaged in zikr and fikr, muraqaba and muhasaba, ibadat and riyazat; "دنیاوی آلائش سے پاک و صاف تھے", clean of worldly taint; "تصرفات آپ کے بے شمار ہیں", his tasarrufat are countless (p. 23, folio 19).
  - **Works, six titles, three of them OCR-flagged.** "آپ نے بہت سے کتابیں لکھی ہیں": **منہاج الدین**, **البیان الاہل العیان**[OCR?], **اسر الخرق و المونیات**[OCR?], **کشف الاسرار**, **الرعایۃ بحوق اللہ**[OCR?], **کشف المحجوب** (p. 23, folio 19, حاشیہ 12, citing کشف الاسرار اردو ترجمہ ص 12). **Three of the six are OCR-flagged and cannot be identified from the notes** (p. 23, folio 19).
  - **Poetry.** "آپ شاعر بھی تھے۔ آپ کا تخلص 'علی' ہے۔ آپ صاحب دیوان ہیں" — he was also a poet, his takhallus is **علی**, and he possesses a diwan, as he says himself: "میں نے بیت اور اشعار بہت سے کہے ہیں اور ایک دیوان بھی کہا...." (p. 23, folio 19, حاشیہ 13, citing کشف المحجوب اردو ترجمہ ص 3). A Persian **غزل** attributed to him, seven couplets, is printed at p. 24 (folio 20) but is **too damaged to quote**, flagged under the pp. 1–109 input's Doubts D18 (p. 24, folio 20).
  - **The figure names himself at the mazar, in the pp. 211–303 range.** At the mazar "ایک نورانی صورت نمودار ہوئی" — a luminous form appeared — which asked whether he knew who it was and then said "میں علی ہجویری ہوں", "I am Ali Hujwiri" (p. 251, folio 247). The pp. 211–303 worker records that the book's two designations there, "داتا گنج بخش" and "علی ہجویری", match the archive row's principal figure "Hazrat Ali ibn Usman al-Hujwiri (Data Ganj Bakhsh)" (pp. 250–251, folios 246–247).

- **Shrine / Darbar.** Both ranges together yield one locational sentence and a set of naming formulas; everything else about the site is **(not stated)**.
  - **The only statement about the shrine as a place in باب 1**: "مزار پر انوار لاہور میں فیوض و برکات کا سرچشمہ ہے" — the radiant mazar **in لاہور, Lahore**, is a fountainhead of graces and blessings (p. 23, folio 19). This is also one of the two unfootnoted substantive claims in the chapter (p. 23, folio 19; p. 29, folio 25).
  - **Naming of the site in the pp. 211–303 range.** The shrine is named four times as "حضرت داتا گنج بخش رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کے مزار" (pp. 250, 251; folios 246, 247), twice with the epithet "مزار پر انوار", the radiant mazar (p. 250, folio 246).
  - **Placement caution, carried from the pp. 211–303 input: that chunk never prints a town beside this mazar**, and its Lahore placement is contextual only (pp. 250–251, folios 246–247). The Lahore placement is printed only at p. 23 (folio 19), in the other range.
  - **(not stated) throughout both ranges**: no quarter, street, locality or building description; no urs date; no custodian or sajjada nashin; no Auqaf; no endowment; no visitor numbers; no dispute over the darbar (pp. 21–29, folios 17–25; pp. 250–251, folios 246–247).
  - **The mosque he built at Lahore is the only building in باب 1, and it is not the shrine** (pp. 27–28, folios 23–24) — see Practices & Events.

- **Practices & Events.** The pp. 1–109 material is all doctrine, teaching and one building; the pp. 211–303 material is all about attendance at the mazar itself.
  - **A mosque built at Lahore by Ali Hujwiri, with a disputed qibla.** "لاہور میں سکونت اختیار کرنے کے بعد آپ نے ایک مسجد تعمیر کرانا شروع کی۔ مسجد کی سمت قبلہ رو نہ ہونے کی وجہ سے علماء نے اعتراض کیا" — after settling in Lahore he began to have a mosque built, and the **ulama objected** that its direction was not qibla-facing; "اس مسجد کی محراب جنوب کی طرف معلوم ہوتی تھی", the mihrab appeared to face **south**; he answered none of the objections and the work continued (p. 27, folio 23). When it was finished "آپ نے علماء و مشائخ کو بلایا' سب نے وہاں نماز پڑھی' آپ نے امامت فرمائی" — he summoned the **ulama and mashaikh**, all prayed there and **he led the prayer** (p. 28, folio 24, حاشیہ 20, citing سفینۃ الاولیاء فارسی ص 364). **No date, cost, patron or site within Lahore is given** (pp. 27–28, folios 23–24).
  - **A doctrine that a faqir must avoid rulers.** "فقیر کو چاہیے کہ بادشاہوں اور حاکموں کی آشنائی و معرفت کو سخت خطرناک سانپوں اور بڑے بھاری اژدہاؤں کی معرفت و آشنائی سمجھے اور جانے۔ کیوں کہ جب بادشاہ کا تقرب فقیر کو حاصل ہوا تو جانے کہ اس کا زاد اور توشہ برباد ہو گیا" — a faqir should reckon acquaintance with **kings and rulers** as acquaintance with venomous snakes and great dragons, because once a faqir gains a king's favour let him know his provision and viaticum are ruined; and "جب ترکی ٹوپی تو نے سر پر رکھ لی تو اس سے کچھ فقیری حاصل نہیں ہوتی", putting a Turkish cap on your head gains you no faqiri (p. 25, folio 21, حاشیہ 16, citing کشف الاسرار اردو ترجمہ ص 3). The pp. 1–109 worker reads this as **a principled refusal of state patronage, stated by the saint of Data Darbar himself** (p. 25, folio 21).
  - **A saying against sama.** "مبتدی[OCR?] کو چاہیے کہ وہ **سماع** کے نزدیک نہ جائے اور اس سے کنارہ کش رہے" — a beginner[OCR?] should not go near **sama** and should keep away from it (p. 27, folio 23). The pp. 1–109 worker notes it is **relevant to any claim about musical practice at the darbar, and that it is a restriction, not a licence** (p. 27, folio 23).
  - **Charity argued from the grave.** "دولت کو ایک عذاب جان اور اس کو اہل فاقہ لوگوں کو دیدے اور تصدق کر' کیونکہ آخر قبر میں کیڑے کھائیں گے اور اگر تو نے یہ بخشش میں دی دے تو وہ تیرے دوستدار رہیں گے" — reckon wealth a torment, give it to the starving and give it as **sadaqa**, for in the end worms will eat it in the grave, whereas if you give it away as a gift they will remain your friends (pp. 26–27, folios 22–23).
  - **Wird.** "اللہ تعالیٰ کے اسماء حسنیٰ میں سے اسم **یا حبیب** اور **یا لطیف** کے معنوں کو اپنے وجود میں رکھ لے اور ان کو اپنا ورد بنا لے" — take the meanings of the names **Ya Habib** and **Ya Latif** into your being and make them your wird (p. 27, folio 23, حاشیہ 19, citing کشف الاسرار اردو ترجمہ ص 4). **The only wird prescribed for him in the pp. 1–109 range** (p. 27, folio 23).
  - **Teachings, five headed sections plus ten aqwal** (pp. 24–27, folios 20–23): **نفسانی غرض**, self-interest, which lifts the blessing from any work (حاشیہ 14, کشف المحجوب ص 48، 49); **صوفیوں کی صحبت**, the company of the Sufis, a fourfold analysis of the motives of those who seek it, ending on those who "اپنی آفتوں کو ان کی اصلاح میں چھپائیں اور ان کا جامہ پہنیں", hide their own afflictions in the Sufis' correction and wear their garb (حاشیہ 15, کشف المحجوب ص 222); **مجاہدہ نفس**, the struggle against the self, which "رسول اللہ صلی اللہ علیہ وسلم نے … جہاد پر بزرگ رکھا ہے", the Prophet held greater than jihad; **فقیر**, on rulers (above); **دس چیزیں**, ten things that devour ten (حاشیہ 17); and **آٹھ کلمات**, eight sayings selected from Luqman Hakim (حاشیہ 18) (pp. 24–27, folios 20–23).
  - **The shrine as a place of assigned, timed service.** Attendance there is ordered by a pir — "کہ حضرت داتا گنج بخش رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کے مزار پر انوار پر پابندی سے حاضر ہوا کریں" (p. 250, folio 246) — performed "بارہ برس تک نہایت پابندی سے", **twelve years, very strictly**, and answered by the figure at the shrine with "یہ اس خدمت کا صلہ ہے، جو تم نے بارہ سال کی ہے" — this is the reward of the service you have done for twelve years (p. 251, folio 247). The pp. 211–303 worker reads this as **attendance at a shrine framed as an assigned term of service with a stated payoff** (pp. 250–251, folios 246–247).
  - **What is conferred there.** The form "آپ کو نعمت باطنی سے مالا مال کر دیا، آپ کو ولایت عطا فرمائی اور شراب وحدت سے مدہوش و سرشار کیا" — enriched him with inward bounty, **granted him ولایت, wilayat**, and made him drunk with the wine of unity (p. 251, folio 247).
  - **One of two places where a saint found stillness**: "دریا کے کنارے اور حضرت داتا گنج بخش رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کے مزار پر سکون' یک سوئی پاتے" (p. 250, folio 246), and a fixed station in a daily timetable between the dawn prayer and the maktab (pp. 250–251, folios 246–247).

- **Legends & Miracles.**
  - **The couplet that made the laqab, conferred posthumously at the tomb by another saint.** When **حضرت خواجہ معین الدین حسن چشتی سنجری** came to Lahore "تو آپ کے مزار مبارک پر اعتکاف فرمایا", he performed **اعتکاف, itikaf**, at Hujwiri's blessed mazar, and on leaving recited "گنج بخش فیض عالم مظہر نور خدا / ناقصاں را پیر کامل کاملاں را رہنما"; "اس روز سے آپ داتا گنج بخش مشہور ہوئے", from that day he became famous as Data Ganj Bakhsh (pp. 21–22, folios 17–18, حاشیہ 3, citing کشف الاسرار اردو ترجمہ ص 8). The pp. 1–109 worker records this as **the only act of devotion performed at the shrine in that range, and the origin the book gives for the shrine's name** (pp. 21–22, folios 17–18) — the pp. 211–303 range supplies a second such act at pp. 250–251 (folios 246–247).
  - **The dream that assigned Lahore, with no source footnoted.** "مرشد کے حکم سے ہندوستان تشریف لائے" — he came to Hindustan by his murshid's command; one night he saw his pir in a dream saying "تم کو لاہور کا قطب بنایا گیا ہے۔ لاہور تمہارے سپرد کیا گیا ہے۔ لاہور جاؤ" — you have been made the **qutb of Lahore**, Lahore has been entrusted to you, go to Lahore. He objected in the dream that Khwaja Hasan Zanjani was already there, "ان کی موجودگی میں میرا لاہور جانا بے کار ہے", and the pir replied there was no need for argument and he should set out quickly (p. 22, folio 18). **No source is footnoted for the dream** (p. 22, folio 18). The pp. 1–109 worker adds the observation — **an inference of the chunk worker, not a statement of the book** — that the institutional form is a city assigned as a jurisdiction to one saint at a time, the same form as the "ولایت" grants at pp. 78–79, 92, 94 (folios 74–75, 88, 90).
  - **The wisdom of the command shown by a funeral.** Meeting Hasan Zanjani's bier on the morning he entered Lahore, "اب آپ کو معلوم ہوا کہ پیرو مرشد کے فرمان میں کیا مصلحت تھی" — now he understood what the expediency in his pir's command had been (p. 23, folio 19, حاشیہ 9).
  - **The mosque whose qibla was proved by lifting the veils.** After the congregation had prayed he asked those present to consider where the Kaaba lay; "آپ نے توجہ فرمائی' حجابات اٹھ گئے' سب نے دیکھا کہ کعبہ سامنے اور مسجد کی سمت صحیح ہے" — he directed his attention, the veils were lifted, and all saw the **Kaaba in front of them** and the mosque's direction correct (p. 28, folio 24, حاشیہ 20, citing سفینۃ الاولیاء فارسی ص 364). The pp. 1–109 worker records this as **the book's only karamat of Hujwiri in that range, settling an architectural dispute by vision rather than by instrument** (p. 28, folio 24).
  - **A luminous form at the mazar naming itself**, granting **ولایت**, wilayat, and the wine of unity, and telling the attendant this was the reward of twelve years' service (p. 251, folio 247).
  - Of Hujwiri generally the author says only "تصرفات آپ کے بے شمار ہیں" — his tasarrufat are countless (p. 23, folio 19) — and names none beyond the mosque in that range (pp. 21–29, folios 17–25).

- **Significance.**
  - **باب 1 is a hagiography with a citation apparatus resting on a narrow base.** Its twenty حواشی cite **six works only** — کشف المحجوب (اردو[OCR?] ترجمہ), سفینۃ الاولیاء (فارسی), خزینۃ الاصفیاء, کشف الاسرار (اردو ترجمہ; once "ردو[OCR?] ترجمہ"), نفحات الانس (فارسی), فوائد الفوائد (اردو ترجمہ) — four of them read in Urdu translation and two in Persian; **nine of the twenty point to a single book**, کشف الاسرار (اردو ترجمہ), and three to سفینۃ الاولیاء at three different pages (ص 16، 164، 364) (p. 29, folio 25).
  - **Every substantive claim about the saint carries a note except two**: the Hujwir-is-a-quarter etymology (p. 21, folio 17) and the single mazar sentence (p. 23, folio 19) (p. 29, folio 25).
  - **The chapter's institutional yield for the archive is close to nil and its silence is systematic**, not accidental: the author's stated programme (p. 18) contains no shrine, urs, custodianship or endowment category, and the entry gives the mazar one line (p. 23, folio 19).
  - **In the pp. 211–303 range the shrine is the source of another saint's wilayat** (p. 251, folio 247) — the pp. 211–303 worker records it as the only place in that range where a shrine, rather than a living pir, confers **ولایت** (p. 251, folio 247).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **D3. The Hujwiri death year and the archive row's `1072`.** The book gives **465ھ** (حاشیہ 10) and, as
  a minority view, **456ھ** (حاشیہ 11) (p. 23, folio 19); the archive row **data-darbar** carries
  **1072**. **The chunk never gives a Christian-era year for anything**, so the correspondence between
  "465ھ" and "1072" is **not something this range establishes and is not asserted here**. Record the
  book's two Hijri years with their sources and let a human settle the era. **The minority reading
  456ھ, nine years earlier, is the real variant and should not be dropped.**

18. **The `data-darbar` mapping rests on the figure's name, not on a printed town.** The book names the
    shrine four times as "مزار" of "حضرت داتا گنج بخش" and has the figure there say "میں علی ہجویری
    ہوں" (pp. 250–251, folios 246–247) — both designations matching the row's principal figure element
    for element. **But the chunk never prints a town beside that mazar.** The Lahore placement is
    contextual only (p. 249, folio 245; pp. 250–251, folios 246–247; pp. 251–252, folios 247–248). **A
    human should confirm that the book intends the Lahore shrine**; a second Data Ganj Bakhsh elsewhere
    is not raised by the text but is not excluded by it either.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the reign of "شہنشاہ اکبر", the date printed "بروز شنبہ بتاریخ 2 محرم 980ھ"; quoted source — the sentence carries حاشیہ 5 of باب 49, "انوار العارفین (فارسی) صفحہ 367", p. 245, folio 241): in باب 49, on حضرت شیخ جلال الدین تھانیسری, under the heading "اکبر کی باریابی": "شہنشاہ اکبر بروز شنبہ بتاریخ 2 محرم 980ھ کو مزار حکیم کی بغاوت کو فرد کرنے کی غرض سے پنجاب جاتے ہوئے تھانیسر میں رکا" — Punjab is the destination of an imperial campaign against a revolt; the stop, and the shaikh's خانقاہ, are at تھانیسر, which is not said to be in پنجاب. "مزار حکیم" and "فرد" are printed so and carry no flag; the sense calls for a person's name and a verb of suppressing, so check the page image before quoting (p. 243, folio 239).

## From H. T. Sorley, Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/sorley_shah_abdul_latif_of_bhit.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "between 1748 and l75I" [text-layer?] [1751]; quoted source: Sir Alfred Lyall, *History of India*): Ahmad Shah of the Abdali Afghans, after Nadir Shah's assassination, rode east "to capture Afghanistan and from that base he seized the whole of the Punjab between 1748 and l75I" [text-layer?] [1751] (p. 17, folio 8).
- (book's word: "the Punjab rivers"; refers to: the centuries before "the mid­eighteenth century", when the north-west barriers were "for ever closed by the growth of the kingdom of Afghanistan ... and the rise of the S ikhs" [text-layer?] [Sikhs]; book's historical prose): invaders by-passed Sind: "the wandering hordes which poured over the frontier barriers of the nor th-west [text-layer?] [north-west] ... passed rapidly to the more prom ising [text-layer?] [promising] fields of the Punjab rive rs [text-layer?] [rivers] and the Ganget ic [text-layer?] [Gangetic] plain in their assaults on t he pen insular land of Hindustan" (p. 22, folio 13).
- (book's word: "the plains of the Punj ab" [text-layer?] [Punjab], "the Punjab"; refers to: 1758 and 1760; book's historical prose): when the Company re-entered Sind in 1758 "Afghans, Sikhs and Marathas were at each other's throats in the plains of the Punj ab" [text-layer?] [Punjab] (p. 46, folio 37); in 1760 "The Afghan and Maratha armies were threatening each other in the Punjab" (p. 70, folio 61).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, the period of the chapter; book's historical prose reporting Sarkar, *Moghul Administration* (Second Series)): "Sarkar has also remarked that, with the Punjab, Sind was the home of the leather industry" (p. 107, folio 98).
- (book's word: "the Punj ab" [text-layer?] [Punjab]; refers to: "the seventeenth century"; book's historical prose): against the rule that Moghul public money went to tombs and palaces rather than public works, "J:tere [text-layer?] [There] is, however, an exception to this generalization in the seventeenth century canal enterprise in the Punj ab [text-layer?] [Punjab] and improvements carried out on the old · Delhi Jumna canal"; the book does not say where in the Punjab (p. 122, folio 113).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "Punjab"; refers to: "1 756-8" and "1 758" as printed (years split by a space [text-layer?]), in the column "Events in Indian History"; chronological appendix): "Sikhs rise in the Punjab" (p. 201, folio 192); "Marathas occupy Punjab" (p. 202, folio 193). The book does not bound "the Punjab" here.
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "a Punjab tale"; refers to: period not stated, before the Risalo's composition; author's argument): "The story of Suhini and Mehar is a Punjab tale which has found a home in Sind and it must have come into Sind through the immigration from the Punjab of those Hindu races which form an important element in the population, as has been made clear in the Census of India volume for 1931 relating to the Bombay Presidency" (p. 270, folio 261); of the Risalo's stories, "one of therii frorn t4e Punj a.9" [text-layer?] [one of them from the Punjab] (p. 271, folio 262). Punjab figures only as the source of a migration and a tale; the statement is chiefly about Sind's population.

## From Annemarie Schimmel, Pain and Grace

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_pain_and_grace.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book calls him "Ali Hujwiri—called Data Ganj Bakhsh—" and, elsewhere and in the index, simply
"Hujwiri". **No ʿurs, no custodian, no building date, no visitor figure and no endowment are given**
— all **(not stated)**.

- **Shrine / Darbar** — **A tomb described as a functioning place of pilgrimage — the only living cult
  site named in the whole book outside Bhit Shah and Delhi.** "the first Persian treatise on Islamic
  mysticism, the Kashf al-mahjub, was written by Ali Hujwiri—called Data Ganj Bakhsh—(d.1071), whose
  tomb in Lahore is still a place of pilgrimage for the people" (p. 8, folio 4). **Death year as
  printed: "(d.1071)"; the archive row gives 1072 — see Doubts.**
- **Significance** — **He is Schimmel's earliest instance of the mystics' arrival**, named to date it:
  "the mystics who had settled in the Subcontinent as early as the 11th century" (p. 8, folio 4).
- **Significance** — **Cited twice more as an author only, with no Lahore and no shrine**: "Hujwiri
  praised Abu Hanifa the Kufian" (p. 201, folio 201, footnote 11 citing "Hujwiri, Kashf al-mahjub,
  transl. R.A. Nicholson, p. 93 ff."); and "long before him one finds Hujwiri's biting remarks about
  those who make their patched frocks almost an object of worship" (p. 203, folio 203, footnote 14
  citing "Hujwiri, lc. p.48 and 56 about the `khirga`[OCR?] [khirqa]").
- **Apparatus** — The index gives **"Hujwiri 4, 201, 203"** (p. 303, folio 303) and
  **"Kashf al-mahjub (Hujwiri) 4"** (p. 305, folio 305). **No "Data Ganj Bakhsh", no "Data Darbar"
  and no Lahore attribution in the index** — **(not stated)** — though "Lahore 2,4, 9, 47" (p. 305,
  folio 305) shares p. 4. The Kashf is listed in Nicholson's translation (p. 292, folio 292).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **Single-row person attachments under the 18 September ruling, where the book names the figure only
  as an author or a literary reference.** `mazar-e-iqbal` (named four times, always as a thinker);
  `mazar-of-bulleh-shah` (a poet and a legend, no place ever); `garh-maharaja-shorkot` (a poet and
  two death years); `mausoleum-of-waris-shah` (an author and a poem); `data-darbar` at pp. 201 and
  203 (an author only — though p. 8 gives him a tomb and a living cult, so that row is independently
  safe); `shrine-of-sachal-sarmast` (every mention is about his poetry); `darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz`.
  **All are mapped under the ruling. If the archive later prefers shrine-relevant mentions only,
  these are the bullets to strike, and `mazar-e-iqbal` and `darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz` are the two
  weakest.**

| row | the book prints | the archive row |
|---|---|---|
| `data-darbar` | "(d.1071)" (p. 8, folio 4) | 1072 |

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind and the Panjab", "the Panjab"; refers to: the conquests "first by Nadir Shah and then by Ahmad Shah Durrani", and "the days of Fariduddin Shakarganj, the Chishti saint of Pakpattan (d. 1265)" — as printed; book's historical prose): "The countries which were conquered first by Nadir Shah and then by Ahmad Shah Durrani, Sind and the Panjab, could likewise boast of a rich literary heritage. In the Panjab, popular mystical poetry may have emerged as early as in the days of Fariduddin Shakarganj"; "Numerous folk songs and ballads existed but were never committed to writing" (p. 16, folio 12).
- (book's word: "the province"; refers to: "the late 17th and the 18th century" — as printed; book's historical prose): "The golden age of Muslim Panjabi poetry is the late 17th and the 18th century, e.g., the time when the province was invaded one time after the other by Mahrattas, Sikhs, and, in between, by the liberating Muslim armies" (p. 17, folio 13).
- (book's word: "a Panjabi"; refers to: "even today", present day of writing; author's argument): of Warith Shah's *Hir Ranjha* (composed "around 1794"): "even today a Panjabi will be deeply touched when this moving story is sung" (p. 17, folio 13).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Nadir Shah's invasion, "before he reached Delhi in 1739" — as printed; book's historical prose): Nadir Shah "first conquered Sind and the Panjab before he reached Delhi in 1739"; Ahmad Shah Abdali's influence "on the scene in Sind is not as important as it was in the northern areas of present day Pakistan" (p. 30, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "1750" and after, the reign of Ahmad Shah of Delhi, to "1754" — as printed; book's historical prose): as the Mughal empire "had broken into pieces", "Sikh threatened the Panjab"; "Nadir Shah's successor, Ahmad Shah Durrani, invaded the Panjab several times, coming from his native Afghanistan" (p. 50, folio 48).
- (book's word: "Panjabi", "rural provinces"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's lifetime and "later" poets, period otherwise not stated; author's argument): similarities with "Panjabi mystical folk poetry, which reached its apex with Bullhe Shah during exactly the same period" are "conspicuous"; Siraiki "forms the transition to Panjabi; thus, the imagery of both, or rather all three, linguistic areas is very closely related, as is to be expected from the inhabitants of predominatly [OCR?] [predominantly] rural provinces" (p. 154, folio 154).
- (book's word: "both Sind and the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "Sohni is one of the favorite folk tales in both Sind and the Panjab" (p. 156, folio 156).
- (book's word: "the poetry of Sind and the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "A topic which is not rarely found in the poetry of Sind and the Panjab is the spinning of cotton, one of the most important occupations of women"; the Kapa'iti [printed "Kdpd ?it?" [OCR?]] melodies are "traditional folk melodies sung by the womenfolk while spinning" (p. 160, folio 160).
- (book's word: "the Panjab", "Panjabi contemporaries"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's lifetime (the book dates him "1689-1752"); author's argument): Latif "stands before us as the representative of that type of the rural mystical tradition in which his contemporaries in the Panjab and, to a certain extent, in the Pashto speaking areas were rooted"; "Shah Abdul Latif, as did his Panjabi contemporaries and his successors in Sind, accepted the Hindus who were striving on the way to perfection as true pilgrims in the eternal Light" (p. 267, folio 267).

## From Christopher Shackle, Bulleh Shah: Sufi Lyrics

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/shackle_bulleh_shah_sufi_lyrics.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: Bullhe Shah's lifetime — the book gives "Bullhe Shah (d. 1758)"; book's historical prose): "He lived at a time of great social upheaval in Panjab,1 as the central authority of the declining Mughal empire came under increasing challenge from armed rebellion by the Sikhs and from local notables establishing their auton-omy" [text-layer?] [autonomy]; the "1" fused to "Panjab," is an endnote marker (note 1: "See further Nijjar 1972, Alam 1986."), not a figure; "these unsettled conditions are occasionally re­flected [text-layer?] [reflected] in the poetry" (p. 8, folio vii).
- (book's word: "eigh­teenth-century Panjab" [text-layer?] [eighteenth-century Panjab]; refers to: "early eigh­teenth-century" [text-layer?] [eighteenth-century], as the book states; author's argument): "Bullhe Shah’s critique of the social upheavals in early eigh­teenth-century [text-layer?] [eighteenth-century] Panjab, which is so interesting to many modern readers, represents only a very small, if vividly expressed, part of his poetry" (p. 15, folio xiv).
- (book's word: "Panjab", "Panjabi society"; refers to: the time of kāfī 34, "The twelfth century of the Hijri era", which the note says "corresponds to the eighteenth century C.E. (1100 A.H. = 1688 C.E.)" — digits as printed; editor's note): the kāfī's allusive verses "appear to refer to a separation of Bullhe Shah from Shah Inayat due to the troubled conditions then prevailing in Panjab"; the note compares kāfī 4 "for the allusions to the disturbed state of contemporary Panjabi society" (pp. 165–166, folios 140–141).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: the poet's own time, "The twelfth century" as the verse puts it — see the previous bullet for the note's reading; translation of the poet's verse, kāfī 34 — FLAG: verse register, kept because it characterises the region): "The twelfth century4 has come with gaping mouth. The door of doom and torment has opened, and Panjab is in a bad state. It has been struck by fear of the pit of hell." (the "4" fused to "century" is an endnote marker) (p. 52, folio 27).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: period not stated in the note; editor's note on kāfī 143, n. 35): a verse is "Taken to be an allusion to the Sikhs, whose guerilla horsemen ultimately destroyed the Mughal authority in Panjab" (p. 193, folio 168).
- (book's word: "eighteenth-century Panjab"; refers to: the eighteenth century, as the book states; editor's note on kāfī 86, n. 3): "The Ranghars were a tribe of Muslim Rajputs who achieved prominence in eighteenth-century Panjab, and who were proverbial for their arrogance" (p. 179, folio 154).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: "the Mughal period and beyond", following "The Muslim conquest of northern India" — no date given; book's historical prose): "The Muslim conquest of northern India extended this Persianate cultural world to Panjab, where Persian remained the dominant literary language of the ruling elite through the Mughal period and beyond. A strong Sufi presence was rapidly established with the arrival of charismatic fig­ures [text-layer?] [figures] associated with the main Sufi orders" (p. 11, folio x).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: the Sufi poets "who lived at different times", and their collection "only in the late nineteenth century"; book's historical prose): "the lyrics of Bullhe Shah and the other Sufi poets, who lived at different times and in different areas of Panjab, were never systematically preserved by an or­ga­nized [text-layer?] [organized] faith community, and they were for the most part rec­orded [text-layer?] [recorded] and assembled in printed collections only in the late nineteenth century" (pp. 9–10, folios viii–ix).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: "the British conquest of Panjab in the 1840s" and after, as the book states; book's historical prose): "Only with the British conquest of Panjab in the 1840s did the literary culture of the Sufi tradition come to be overlaid by new patterns of modernity"; Persian "was quite rapidly replaced as the language of education, administra-tion [text-layer?] [administration], and elite literature by Urdu and Eng­lish [text-layer?] [English]"; "Publishers in Lahore" brought the older Sufi poetry "into the new print culture" (pp. 11–12, folios x–xi).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: "twentieth-century" understandings and "the new middle class of the colonial period", as the book states; book's historical prose): "In Panjab the formulation of nationalist un­der­stand­ings [text-layer?] [understandings] was no simple pro­cess [text-layer?] [process], given the complexities created by the simultaneous currency of three closely related languages, Urdu, Hindi, and Panjabi, whose differences were exploited to sharpen the rival cultural identities of Muslims, Hindus, and Sikhs, respectively" (p. 12, folio xi).
- (book's word: "western Panjab"; refers to: the setting of the Hir–Ranjha legend, period not stated; book's prose on the poet's legendary material — the book itself marks the unit as "western"): "The greatest of these local legends is the story of Hir and Ranjha, which is set in the world of the Jat pastoral tribes of western Panjab" (p. 20, folio xix).

## From Taufiq Rafat, Bulleh Shah: A Selection

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rafat_bulleh_shah_selection.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: Bulleh Shah's lifetime, "a time of great upheaval. The Mughal Empire was breaking up" — as the Translator's Note states it; Translator's Note, Taufiq Rafat): "the Punjab was being persistently rent by petty chiefs and marauders"; "The full bitterness of Bulleh Shah’s ‘Punjab has gone to the dogs’ will only come through against this backdrop" (p. 16, folio xvi).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: Bulleh Shah's adult life, from Aurangzeb's death "in 1707" (margin heading "End of Mughal rule 1707.") — as the book states; book's historical prose, Introduction): Bulleh Shah's "depressive outlook was in no small measure produced by the political situation in the Punjab"; he was "deeply bruised" by the upheavals "taking place in the Punjab and was depressed by the sufferings of the common man"; "After Aurangzeb, the halcyon days that the Punjab had enjoyed quickly came to an end. Already badly defended, the North-Western region of the empire became increasingly subject to foreign" invasions "and inner centrifugal quarrels" (p. 26, folio 6).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the region", "the Pun-jabi of today"; refers to: from "The period of Muhammad Shah" (accession "in 1719" as printed) "into the 19th century", with a claim about the present day; author's argument, Introduction): that period "coincided with the beginning of the misfortunes of the Punjab. The decay of Mughal administration gave rise to small local tyrannies which lasted into the 19th century and formed the character of the" inhabitants "of the region"; the crisis of character in the Punjabi of today "is traceable to this period of duress in the history of his land" (pp. 26–27, folios 6–7; the sentence carries note 4 — see the Janus bullet below).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: after "The invasion of Nadir Shah in 1738" and Abdali's invasions "in 1748 and then a third in 1752," — as printed; book's historical prose, Introduction): "Marauding Sikh bands formed themselves into robber baronies and imposed their own system of extortion on the Punjab farmer"; Abdali's "treatment of the Punjab weakened the government in Lahore and delivered the various doabs into the hands of the Sikh" confederacy "called the misis" [text-layer?] [misls]; Bulleh Shah "was fated to see a second invasion of the Punjab by Ahmad Shah Abdali in 1748 and then a third in 1752," (p. 27, folio 7).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "1758", the year of Bulleh Shah's death, as printed; book's historical prose, Introduction): "As Bulleh Shah lay dying in 1758 at the age of 78, the rape of the Punjab was proceeding apace" (p. 28, folio 8).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the province"; refers to: "In the years to come" after Aurangzeb's period, with Banda Bairagi "(1670-1716)", Abdul Samad Khan, Zakriya Khan, Nadir Shah and "Shahidganj in 1746" — as printed; book's historical prose, Introduction): "In the years to come, when plunder became routine in the Punjab, Bulleh Shah had no choice of factions. The Sikh marauders under Banda Bairagi (1670-1716) were to lay waste the whole of the province excluding Lahore and Qasur, imposing bone-crushing taxation .on [text-layer?] [on] the common peasant for the raising of armies on both sides" (pp. 42–43, folios 22–23).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated — the passage runs from "The ancient cities of the Indus" to the present; author's argument, Introduction): "In terms of written sources, the Punjab and indeed the whole of the Indus basin, has always" seemed "a wasteland to scholars" (pp. 46–47, folios 26–27).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated — said of the survival of Baba Farid's and Shah Husain's poetry through Sikh collectors, the latter "in the 1940’s"; author's argument, Introduction): "A lack of intellectual tradition among the Muslims of the Punjab accounts for this phillistine [text-layer?] [philistine] indifference to the classics. Bulleh Shah was treated no differently" (p. 47, folio 27).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: after Dr. Nazeer Ahmad's selection "In 1976", i.e. the present of the Introduction; author's argument, Introduction): "After this the Punjab awaits a proper" scholarly "assessment of its greatest poet, a tryst that may never take place given the regional antecedents in the realm of letters" (p. 49, folio 29).
- (book's word: "Punjabi literature in Pakistan", "the Punjabis"; refers to: the present of the Introduction, date not stated in the passage; author's argument, Introduction — language-centred, kept because it states a condition of the Punjabis in Pakistan in general): "Punjabi literature in Pakistan is in a state of neglect. The language has become a purely spoken medium and the Punjabis are no longer able to" decipher "it in the written form" (p. 50, folio 30).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "Punjabi"; refers to: "Viewpoint weekly (July 23, 1981)", present day; quoted source — the columnist "Janus", "‘Are you a Punjabi?’", a satirical questionnaire "in which ‘c’ stands for the Punjabi response", which Notes on the Introduction note 4 says "has accurately enumerated the ingredients of this lapse from character"): item (5), "You have lived in the Punjab all your life, but suddenly you come to know that your ancestors had come here from Central Asia", with the "Punjabi response" "(c) What the hell! I am a Bokhari and superior to all the low stock inhabiting the Punjab"; item (10), "All regional ‘languages [text-layer?] [languages] in Pakistan are taught in schools except Punjabi. All regional languages in Pakistan possess dictionaries except Punjabi"; item (11), "All regional personalities are moulded by a deep love of their idiom. A Sindhi will read Sindhi poetry and can tell you who is the greatest living Sindhi poet; so will the Pathan and the Baluch, Except [text-layer?] [Baluch. Except], of course, the Punjabi" (p. 236, folio 216; pp. 237–238, folios 217–218).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period as the verse gives it, "This century"; translation of the poet's verse — the poem "COME SEE ME ONCE IN A WHILE"; flagged: a verse line, kept only because it says something about the region and the Translator's Note (p. 16) reads it as political): "Now hell has opened its bogs. / Punjab has gone to the dogs. / This hell is the nethermost hell." … "This century cramps our style." (p. 149, folio 129).

## From Waris Shah, Hir and Ranjha (Usborne tr.)

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/waris_shah_hir_ranjha.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the time "When Shakarganj made his abode at Pakpattan", period not stated; translation of the poet's verse, the Invocation — FLAG: verse): "When Shakarganj made his abode at Pakpattan, the Punjab was delivered of all its troubles.4"; the annotator's note 4 rebuts it: "Much as one would like to agree with Waris Shah this is not historically correct. The Punjab has had plenty of troubles even after Baba Farid honoured it by making it his home and his place of burial!" (p. 2, folio 1).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: the annotator's present, "still"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Ranjha is still a well-known caste of the Jats in the old Punjab." (p. 3, folio 2).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: the annotator's present, "now"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Mauj Din is now a common name In the old Punjab." (p. 4, folio 3).
- (book's word: "the Punjab village"; refers to: past, "were", period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "Spinning parties — the Punjabi name is ‘tarinjan’ or ‘bhora’ — were a famous institution of the Punjab village. Young girls, generally unmarried ones, sat together wit [text-layer?] [with] their spinning-wheels and competed with each other in spinning skill and scandalous conversation." (p. 4, folio 3, note 14).
- (book's word: "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Lassi is a favourite drink in the Punjab countryside." (note 6); "Washing the hair with curds is still well known in the Punjab countryside." (note 8) (p. 4, folio 3).
- (book's word: "a Punjab village"; refers to: the annotator's present, "even now"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "This is how a morning begins even now in a Punjab village." — glossing the verse's dawn at the mosque (p. 5, folio 4, note 4).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Khojas are a well-known tribe of Hindu converts to Islam in the old Punjab who are mainly engaged in trade." (p. 7, folio 6, note 6).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the campaigns of Mahmood of Ghazna" and "1748" as printed; annotator's note, historical): "The Punjab has often been the battleground of invading forces from the North. The campaigns of Mahmood of Ghazna are famous. Here, the poet may be thinking of Ahead [text-layer?] [Ahmad] Shah Abdali, whose capital was Kandahar. and who, in 1748, began a series of invasions of India through the Punjab." (p. 8, folio 7, note 8); a later note on the verse "today the Punjab has fallen into the hands of Kandaharis5" says "This is probably a reference to the invasion of Ahmad Shah Abdali, whose capital was Kandahar. See Chapter 4, note 8." (p. 48, folio 47, note 5).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "in the past", and "the Anglo-Sikh treaty of 1809" as printed; annotator's note, historical): glossing the verse "as if the armies of the Punjab had fallen upon Hindustan.2" (p. 7, folio 6): "Historically speaking, this is an imaginary situation, as in the past the armies of the Punjab are known to have defended Hindustan on more than one occasion rather than to have fallen upon it at any time. Even the Sikh power which rose long after Waris Shah’s poem was written, was contained on the banks of the Sutlej river by the Anglo-Sikh treaty of 1809, , and never became a menace to Hindustan. The poet’s flight of fancy may have been inspired by a mistaken sense of local patriotism." (p. 8, folio 7, note 2). The book marks "the Sikh power" as a unit of its own; no boundary is inferred.
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; translation of the poet's verse — FLAG: verse similes, nothing said about the region beyond invasion as a figure of speech): "as if armies from Kandahar had swept over the Punjab.8" (p. 7, folio 6); "today the Punjab has fallen into the hands of Kandaharis5" (p. 47, folio 46); "looted him as armies have looted the Punjab3" (p. 44, folio 43 — the note answering marker 3 was not identified).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "There are also other ‘Bars’ in the old Punjab, such as ‘Nili Bar’ in the Montgomery area (now renamed Sahiwal). ‘Bar’ may he [text-layer?] [be] described as a jungle." (p. 10, folio 9, note 3; the note's first sentence, on Sandal Bar "between the Jhelum and Chenab flyers" [text-layer?] [rivers], is one territory and is not carried).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the more fashionable Punjabi turban"; refers to: "the late nineteenth century" as printed, and present tense; annotator's note, historical): the turban tuft "is the well-known ‘turrah’, beloved of the rural aristocracy of the Punjab. It spreads like a peacock’s tail over the side or the back of the more fashionable Punjabi turban. The tufted turban is a comparatively recent development, and i5 [text-layer?] [is] said to have been invented by the ‘mirasis’ or hereditary bards of the Punjab in the late nineteenth century." (p. 11, folio 10, note 7).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab area"; refers to: the annotator's present, "still"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): bhang "is a favourite with the mystically inclined, particularly among Muslims and Sikhs. It is still smoked, chewed, eaten or drunk in the old Punjab area by wandering mendicants and many of those seeking spiritual exaltation or sexual pleasure" (p. 12, folio 11, note 4).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; annotator's note, historical/literary): "Sohni-Mahiwal has its setting In The old Punjab." — Sohni "belonged to Gujurar [text-layer?] [Gujrat], on the river Chenab"; "SoJtni-Mahiwal [text-layer?] [Sohni-Mahiwal] and Sossi-Funoo [text-layer?] [Sassi-Punnoo] have also travelled beyond their original setting, being popular both in the old Punjab and Sind." (p. 12, folio 11, note 5). Also under `sindh`.
- (book's word: "the Punjab villages"; refers to: the annotator's present, "even now"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): of a mother's threat, "A grossly exaggerated way of asserting parental authority. - (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Fingers in their mouths is a typical female gesture common in the old Punjab." (p. 14, folio 13, note 10).
- (book's word: "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: past, "had", period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "Old houses in the Punjab countryside had a big courtyard with a number of living-rooms and bedrooms round it. - (book's word: "the rural Punjab"; refers to: past, "used to be", with "In our times" contrast; annotator's note, historical): "A spinning-wheel (charkha) used to be the inseparable companion of every woman, old or young, in the rural Punjab. Young girls and brides had red spinning-wheel, of smaller size. The spinning-wheel was an important link in rural society. Women spun cotton yarn, and the village weaver made it into cloth." (p. 17, folio 16, note 1).

They also engage in agriculture on a large scale. A number of Punjab towns, such as Gujranwala, Gujrat and Gujar Khan are named after the Gujar tribe." (p. 19, folio 18, note 3).

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: present tense, "has always been", period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present — FLAG: about one tribe, framed against the region): "the Sial tribe has always been more than usually considerate towards its women.
