Directly relevant to any Pakistani shrine's ʿurs calendar.
- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "a British college in Lahore"): **the colonial education demanded of a shrine heir was to be had at Lahore** — "a debate raged in Chishti circles in the 1930s over whether or not the British could compel a minor who had become diwan at Pakpattan to obtain education at a British college in Lahore" (p. 116, folio 103). **The college is not named: (not stated).**
- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore"): **Lahore is one of four cities where a twentieth-century Chishti worked as a journalist** — Zauqi Shah's newspaper work at Peshawar, Lahore, Hyderabad-Sind and Karachi (p. 139, folio 126).
- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore"): **a Lahore private collector supplied one of the book's two appendix manuscripts** — "(personal collection of Carl Ernst; thanks to Mr. Khalil al-Rahman Dawoodi of Lahore)" (p. 159, folio 147); the same man is named again as the donor of the `Adab al-talibin` manuscript copied at Taunsa (p. 243, folio 233).
- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore"): **a migration stage in the appendix's narrative** — "After passing some time in Lahore, they went south" (p. 171, folio 159).
- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore"): **Lahore is the book's most frequent Pakistani place of publication, and the destination of a political movement.** Lahore imprints named in the bibliographies include `Lahore: Sufi Foundation, 1403/1983` (p. 210, folio 199; p. 236, folio 226), `Lahore: Islamic Book Foundation, 1399/1979` (p. 217, folio 206; p. 238, folio 228; p. 240, folio 230), `Lahore: Artistic Printing Works, 1903` (p. 223, folio 212), `Lahore: Majlis Shah Hussain, 1967` (p. 229, folio 218), `Urdu trans. Muhammad Baqir. Lahore, 1965` (p. 235, folio 225) and `Ed. Lahore, 1312` (p. 238, folio 228); Lahore is also recorded as the destination of the Jama'at-i-Islami in 1939 (pp. 214–231, folios 203–220).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated — "the early Muslim saints in general, and the Chishti masha'ikh in particular"; author's argument): "Not only for Gujarat, Bengal, but also for Uttar Pradesh, Punjab, and the Deccan does the significance of the early Muslim saints in general, and the Chishti masha'ikh in particular, mark a particular kind of South Asian spiritual authority that is not only political and religious but also cultural"; the authors describe "a spectrum of spiritually charged spaces or sites" that are "devotional loci for Indian Muslims from several classes, and also for Hindus" (p. 110, folio 97). Punjab is one of five regions named together; nothing specific to Punjab is said.
- (book's word: "the Punjab", which the book calls one of "two provinces"; refers to: Akbar's reign, the Fatehpur Sikri phase, which he "abandoned ... by 1585" — as the book states; author's argument): through Shaykh Salim and "Shaykh Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar in the Punjab", "Through a twofold, redoubled Chishti loyalty, Akbar could anchor his imperial legitimacy in two provinces adjacent to Uttar Pradesh, the Punjab and Rajasthan, both of which also happened to be crucial to the political-military ambitions of his reign" (pp. 112–113, folios 99–100). The book's own unit is a Mughal-period "province"; its extent is not stated.
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the eighteenth century"; book's historical prose): "The Nizami Chishtis came to prominence in the eighteenth century, principally in Delhi, the Deccan, and Punjab, through masters who were related to but in tension with local political leaders" (p. 119, folio 106).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "Punjabi Hindus"; refers to: Shaykh Sulayman Taunsawi's time, "as British colonialism began to take hold of India" — Taunsawi is "(d. 1850)" on the same page; book's historical prose): Taunsawi "urged his followers to participate in resistance against Sikh aggression in the Punjab, and so, apparently for the first time in the annals of Chishti history, we have spiritual biographies drawn into partisanship on behalf of one political group (Muslim) over against another (non-Muslim)"; "Notable, however, is the effort of Shah Sulayman to maintain cordial relations with Punjabi Hindus even while he opposed their Sikh neighbors" (p. 121, folio 108). The supporting endnote (n. 10) cites M. Zameeruddin Siddiqi, "The Resurgence of the Chishti Silsilah in the Punjab during the Eighteenth Century" (p. 214, folio 203).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the southern Punjab"; refers to: Taunsawi "(d. 1850)", his successor Muhammad 'Aqil "(d. 1814)" and the latter's disciple Gul Muhammad Ahmadpuri "(d. 1827)" — as printed; book's historical prose): Taunsawi "consolidated the order by stressing learning through establishment of institutions: not only the academy called Dar al- 'Ulum at Taunsa on the upper Indus, but also other religious schools throughout the Punjab"; 'Aqil and Ahmadpuri "both of whom founded religious schools in the southern Punjab that provided free instruction and lodging to hundreds of students" (pp. 121–122, folios 108–109).
- (book's word: "the upper Punjab"; refers to: "the nineteenth century"; book's historical prose): the line from Shah Mirra "resurfaces in the nineteenth century in the figure of Hafiz Sayyid Muhammad 'Ali Khayrabadi, marking the expansion of the Nizami branch of Chishti Sufism through disciples of Shaykh Sulayman Taunsawi in the upper Punjab" (p. 71, folio 57).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the colonial period"; author's argument): "If we were to follow only the dictates of political history and the criteria of social science, the test case for the Chishti Nizami Sulaymaniyya lineage in the colonial period might seem to be the battles over shrines in the Punjab"; the example given is Taunsa, and "Political power struggles internal to the Sulaymaniyya can be traced to British manipulation and also the rivalry of local Sikhs, Hindus, and Muslim reformers" (p. 124, folio printed "Ill" [text-layer?] [111]). The authors then set this political reading aside: "political contestation is only part of the narrative".
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: present day ("continues to be intoned"); ethnographic present): "The name Qalandar continues to be intoned in popular qawwali lyrics of the Punjab, and it has been appropriated by a mainstream Sufi group, the Indian Qalandari order centered at Kakori near Lucknow" (p. 36, folio 22). Which side of the border is not stated.

## From Saiyid Athar Abbas Rizvi, A History of Sufism in India, vol. 1

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rizvi_history_of_sufism_india_1.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

**38 bullets across chunks 002, 003, 004, 006, 007, 008, 011, 020, 026 and 027, and they fall into two
sharply different kinds.** For most of the book Hujwiri is a *text*, cited at second hand through
Nicholson's translation; in chunk 007 (pp. 130–131, folios 112–113) he becomes a *person*, with a
birthplace, a teacher, a journey, a city that disliked him, a tomb and three irreconcilable death
dates. The book's names for him: "‘Ali bin ‘Usman al-Jullabi al-Hujwiri" (p. 41, folio 25),
"Abu'l-Hasan ʿAli bin ʿUsman bin ʿAli al-Ghaznawi al-Jullabi al-Hujwiri" (p. 80, folio 64), "Shaikh
‘Ali Hujwiri" (p. 139, folio 121), "Shaikh Hujwiri" (p. 130, folio 112) and, most often, plain
"Hujwiri".

- **Saint**
  - **Birth place and year, given approximately.** "As his name implies, Hujwiri was born in the Ghazna
    suburb, about 1009" (p. 130, folio 112). **The suburb is not named beyond what the nisba implies —
    (not stated).**
  - **His teachers, named from his own book.** "He studied under several sufi masters, some of whom he
    names in his Kashf al-Mahjub were Abu'l-Qasim Gurgani, Khwaja Muzaffar, Abu'l-‘Abbas Ahmad bin
    Muhammad al-Ashqani. However, Khattali of Syria was his main teacher" (p. 130, folio 112) — the
    teacher being "Abu'l-Fazl Muhammad bin al-Hasan Khattali, a disciple of Husri (d. 371/981-82) of the
    school of Junaid" (p. 130, folio 112). The index carries the teacher too: "Abu'l-Fazl Muhammad b. …
    al-Hasan … Khattali, 111" (p. 458, folio 434, the entry scrambled by the OCR's column reading).
  - **Sent to Lahore against his own objection.** His pir "asked a young disciple … to follow Husain
    Zinjani" to the Panjab; "Hujwiri objected on the grounds that already an eminent sufi was there. His
    pir, however, ordered him to obey" (p. 130, folio 112).
  - **The arrival, dated by inference, and the death that met it.** "Hujwiri reached Lahore after dark.
    In the morning Shaikh Husain Zinjani's coffin was carried out for burial—he had died during the
    night" (p. 130, folio 112); "It would seem that Hujwiri probably reached Lahore in c. 1035, while
    Sultan Mas‘ud I (1031-41) reigned in Ghazna" (p. 130, folio 112). **Rizvi marks the date as
    inference ("It would seem … probably").**
  - **A trans-regional life after settling, given in numbers.** "Even after settling in Lahore, Hujwiri
    kept in contact with sufis in Transoxiana, Khurasan and Syria. He made long trips at least twice
    during his lifetime, each for more than two years. When Khattali died in 1065, Hujwiri was by his
    side. After 1067, Hujwiri returned to Lahore, remaining there until his death" (p. 130, folio 112).
    **So the "settling" of c. 1035 is interrupted by at least one long absence; see Doubts.**
  - **Three incompatible death dates in one paragraph, one of them read off the tomb, and the archive
    row prints a fourth position as settled.** "According to the tablet on his tomb, the saint died in
    465/1072-73. Nicholson believed that Hujwiri died sometime between 465 and 469/1076—77. However, the
    Kashf al-Mahjub recorded Khwaja ‘Abdullah Ansari, who died in 1089, as amongst the contemporary
    saints. It would seem, therefore, that Hujwiri died some time after that date" (p. 130, folio 112).
    **Rizvi's own conclusion is the latest of the three: after 1089** — which is also what the index
    prints, "Hujwiri Abu'l Hasan ‘Ali b. “Usman (d. after 1089), 29, 34, 43, 45, 52. 61, 64, 90, 92,
    97-99, 101, 111-13, 121, 302" (p. 468, folio 444). **The archive row carries 1072, i.e. the tomb
    tablet, the one reading Rizvi argues against.** See Doubts.
  - **The title, its meaning, and that it is posthumous.** "Later Muslims posthumously conferred on
    Shaikh Hujwiri the title, Data Ganj Bakhsh, ‘Distributor of (Unlimited) Treasure’" (p. 130,
    folio 112). **The index carries the archive's own name only as a bare cross-reference with no
    numbers of its own: "Data Ganj Bakhsh, see Hujwiri" (p. 464, folio 440).**

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The tomb as a standing, visitable site, and as a place of ascetic practice.** "His tomb has always
    been greatly venerated by sufis and Muslims alike. Among early mystics who undertook hard ascetic
    exercises in Lahore at the Shaikh's tomb was Khwaja Mui‘nu'd-Din [OCR?] [Muʿinuʾd-Din] Chishti, the
    founder of the leading Indian order, the Chishtiyya" (p. 130, folio 112). **No location within
    Lahore, no builder, no date and no description of the tomb are given — all (not stated). This is the
    only sentence in the book that links the region's two most-visited shrines, and it is a claim about
    ascetic practice at a grave.**
  - **The two Lahore tombs together.** Khwaja Muʿinuʾd-Din "reached Lahore and stayed near the tombs of
    Shaikh Husain Zanjani and Shaikh ‘Ali Hujwiri" (p. 139, folio 121). **Four positions on whether he
    arrived at a tomb or at a living man are printed on that one page — see Doubts and the
    `shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib` subsection.** Rizvi's own ruling: "Fawaidu'l Fu‘ad's
    statement appears to be correct and it is likely that the Khwaja stayed near the two tombs" (p. 139,
    folio 121, footnote 3).
  - **"Data Darbar" as such, and any tomb, ʿurs or custodian at Lahore, is not an index headword** —
    (not stated) (p. 468, folio 444; p. 464, folio 440).

- **Lahore, and the hostility of his own city**
  - **He was opposed there, and said so.** "The Kashf al-Mahjub suggests that in the eleventh century a
    number of mystics and scholars who had settled in Lahore were strongly hostile to the views of its
    author." An unnamed "expert in Qur'anic commentaries, sharply disagreed with his interpretation of
    fana' and baqa.'"; "Some Lahore sufis believed that gnosis emanated from inspiration; Hujwiri
    disparagingly called this view ‘Brahmanical.’"; and "These developments so distressed Hujwiri that he
    considered himself a ‘captive among uncongenial folk’ in Lahore." (p. 131, folio 113), footnoted
    "Nicholson, p. 91. The original does not indicate that he was actually imprisoned, see Nicholson's
    preface p. X." **Rizvi explicitly cancels a literal reading of "captive".**
  - **The one Lahore imprint in the bibliography is his book, not his shrine.** "Hujwiri,
    Kashfu'l-Mahjub, Lahore, 1923, English tr. R.A. Nicholson, London, 1936." (p. 442, folio 418).

- **The Kashf al-Mahjub, and what Rizvi uses it for**
  - **Rizvi's assessment, which is the warrant for citing him throughout.** "Hujwiri's Kashf al-Mahjub is
    also the first known manual of sufism written in Persian. Composed towards the end of his life, the
    work draws upon the vast source material available in Arabic and is a most authoritative exposition
    of sufism according to Junaid's school"; "Hujwiri however, is not merely a compiler. Firstly, he
    explains the doctrines of various schools, then he relates and assesses them. His comments form the
    most authoritative classification of sufi thoughts and practices" (p. 131, folio 113). Also "Another
    prolific sufi author was Abu'l-Hasan ʿAli bin ʿUsman bin ʿAli al-Ghaznawi al-Jullabi al-Hujwiri. Of
    his many works only his greatest, the Kashf al-Mahjub, has survived" (p. 80, folio 64), in the
    section headed "Sufi Literature" (p. 78, folio 62), between Qushairi and Ghazali. **No date of
    composition, no patron, no place of writing — (not stated).** The index gives the book its own
    headword: "Kashf al-Mahjub, 28-29, 37, 47, 64, 90, 111-13, 171" (p. 471, folio 447).
  - **Eight works listed from the Kashf al-Mahjub itself, in a numbered list whose numerals 4–8 are
    destroyed.** "1. Minhaj al-Din, a work on sufi practices, containing a detailed account of the Ahl-i
    Suffa and a full biography of Husain bin Mansur al-Hallaj. 2. Asrar al-khiraq wa'l-ma‘unat, a work on
    the patched garments worn by sufis. 3. The Kitab-i fana' wa baqa' written, the author relates, ‘in
    the vanity and rashness of youth.’"; then, un-numbered in the OCR, "A work of which no title is
    given, in which the sayings of al-Hallaj are explained", "The Kitab al-Bayan li-Ahl al-‘Iyan, a work
    on the union with God", "The Bahr al-Qulub", "Al-Ri‘ayat li-Huqug [OCR?] [li-Huquq] Allah on Divine
    Unity", "A work on faith of which the title is not specified" (p. 131, folio 113). **The count of
    eight is the Pass 1 worker's reading of a damaged list — see Doubts.**
  - **The four Righteous Caliphs as sufi exemplars, all from his book** (p. 41, folio 25): Abu Bakr as "a
    paragon of voluntary poverty to the sufis" who "taught them to renounce all their material goods for
    Islam" (footnote 1, to the Kashf al-Mahjub, pp. 70-1); ‘Umar's "patched gown or muraqqa’ [OCR?]
    [muraqqaʿ]" as "a symbol of his self-denial", with the doctrine that "spiritualists outwardly were a
    part of mankind, but inwardly their hearts clung to God" (footnote 2, "ibid. p. 72"); ‘Usman's life
    as "the best of all possible examples in resignation during a crisis" (footnote 3, "ibid, pp. 73-4");
    and ‘Ali as "their Shaikh (leader or teacher) in both the theory and practice of sufism", a model "in
    respect to the truths of outward expressions and the subtleties of inward meanings, the stripping of
    one's self of all property either of this world or of the next, and consideration of divine
    providence" (footnote 4, "ibid, p. 74").
  - **The Uways al-Qarani material** — the saying that safety lay in solitude because "the heart of the
    solitary one was free from thoughts of others" carries footnote 5, "Sibid" [OCR?] [ibid] (p. 41,
    folio 25). **The prayer of al-Baqir** — "O my God and my Lord, night has come, and the power of
    monarchs has ceased" — is footnoted to the Kashf al-Mahjub, p. 78 (pp. 42–43, folios 26–27).
  - **Two episodes recorded in his own voice.** "The following conversation was recorded in the Kashf
    al-Mahjub between a Bedouin and Hasan on the idea of sabr (patience)" (p. 44, folio 28) — ending
    "This is the mark of true sincerity" and "Association with the wicked produces suspicion of the
    good"; and "The following passage from Hujwiri's Kashf al-Mahjub, relates the significance of
    sincerity and devotion in prayers, rather than language and form" (p. 45, folio 29) — the
    Hasan-of-Basra-and-Habib dream, quoted under Legends below.
  - **He is the authority on a Balkh Sufi's lost books** — "According to Hujwiri, Shaqiq wrote a number
    of books on Sufism but none are in existence" (p. 51, folio 35).
  - **He summarises the malāmatī path, with a verse.** Of Hamdun al-Qassar's doctrine of blame: "Hujwiri
    sums it up like this: 'In true love there is nothing sweeter than blame, because blame of the Beloved
    makes no impression on the lover's heart: he heeds not what strangers say, for his heart is ever
    faithful to the object of his love.' / ''Tis sweet to be reviled for passion's sake.'" (p. 59,
    folio 43), footnoted "Nicholson, pp. 67, 125-26; NU, p. 60" — **the siglum `NU` is nowhere expanded
    in the book; see Doubts.** Restated later: "As Hujwiri explained at great length, malamat (blame) was
    the distinctive practice of the Qassaris" (p. 324, folio 302), footnoted "Nicholson, pp. 183-84, See
    Chapter One. pp. 41-42, supra." **This is the book's doctrinal background for the archive's only
    Malamati row, `madho-lal-hussain`, and the book makes no connection between the two — see Doubts.**
  - **He glosses a saying of Shah ibn Shujaʿ of Kirman in his own voice.** "Hujwiri records an interesting
    sentence Abu'l Fawaris is reported to have expressed, and makes the following comment: '"The eminent
    have eminence until they see it, and the saints have saintship until they see it," that is, whoever
    regards his eminence loses its reality, and whoever regards his saintship loses its reality.'"
    (p. 61, folio 45).
  - **He settles fanaʾ and baqaʾ against two named errors.** "Hujwiri comments: 'In annihilation there is
    no love or hate, and in subsistence there is no consciousness of union or separation. Some wrongly
    imagine that annihilation signifies loss of essence and destruction of personality, and that
    subsistence indicates the subsistence of God in Man; both these notions are absurd.'" (p. 68,
    folio 52; footnoted "Nicholson, p. 243").
  - **He is the source for the eternity of the spirit and for the Hululis.** "Discussing the theory of the
    eternity of the spirit, Hujwiri related that Sunnis also believed that the spirit was non-eternal
    (muhdas), that it existed prior to the formation of the body, but that it could not be transferred
    from one body to another" (p. 77, folio 61); "Hujwiri reminds us that Hululis and other followers of
    metempsychosis who believed that the spirit was an eternal characteristic of God, stressed that he
    could never become an attribute of His creatures" (p. 77, folio 61); his own words, "'The spirit is
    created and is under God's command. Anyone who holds another belief is in flagrant error and cannot
    distinguish what is non-eternal from what is eternal. No saint, if his saintship be sound, can
    possibly be ignorant of the attributes of God.'" (p. 77, folio 61; "Nicholson, pp. 263-64"). **Note
    the internal tangle in the middle sentence — see Doubts.** The back-reference in Chapter Six places
    him among the *evidence for Hindu–sufi interaction*: "However Hujwiri's disappointment with certain
    sufis of his own time, whom he called Hululis, has already been mentioned, as has his assessment of
    current Brahmanical influence on sufi thought in Islam" (p. 345, folio 323), footnoted "Nicholson,
    pp. 236, 243, 260-66, 271".
  - **He is the arbiter of which sufi sects are legitimate.** "According to Hujwiri the sufi sects named
    after the above Masters were approved ones. He gives a detailed description of each in the Kashf
    al-Mahjub; Nicholson, pp. 176-260" (p. 77, folio 61, footnote 1); the Masters and cities in the
    sentence the note hangs on are "al-Hakim in Tirmiz, Abu'l-ʿAbbas Sayyar in Marw, Qassar in Nishapur,
    Bayazid in Bastam, Khafif in Shiraz, Sahl in Tustar, and around Kharraz, Muhasibi, Junaid and Nuri in
    Baghdad" (p. 77, folio 61).
  - **Rizvi ranks him above Suhrawardi on samaʿ, outright.** "However, the Shaikh was unable to excel
    Hujwiri's unique means of defending sama', who says:" followed by the passage on *pay-bazi* and
    *iztirab* (pp. 110–111, folios 92–93). His book is also the benchmark for Suhrawardi's ethics:
    Suhrawardi's section "was a marked improvement over related chapters in Hujwiri's Kashf al-Mahjub;
    Suhrawardi could make an outstanding contribution on this very important subject for by the twelfth
    century khanqah life was at its highest peak" (p. 109, folio 91); and he is the source of the
    two-fold division the whole hospitality code rests on — "Like Hujwiri, Suhrawardi divided the people
    of khanqahs into two: residents and travellers" (p. 109, folio 91).
  - **The khirqa's prophetic warrant and its allegorical reading.** "According to Hujwiri, the wearing of
    the muragqa [OCR?] [muraqqa] or khirqa was practised by Muhammad and his companions" (p. 120,
    folio 102); "Hujwiri gives the following allegorical interpretation of the cloak's significance: ‘Its
    collar is annihilation of intercourse (with men), its two sleeves are observance (hifz) and
    continence (‘ismat), its two gussets are poverty and purity, its belt is persistence in
    contemplation, its hem (Kursi) is tranquillity in (God's) presence, and its fringe is settlement in
    the abode of union.’" (p. 120, folio 102).
  - **The novice's three-year probation — the one initiation rule in Chapter One with a named source and
    a number.** "According to Hujwiri, sufi Shaikhs prescribed a probationary period to novices of three
    years before instructing them in the real secrets of the Path (Tariga [OCR?] [Tariqa]). The first
    year of sufi apprenticeship was devoted to serving the people; the second, to the service of God and
    the third to guarding the heart" (p. 117, folio 99), sourced only as "Nicholson, p. 54".
  - **His book is a model of literary style for Amir Khusraw.** "1. The style of sufis and saints, which
    is of two kinds: (a) Of men of "gravity and stations," like that to be met with in Kashfu'l-Mahjub,
    Suluku'l-Musafirin, and other similar works" (p. 189, folio 171). **The person is not named there at
    all — the mapping is on the title; see Doubts.**
  - **The Kashf al-Mahjub is twice quoted without its author being named.** "This following quotation from
    the Kashf al-Mahjub is from amongst Ahmad's more important sayings" (p. 53, folio 37), introducing
    Ahmad ibn Khazruya's saying that "the way to God is like the blazing sun; do thou seek thyself, for
    when thou hast found thyself thou art come to thy journey's end, inasmuch as God is too manifest to
    admit of His being sought"; and "In this passage from the Kashfu al-Mahjub he says" (p. 62, folio 46),
    introducing Sahl al-Tustari on Law and Truth (p. 63, folio 47). **The title is spelled two ways,
    "Kashf al-Mahjub" (p. 53) and "Kashfu al-Mahjub" (p. 62).**

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The hierarchy and cosmic function of the saints, with Hujwiri's claim of direct sight.** "Hujwiri
    saw in sufi saints a vehicle by which 'the Truth and the proof of Muhammad's veracity' could be
    continued. Drawing upon Tirmizi's thesis of sainthood, he outlined the hierarchy of saints" (p. 115,
    folio 97): "(God) has made the Saints the governors of the universe"; "Through the blessing of their
    advent the rain falls from heaven, and through the purity of their lives the plants spring up from
    the earth, and through their spiritual influence the Muslims gain victories over the unbelievers";
    "Among them there are four thousand who are concealed and do not know one another"; and the graded
    officers — "three hundred, called Akhyar, and forty, called Abdal, and seven, called Abrar, and four,
    called Awtad, and three, called Nugaba [OCR?] [Nuqaba], and one, called Qutb or Ghaws. All these know
    one another and cannot act save by mutual consent." — closing with "I myself—God be praised!—have had
    ocular experience (khabar-i 'iyan) of this matter." (pp. 115–116, folios 97–98). **On the bare name
    "Tirmizi" see Doubts.**
  - **Hasan of Basra corrected by God in a dream, from his book.** "The same night, Hasan dreamed that he
    saw God and said to Him: ‘O Lord, wherein does Thy good pleasure consist?' and that God answered: ‘O
    Hasan, you found My good pleasure, but did not know its value: if ... you had said your prayers after
    Habib, and if the rightness of his intention had restrained you from taking offence at his
    pronunciation, I should have been well pleased with you'" (p. 45, folio 29).

- **Significance**
  - **Rizvi treats him as the authority who classifies Sufism and as a saint whose grave drew the founder
    of the Chishtiyya** (pp. 130–131, folios 112–113). **Everything else the archive would want — the
    shrine's fabric, its ʿurs, its custodians, its income — is (not stated) in 491 pages**, and every
    quotation from him in this book is at second hand, through Nicholson's English.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

| row | the book prints | the archive row | status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `data-darbar` | three positions on one page — tomb tablet 465/1072-73; Nicholson, between 465 and 469/1076-77; **Rizvi's own: after 1089** (p. 130, folio 112); index "d. after 1089" (p. 468, folio 444) | `figure_died` **1072** | **The row prints the tomb-tablet reading, which is the one Rizvi explicitly argues against. A human must decide whether the entry keeps 1072, prints all three, or follows the book.** |

3. **`data-darbar`'s title-only bullet stretches the 18 September ruling one step.** At p. 189 (folio 171)
   the book prints only "Kashfu'l-Mahjub" inside Amir Khusraw's list of styles; **the person is not named
   at all**, and the link to the row's principal figure rests on the authorship the book states elsewhere
   (p. 109, folio 91). **A consolidator who will not accept a title-only mention should delete that
   bullet; nothing else depends on it.** The same judgement covers the single subordinate clause at p. 324
   (folio 302), which carries no fact about the man, the book or the shrine.

### Place-level material: LAHORE

*The book's place-level material on "LAHORE" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi, bibi-pak-daman, darbar-abul-muali-qadri, data-darbar, ganj-e-inayat-sarkar, ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed, gurdwara-babay-nanki, gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore, gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan, gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang, gurdwara-chowmala-sahib, gurdwara-dera-sahib, gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji, gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore, gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh, gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian, jain-mandir-lahore, khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz, krishna-mandir-ravi-road, loh-temple-lava-temple, madho-lal-hussain, mazar-e-iqbal, peer-makki, samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh, shah-jamal, shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh, shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal, shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari, shrine-of-mian-mir, shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib, shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri, tahir-bandagi-qadri, tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak, valmik-mandir-naqi-road, wasif-ali-wasif** — **LAHORE, the archive's 35 rows.** What the book actually attaches to the bare name: an eleventh-century city hostile to its own saint — "a number of mystics and scholars who had settled in Lahore were strongly hostile to the views of its author", "Some Lahore sufis believed that gnosis emanated from inspiration", "a ‘captive among uncongenial folk’ in Lahore" (p. 131, folio 113); a route terminus, "one of the main routes from Multan to Lahore and Delhi" (p. 158, folio 140) and "Qazi Shu'aib, went to Lahore" (p. 157, folio 139); a capital, "Lahore had become the capital", with "the Khwaja reached Delhi from Lahore" and "His father, Ahmad, migrated from Lahore to Delhi" (pp. 135, 140, 145; folios 117, 122, 127); a city destroyed and a merchant colony — "the destruction of Lahore, by the Mongols in 1241" and "A group of Lahore merchants visited Gujarat" (pp. 241–242, folios 221–222); a city Babur conquered in 1523-24 (p. 367, folio 345); a compass bearing, "about forty miles south-west of Lahore" (p. 407, folio 383); and one named building that is not a shrine, "the Padishahi mosque at Lahore" (p. 339, folio 317, footnote 3), inside Goldziher on relic worship. **Chunk 001's Doubts records four further Lahore mentions in pp. 1–29 whose individual page references are not carried in the Pass 1 text; the conversion is recorded at chunk level (pp. 1–29) and a human must recover the pages.** **NOT converted: the two Lahore-as-imprint mentions — "published in Lahore in 1909" and "Oriental College Magazine, Lahore X/1" (pp. 300, 310; folios 278, 288), and the Lahore imprints throughout the bibliography (pp. 442, 446, 452, 454; folios 418, 422, 428, 430) — which are publication data, not places in the narrative.**

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Ghaznawid rule, "Yamain al-Dawla Mahmud (388/998-421/1030)" and "Bahram Shah (1118-52)" as printed; book's historical prose): Mahmud "established Ghaznawid rule over Khurasan, Afghanistan and the Panjab"; after two defeats by 'Ala'u'd-Din of Ghur, "Bahram Shah was forced to flee to the Panjab" (p. 83, folio 67).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Mahmud of Ghazna's annexation and after, with Hujwiri reaching Lahore "in c. 1035, while Sultan Mas'ud I (1031-41) reigned in Ghazna"; book's historical prose): "the annexation of the Panjab by Mahmud of Ghazna and its incorporation into his empire prompted many sufis to settle in the area"; Khattali "ordered his disciple, Shaikh Husain Zinjani to move to the Panjab", and later sent Hujwiri after him (p. 130, folio 112).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "the middle of the twelfth century", after "'Ala'u'd-Din Husain of Ghur in 1150-51, followed by the Ghuzz invasion six years later"; book's historical prose): Baba Farid's ancestors "came from Kabul, but migrated to the Panjab in the middle of the twelfth century" (p. 156, folio 138); the destruction of Ghazna and the Ghuzz invasion "drove a large number of people from that region to the Panjab for safety"; the book names "The Ghaznawid Sultan of the Panjab" as the one who made Farid's father Qazi of Kahtwal (p. 157, folio 139).
- (book's word: "the southern part of the Panjab"; refers to: "1175", as printed; book's historical prose): "A hundred and fifty years later in 1175 Mu'izzu'd-Din Muhammad bin Sam seized Multan, establishing firm control and Sunni rule over the southern part of the Panjab" (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: after Jalalu'd-Din's defeat "in November 1221" and Chingiz Khan's march back west; book's historical prose): Chingiz Khan's "absence enabled Itutmish [OCR?] [Iltutmish] to consolidate power in the Panjab, seven years later routing from Sind his rival, Nasiru'd-Din Qubacha" (p. 152, folio 134).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "previous years" before Muhammad bin Tughluq's scheme "attempted in 1327"; book's historical prose): the Sultan raised "the slogan of jihad against the Mongols, who in previous years had devastated the Panjab and the region around Delhi" (p. 193, folio 175).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "Timur's invasion in 1398-99", as printed; book's historical prose): "Timur's invasion in 1398-99 devastated both the Panjab and Delhi" (p. 290, folio 270).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: the time of Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din Bukhari, Makhdum-i Jahaniyan, born "15 Sha'ban 707/9 February 1308", and of "Sultan Firuz Shah Tughlug" [OCR?] [Tughluq]; book's historical prose): the Suhrawardiyya order "spread from Uch to Gujarat, the Panjab and Kashmir even extending its influence to the Delhi of Sultan Firuz Shah Tughlug [OCR?] [Tughluq]" (p. 299, folio 277).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the time of "Mongol domination of Central Asia and Iran", Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya and Baba Farid; book's historical prose): Qalandariyyas and Jawaliqis passed through Multan, where they "often came into conflict with Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya"; "From the Punjab, the displeased qalandars moved to Delhi creating scenes wherever they saw a khanqah. Some went as far east as Bengal" (p. 325, folio 303).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "the fifteenth century", with "The Mongol invasions between the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries" behind it; book's historical prose): "In the fifteenth century, the Panjab enjoyed a peaceful period due to Afghan rule. The Mongol invasions between the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries had devastated portions of the Panjab and the Afghans had successfully contributed to its re-settlement and urbanization by founding many new towns and promoting trade and commerce" (p. 408, folio 384).
- (book's word: "the Panjab", "west Panjab"; refers to: "About 1520" and Babur's third invasion "in 926/1520", as printed; book's historical prose): Guru Nanak returned from eastern India "only to find the Panjab plunged into a number of significant political crises"; Babur "started unsuccessful negotiations with Ibrahim to annex west Panjab to Kabul. After two minor raids around the region between the Indus and Jhelum, Babur invaded a third time over the Panjab in 926/1520 reaching as far as Sialkot"; a footnote says Daulat Khan "Gradually he also became the governor of the Panjab and in [1523 [OCR?] invited Babur to invade India" (p. 409, folio 385).
- (book's word: "Panjabi", "the Panjab"; refers to: "Guru Nanak's period"; quoted source, W.H. McLeod, *Guru Nanak and the Sikh religion*, quoted so that Rizvi can reject it): "the Panjabi sufism of Guru Nanak's period had evidently departed radically from the classical pattern of Arab and Persian Sufism"; most Muslims Nanak met "would, however, represent in varying degrees the blend of modified orthodoxy and debased Sufism which was dominant … in the Muslim community of the Panjab during this period" (pp. 419–420, folios 395–396).
- (book's word: "Panjabis", "the Panjab"; refers to: "the time of Guru Nanak"; author's argument, against McLeod): "all literate Panjabis, both Hindus and Muslims, at the time of Guru Nanak were conversant with" 'Attar, Rumi and Sa'di; McLeod "seems to be unaware of Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus Gangohi who lived in Shahabad, his disciple, Shaikh Jalal of Thaneswar, and the Qadiris who had made the Panjab the main centre of their activities" (p. 420, folio 396).

## From Omar Kasmani, Queer Companions

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/kasmani_queer_companions.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

*Both site-specific (named twice as a shrine) and inside the Lahore place-level line below.*

- **Shrine / Darbar.** "Then, once the fair was over, Baba decided to board a bus, arriving at Data
  Darbar, Lahore's most prominent shrine complex. It was here that, according to Baba, they first
  found mentorship under the tutelage of a fakir." (p. 65, folio 48) Baba stayed roughly a year:
  "Another year would pass and even though the dreams from the village weren't really forgotten, the
  visions had stopped." (p. 65, folio 48) In the same paragraph, "It was the time of the saint's
  festival, and countless other fakirs had gathered in the city." (p. 65, folio 48) — **the saint is
  not named and this must not be read as Data Ganj Bakhsh's ʿurs**; see Doubts.
- **The shrine named in the References, spelled two ways four years apart.** `Strothmann, Linus.
  2012. "Managing Piety at the Shrine of Data Ganj Bukhsh, Lahore, Pakistan." PhD diss., Freie
  Universität Berlin.` and `Strothmann, Linus. 2016. Managing Piety: The Shrine of Data Ganj Bakhsh.
  Karachi: Oxford University Press.` (both p. 216, folio 199). Strothmann 2016 is cited for shrine
  economics: "For an account of the distribution of shrine revenues and the economic dimension of a
  Sufi shrine in Lahore, see Strothmann (2016, chapters 5 and 6)." (p. 189, folio 172, ch. 1 n. 26)
  — **the body text does not say which Lahore shrine that is**; the book's own bibliography says
  Strothmann's shrine is Data Ganj Bakhsh.
- **The row's principal figure, Hazrat Ali ibn Usman al-Hujwiri (Data Ganj Bakhsh), is never named in
  the book: (not stated).** No date, no custodian, no ʿurs date and no description of the complex
  beyond "Lahore's most prominent shrine complex". The index carries no `Data Ganj Bakhsh` or
  `Hujwiri` headword (p. 219, folio 202).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **Name-spelling divergences between book and archive, all of which a consolidator will hit:** book
  "Bahaʾuddin Zakaria" vs archive "Bahauddin Zakariya"; book "`Faridud-din` Ganjshakr" vs archive
  "Baba Fariduddin Mas'ud Ganjshakar"; book "`Pak-­Pattan`" vs archive "Pakpattan"; book "Jalaluddin
  Surkhposh Bukhari" vs archive "Sayyid Jalaluddin Surkh-Posh Bukhari"; book "Uchh" vs archive "Uch
  Sharif"; book "Madho Lal Husain" vs archive "Madho Lal Hussain"; book "Bibiyan-pak-daman" vs
  archive "Bibi Pak Daman"; book "Data Ganj Bukhsh" and "Data Ganj Bakhsh" four years apart. **The
  death years 1267, 1266 and 1291 match the archive exactly, which is what makes those three
  identifications safe.**

- **Ali / Mawla, Sajjad (Ali b. Zayn al-Abidin), Hussayn, Zuljanah, the Prophet Muhammad, Ibn ʿArabi,
  Ibn Battuta, Jamal al-Din Savi, Raja Bharthari / Raja Virag, Khizr, Devraj, the fairies** — none is
  the principal figure of any archive row. In particular `data-darbar`'s principal figure, "Hazrat
  Ali ibn Usman al-Hujwiri", is a different person from the book's Ali, and the row is mapped on the
  named shrine instead. `tilla-jogian` and `gorakhnath-goraknath-temple` are **not** triggered: the
  book does not connect Raja Bharthari to Gorakhnath or the Naths (p. 188, folio 171, ch. 1 n. 15).

- **"It was the time of the saint's festival"** (p. 65, folio 48) sits two sentences after the Data
  Darbar mention but **names no saint**, and the same paragraph also mentions "another saint's fair
  in a distant place in Sindh Prov-ince". **It must not be read as Data Ganj Bakhsh's ʿurs.**

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's place-level material on "Lahore" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

  - **A terror attack on a Lahore shrine changed security at Sehwan.** "A great deal of what I had
    observed in 2009 was already changing as early as 2011. A terror attack at Lahore's most
    prominent shrine had led to security measures being beefed up at major Sufi sites across the
    country." (p. 104, folio 87) **The shrine is not named and the attack is not dated: (not
    stated)**; the 2011 date attaches to the tightening at Sehwan, not to the attack.
  - **Lahore as the source of Sehwan's pilgrim traffic.** "special trains like the Jhule Lal Express
    were initiated to facilitate the annual journey of pilgrims connecting Sehwan with Lahore, the
    capital of Punjab" (p. 70, folio 53); the Bukhari brothers "would go on to organize pilgrimages
    from Lahore to Sehwan, reserving entire trains for their annual journeys. Private patronage such
    as theirs greatly complemented the state's project in Sehwan as much as it was motivated by it."
    (p. 61, folio 44)
  - **Lahore as a place fakirs go to and come from.** Baba boarded a bus and arrived "at Data Darbar,
    Lahore's most prominent shrine complex", finding mentorship under a fakir and staying about a
    year (p. 65, folio 48); "It was the time of the saint's festival, and countless other fakirs had
    gathered in the city." (p. 65, folio 48) A "visiting fakir from Lahore who in the autumn of 2011
    was in Sehwan to perform his duty at the shrine of Lal" kept "his fakir retreat by the banks of
    the river" (p. 195, folio 178, ch. 5 n. 9). Baba's fakir story "traverses Sufi shrines across the
    Punjab" (p. 73, folio 56).
  - **Lahore after expulsion from a Sehwan lodge.** Murad "relocated to Lahore" and was reported at
    "Bibiyan-pak-daman, a shrine in Lahore dedicated to women saints and particularly revered by the
    Shiʿa" (p. 125, folio 108); later, "imparting the same knowledge of tariqat… to others in Lahore.
    There too his status is the same" (p. 136, folio 119); "Living on the outskirts of Lahore, he had
    visited the shrine a few times only to pay his respects" (p. 141, folio 124). The young woman he
    married was "a young pilgrim visiting from Lahore" whom he "had consulted at the shrine of Bodlo"
    (p. 125, folio 108).
  - **Lahore shrine economics, cited not described.** "For an account of the distribution of shrine
    revenues and the economic dimension of a Sufi shrine in Lahore, see Strothmann (2016, chapters 5
    and 6)." (p. 189, folio 172, ch. 1 n. 26) **The book does not say which shrine.**
  - **Lahore as a place of publication.** `Chishti, Muhammad A. 2008. Syed Usman Marwandi
    al-­Maruf Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar: Hayat, Afkar-­o-­talimat […]. Lahore: Haq.` (p. 205,
    folio 188); `Schimmel, Annemarie. 2003. Mystical Dimensions of Islam. Lahore: Sang-­e-­Meel.`
    (p. 215, folio 198).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "central Punjab"; refers to: "the colonial period" set against "the postcolonial state", as the book reports them; quoted source, Umber bin Ibad 2019): "Though focusing only on central Punjab, Ibad notes that in the colonial period, “the state did not try to control shrines directly through its bureaucracy”; however, the postcolonial state has gone further, especially by virtue of implementing a unique politico-religious ideology" — in his view, a desire "to harness the pluralistic traditions of shrines in favor of “representing a Singular Islam”" (Ibad 2019, introduction) (p. 69, folio 52).
- (book's word: "central Punjab"; refers to: stated in the present tense as a general condition, framed by Baba's childhood in a Punjab village "Decades on" from the 2012–2013 tellings; period otherwise not stated; author's argument): "Being a child in a poor rural family in central Punjab, one who is regarded as intersex means being given to particular bodily horizons, oriented to certain futures more than others"; "a most likely trajectory for a child such as Baba would have eventually involved a life with the khwaja-sara"; such children, "Given male names at birth and in most cases also registered as sons", "do not extend the social promise of family lines that cisgender sons are expected to offer" (p. 73, folio 56).
- (book's word: "parts of Sindh and Punjab"; "Rural Punjab and Sindh" in the cited title; refers to: period not stated — the study is dated 2010 in the References; quoted source, Lubna Chaudhry): "In a study of women and poverty in parts of Sindh and Punjab, Lubna Chaudhry notes how unmarried girls in their teens and early twenties are most vulnerable. She points to discriminatory food portions within the family and limited access to health care, in addition to a lack of share in economic resources, even those that involve their labor" (p. 191, folio 174, ch. 2 n. 24; the study is "Women and Poverty: Salient Findings from a Gendered Analysis of a Quasi-Anthropological Study in Rural Punjab and Sindh", 2010, p. 205, folio 188).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the eighteenth century"; book's historical prose, hedged "It is possible"): on the Nanakpanth among Sindh's Hindus: "The Nanakpanth would have been initiated later. It is possible that the Aroras imported this cult from Punjab during their migrations in Sindh in the eighteenth century. The Nanakpanth is a form of Sikhism that does not recognize the authority of the Khalsa" (p. 43, folio 30).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the second half of the nineteenth century"; book's historical prose, citing Oberoi 1994): "the first attempt to rationalize and uniformize the Sikh tradition was implemented. This process of normalizing the Sikh tradition was centred on Punjab, and it did not concern the Sindhi Nanakpanthis who were heterodox Sikhs" (p. 53, folio 40).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, historical; author's argument citing Lorenzen 1996): "The Nirguni tradition was more influential in Northern India, especially in Punjab, where the Sikh tradition historically derived from the Nirguni tradition of the Bhakti" (p. 89, folio 76).
- (book's word: "the southern present Punjab"; refers to: an unstated past time ("at the time when"); author's argument): the shared devotional culture "would be called Sindhian culture, which refers to a set of social, religious and cultural traits that existed in the Sindhi area at the time when it encompassed the southern present Punjab, and also the territories of Rajasthan and Gujarat" (p. 53, folio 40). The book does not say whether "present Punjab" means the Pakistani province. The Rajasthan/Gujarat half is under `not attached`.
- (book's word: "Southern Punjab", "South Punjab"; refers to: present day for the language; "the Middle Ages" for the historical claim; author's argument, in an endnote, the glossary and the main text): Ch. 6 n. 11: "The Siraiki language is mainly spoken in Southern Punjab, with Multan as capital. Interestingly, this area was a part of Sindh during the Middle Ages" (p. 216, folio 203). Glossary: Siraiki is "a language spoken in Northern Sindh and Southern Punjab" (p. 220, folio 207). In the main text, for some specialists the Siraiki of Northern Sindh is "a distinct language from the Siraiki of South Punjab" (p. 171, folio 158).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, in an endnote and Annex 2): "the Chishtiyya was not widespread in Sindh, while it is dominant in Punjab and North India" (General conclusion n. 1, p. 217, folio 204). "The Chishtiyya is usually considered to be one of the most powerful Sufi orders in Northern India and Punjab" (p. 227, folio 214). The book does not say which Punjab.
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the Pax Britannica imposed by the colonial rule", and present-day scholarship; author's argument): scholars of Sindhi studies treat Sindh's religious encounters "as if comparable dynamics were not implemented in other regions, be they neighbouring Sindh, such as Punjab, Rajasthan or Gujarat, or elsewhere". "During the Pax Britannica imposed by the colonial rule, the exchange of goods and products was reinforced between the different regions of the north-western Indian subcontinent, including Punjab, Rajasthan, Gujarat and Sindh". Beyond the merchants there were "networks of circulating knowledge, and especially religious knowledge" (p. 196, folio not stated). The book does not give this colonial "Punjab" any boundary.

    `location_short` therefore does not reach it, and the ruled count of 35 is reached without it.
    Recorded so the omission is visible and a human can rule. See Doubts.
