## From Pnina Werbner and Helene Basu (eds.), Embodying Charisma

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/werbner_basu_embodying_charisma.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

**Six bullets across four chunks, no two of the same kind, and the shrine is never described. The row
is carried by the saint-as-author ruling and by two mentions inside other people's quoted speech.**

- **Saint**
  - **Named as an author, twice.** Ewing cites him for the malamati stance, describing Sa'in Sahib's
    instruction to Rabi‘a as "a strategy well known in the Sufi tradition, the malamati stance,
    drawing blame onto oneself as a way of removing self-conceit on the path of spiritual development
    (see Hujwiri 1976:62–63)" (p. 183, folio 172); the reference-list entry reads "Hujwiri, Ali bin
    Uthman (1976) [1911] *The Kashf al-Mahjub: The Oldest Persian Treatise on Sufism*, trans.
    `Reynold A.Nicholas`[text-layer?] [Reynold A. Nicholas], Lahore: Islamic Book Foundation."
    (p. 193, folio 182). "Lahore" there is **only the publisher's city of a 1976 reprint**.
  - The Name Index headword is `Hujwiri, A. 172` (p. 246, folio 235) — a printed-page locator, and
    **no first name beyond the initial "A."**.
  - **The book gives him no tomb, no shrine, no death date, no silsila and no epithet of his own: all
    (not stated)** across pp. 180–199.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Named as a shrine only inside quoted speech, twice.** In Zindapir's speech, as proof that saints
    outrank kings: "The tombs of kings do not get izzat [honour]; only those of awliya… Like Data Ganj
    Baksh in Lahore [the shrine of the eleventh-century Sufi saint Hujweri]. If you go to the
    Emperor's tomb [Shah Jahangir's] you will find no people there. But at Data Ganj Baksh there are
    thousands of people all the time" (p. 120, folio 109). **The square-bracketed gloss identifying
    the saint as "Hujweri" and dating him to the eleventh century is Werbner's editorial insertion
    inside the quotation, not Zindapir's words.**
  - In Rabi‘a's interview speech, as a place she went to while her daughter was under another pir's
    treatment: "When she began to get treatment from the Kharian pir, I went to Data Sahib [Lahore's
    most important Sufi shrine]" (p. 178, folio 167). **The gloss "[Lahore's most important Sufi
    shrine]" is Ewing's editorial insertion into the transcript, not Rabi‘a's own words.**
  - **The saint of this shrine is never named in Ewing's passage, nor its tomb, ʿurs, custodians,
    endowment or architecture — all (not stated)** (p. 178, folio 167).
- **Practices & Events**
  - **A faqir consulted at the shrine, and a dream read there.** "I told my dream to a faqir [a
    beggar-ascetic] there" (p. 178, folio 167); "[In the dream] I saw that someone closed a water pipe
    by filling it with dust. Baba [the faqir] said, ‘Your daughter's birth canal is closed… You should
    bring your daughter before me once'" (p. 178, folio 167).
  - The only quantitative claim attached to the shrine is Zindapir's rhetorical "there are thousands
    of people all the time" (p. 120, folio 109) — **a saint's comparison, not a visitor count.**

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`data-darbar` — six mentions, none of them a description of the shrine.** Two are Hujwiri as an
  author (p. 183, folio 172; p. 193, folio 182), one is a Name Index headword (p. 246, folio 235), and
  two are the shrine inside other people's quoted speech (p. 120, folio 109; p. 178, folio 167).
  **In both quoted mentions the identifying gloss is the anthropologist's editorial insertion, not the
  speaker's words** — "[the shrine of the eleventh-century Sufi saint Hujweri]" is Werbner's, "[Lahore's
  most important Sufi shrine]" is Ewing's. **The book itself never names the saint of Data Darbar in
  its own voice and never dates him.** Also note the book's spellings differ from the archive row's:
  "Data Ganj Baksh" and "Hujweri" and "Hujwiri" against the row's "Data Ganj Bakhsh" / "al-Hujwiri".

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's place-level material on "Lahore" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore", 35 rows): Lahore is named across five chunks and never with a
  shrine attached except where the text says Data Sahib. **As the far edge of the ʿurs volunteers'
  catchment at Ghamkol Sharif** (p. 122, folio 111). **As the city Rabi‘a returns to, and as the frame
  for the neighbourhood Samnabad** (p. 180, folio 169; p. 188, folio 177). **As a publisher's city** in
  Ewing's, Malik's and Lindholm's reference lists — at least eleven times across pp. 216–218 (folios
  205–207), plus p. 193 (folio 182) and p. 203 (folio 192). The only substantive Lahore content in the
  book is the two quoted mentions of Data Ganj Baksh / Data Sahib, which are recorded under
  `data-darbar` above and are not repeated here.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjabi villages"; refers to: period not stated, ethnographic present of Werth's chapter; editors' introduction, Werbner and Basu, summarising Lukas Werth's chapter 4): "This is reflected even at the most local level, in the countless shrines to unknown saints found on the outskirts of Punjabi villages. Against the unique biographies of named saints, these anonymous shrines, Lukas Werth discloses here, call for explanation"; their anonymity is "a key feature, he suggests, of their placement as mediators between the dangerous forces of nature and cultural continuity" (p. 23, folio 12). Werth's own fieldwork is at Rajpur in the salt range (see Sweep notes); the generalisation to "Punjabi villages" is the editors'.
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; contributor's endnote, Lukas Werth, ch. 4, n. 9): "I could not find in this case expressive allusions to Khidr, who is often called Zinda pir in Punjab, because he also is thought to have disappeared alive (see Schimmel 1975: 202)" (p. 101, folio 90).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; contributor's argument, Jamal Malik, ch. 9): "The cult of the holy man (pîr) and the associated folk piety is particularly evident in the countryside, where major landlords and large-scale land concentrations are crucial features, especially in Punjab and Sindh. In many cases, the local pîr represents the local landlord or is at least closely related to him" (p. 199, folio 188).

## From Shemeem Burney Abbas, The Female Voice in Sufi Ritual

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/abbas_female_voice_sufi_ritual.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's names for him: "Hazrat Data Ganj Bakhsh Hujwiri", "Data Ganj Bakhsh Hujwiri", "Pir Ali ibn
Usman Data Ganj Bakhsh Hujwiri", "Ali Usman Data Ganj Bakhsh", "Data Sahib", "Data Darbar".

- **Saint**
  - "Pir Ali ibn Usman Data Ganj Bakhsh Hujwiri (d. [numeral dropped, text-layer])" (p. 161, folio not
    stated); a whole endnote reads only "Ali ibn Usman Data Ganj Bakhsh Hujwiri (d.  )." (p. 193,
    folio not stated) — **the parenthesis is empty, both the year and the era marker destroyed, and
    the note's own number is deleted, so what claim it supports is (not stated).** 1072 appears
    nowhere and has not been supplied.
  - The book calls him "himself a great Sufi mystic" (p. 161, folio not stated). The index gives the
    full triple name, `Hujwiri, Ali Usman Data Ganj Bakhsh`, under `Suﬁ Saints belonging to
    Pakistan-Indian silsilās`, with printed locator `xix` (p. 239, folio not stated).
  - **The book's own shorthand for him is fixed by p. 162:** "Data Sahib arrived in Lahore" (p. 162,
    folio not stated). A free-standing note elsewhere reads, in full, "Generally called ''Data
    Sahib.''" (p. 211, folio not stated) — **its callout number is destroyed, so what it glosses is
    (not stated) from the notes alone, though the book's own usage at p. 162 is the obvious referent.**
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **A fieldwork site, and the book's single most substantive passage on state-driven rebuilding.**
    "In recent years, with the coming into power of orthodox Islamic governments in Pakistan, there has
    been a trend to extend and renovate the shrines of Sufis such as Hazrat Data Ganj Bakhsh Hujwiri in
    Lahore into mosques and places of worship" (p. 20, folio not stated); "Data Darbar has been
    extended to almost ten times its original size and has been remodeled with the most expensive
    Italian marble. Huge congregations of devotees perform the Friday prayers in the shrine courtyard"
    (pp. 20–21, folios not stated). **"In recent years" is the only dating the book gives, and the
    numeral-dropping defect is *not* the cause here — the book genuinely writes "In recent years".
    "Almost ten times" survives because it is spelled out. No government is named, no year, no cost
    figure, no Auqaf, no custodian: all (not stated).**
  - **Segregation enforced by the shrine establishment at the ʿurs.** "in the popular culture of today,
    whether at the shrine or in the concert, it is usually a mixed group, except during an urs such as
    at Data Ganj Bakhsh in Lahore, when the shrine establishment segregates the women's sections. Women
    may be hidden from men's view by a curtain, and in some shrines they sit behind a curtain set up
    between them and the musicians" (p. 90, folio not stated). **The ʿurs date is (not stated).**
  - **The book ascribes a silsila the archive row leaves blank.** "In the Chishtiyya Sufi shrines in
    Pakistan, such as Hazrat Data Ganj Bakhsh Hujwiri in Lahore and Baba Fariduddin Ganj-e Shakar at
    Pakpattan Sharif, the same ritual in qawwālī discourse is followed. Since this is an exclusively
    male domain, women's presence at such performances is rare" (p. 84, folio not stated). **Recorded
    as the book's statement, side by side with the archive row's blank; not tidied.** The ritual order
    at such shrines: the ʿurs qawwālī "is always initiated with a qaul or saying of the prophet
    Muhammad, ''Mun kunto Maulā fā Alī-un Maulā,'' in Arabic"; "During an urs performance at the
    Chishtiyya shrines the poetry is focused on Ali, on the saint buried at that particular shrine, and
    on his spiritual lineage" (pp. 84–85, folios not stated).
  - **Named musicians who performed hazrī at this ʿurs**, on the authority of Lahori: "He mentions
    notable musicians such as Ustad Bare Ghulam Ali, Ustad Chote Ghulam Ali, Niaz Hussain Shami, Fateh
    Ali Mubarik, and Dina Qawwal, who performed hazrī in terms of singing mystical texts at the urs of
    Data Ganj Bakhsh in Lahore. He also mentions women musicians who performed hazrī at the Sufi
    shrines in Lahore, especially at the time of the urs" (p. 90, folio not stated). Lahori uses "the
    very term ''hazrī'' in connection with qawwāls who sang at the Sufi shrines in Lahore before the
    partition of [text-layer?: year dropped] and after it" (p. 90, folio not stated).
- **Significance**
  - A fieldwork site in the Lahore group (p. 20, folio not stated).
  - **He appears as a devotee of another shrine**, which is the whole substance of his mention in the
    concluding chapter: he "was a devotee of the shrines of the Bibi Pak Daman" and "offered prayers at
    their shrines and spent considerable time in meditation at the shrines" (pp. 161–162, folios not
    stated). **His own shrine is never named in that chapter.**
  - In a qawwāl's list of poetic sources: "We use poetic texts from Hazrat Muinuddin Chishti, Baba Ganj
    Shakar, Data Ganj Bakhsh, Amir Khusrau, and of course we mostly sing Baba Bulle Shah's poetry" —
    Muhammad Bakhsh at Kasur (p. 111, folio not stated). **A bare epithet: no "Hujwiri", no Lahore, no
    date.**
  - **Not to be conflated with the caliph.** "Hazrat Ali, the fourth caliph of Islam", "from whom the
    Chishtiyya trace descent" (p. 84, folio not stated), and the glossary entry "Ali — Cousin and
    son-in-law of the prophet Muhammad; fourth caliph of Islam and 'titular head of all Sufi
    lineages'" (p. 178, folio not stated), are a different person from Ali ibn Usman al-Hujwiri and
    take no id here. Likewise the "Ali" of "Alī binā-ā imān nahĩ Bāhū-ū-ū / Toṛe dafan vic Medīnā
    hū-ū" / "Without Ali there is no faith—O Bahu / For i-t is buried far in Medina hu" (p. 102,
    folio not stated).

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's place-level material on "Lahore" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore" — 35 rows)
  - **Lahore is the book's densest shrine city and the place-level material is substantial.** "In the
    major mosques in Pakistan, for instance, there is a small space where women can go and pray on
    Friday or on religious festivals such as the Eid. In the local mohallā mosques there is no
    possibility for a female to offer her ritual prayers. The domain is exclusively for male
    participation. Thus, the important spheres of religious and spiritual participation for women are
    the Sufi shrines" (p. 18, folio not stated) — the sentence that makes every shrine in this list a
    women's space in the book's argument.
  - **Hazrī at the city's shrines, before and after partition.** Lahori "also mentions women musicians
    who performed hazrī at the Sufi shrines in Lahore, especially at the time of the urs", and uses
    "the very term ''hazrī'' in connection with qawwāls who sang at the Sufi shrines in Lahore before
    the partition of [text-layer?: year dropped] and after it" (p. 90, folio not stated). A cited work
    carries the practice in its title: "Lahori, Tahir. ''Aulīyā ke Urs.'' In Sohna Shehr Lahore.
    Lahore: Sang-e Meel Publications, [year dropped]" (p. 227, folio not stated).
  - **The city's saints as a genre of local literature.** "Malik, M. Latif. Aulīyā-e Lahore. Lahore:
    Sang-e Meel Publications (Institute of Folk Heritage Library, Pakistan), [year dropped]" (pp. 211,
    228, folios not stated); "Qureshi, Samina. Lahore: The City Within. Singapore: Concept Media,
    [year dropped]" (p. 231, folio not stated); "Kanhyalal, Hindi. Tarikh-e Lahore. [year dropped].
    Reprint, Lahore: Majlis-e Tariqi-e Adab, [year dropped]" (pp. 193, 211, 226, folios not stated).
    **Every year in all three is destroyed.**
  - The author's own family is placed in the city: "the old walled city of Lahore from where I trace
    the ancestry of my maternal family" (p. 24, folio not stated); her ancestors "were appointed as
    Muftis (religious scholars) of the city of Lahore in the reign of the Muslim slave king Shamsuddin
    Iltutmish" (p. 25, folio not stated); "the Muftis and Gardezis who belonged to Lahore and Multan"
    (p. 26, folio not stated); "Mufti Ghulam Sarwar, one of my ancestors, is mentioned together with my
    paternal grandfather, Mufti Muzaffar Din, in Kanhyalal's history of Lahore. Mufti Ghulam Sarwar is
    the author of a history of the Panjab, Tarikh-e Makhazan Panjab" (p. 193, folio not stated).
  - Lahore is the city the author leaves from and returns to in her fieldwork: "Kasur is seventy miles
    from Lahore" (p. 86, folio not stated); she "started from Lahore on a beautiful spring afternoon"
    (p. 105, folio not stated); "Khalida and Jaleel Naqvi participated in the fieldwork in Lahore and
    Kasur" (p. 30, folio not stated); and two of them are shrine custodians elsewhere: "Naqvi, Jaleel,
    and Khalida Naqvi. Custodians, Hazrat Pir Sabir Sharif shrine, Paran Kalyar. Personal interviews
    and fieldwork partners. Lahore, –" — **the year range destroyed** (p. 217, folio not stated).
  - A grassroots musician is interviewed there: "Zarina, Khatoon. Grassroots musician. Personal
    interview. Lahore" (p. 218, folio not stated). Landlords' sons now "live in large urban centers
    like Karachi, Lahore, or Islamabad" (p. 81, folio not stated). Jhang Siyal is placed "to the
    southwest of Lahore in Pakistan" (pp. 121–122, folios not stated). Shalimar Gardens are "Gardens
    built by Shah Jehan, the Mughal emperor" (p. 188, folio not stated) — **no building date: (not
    stated)** — and the Melā Chirāg is held there annually (pp. 95, 185, folios not stated).
  - **`mazar-e-iqbal` reaches this list through Lahore, not through the book's four mentions of
    "Allama Iqbal Open University"** (pp. 204, 210, 217, 218, folios not stated), which name an
    institution and not the person; the book never names Iqbal as a man, an author or a literary
    reference anywhere in the eleven chunks.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: the Bibi Pak Daman's arrival, "according to tradition", when the land was known as "Hind"; book's historical prose reporting tradition — "Supposedly", "It is reported that", "are said to have"): the Bibi Pak Daman "involved the men in disseminating Islam among the lay non-Muslim populations in the Panjab. The women spoke to the male scholars and to their audiences from behind the veil"; "seven hundred male scholars came with the Bibi Pak Daman to Hind, as the land was known then" (p. 161, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated, given in the context of Shah Inayat's descent in Bulle Shah's time; author's argument, and glossary definition): "The arāĩ in the Panjab were gardeners or small cultivators who worked on their own land" (p. 108, folio not stated); the Glossary defines instead "arāĩ A caste of landowners in the Panjab" (p. 178, folio not stated). The two definitions are kept side by side.
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated, present tense, in the discussion of Waris Shah's Hir; author's argument resting on endnoted sources, glossary definition): "The Jats of the Panjab are zamindars (landowners), known for practicing widow-marriage"; the Siyal tribe "is one of the few tribes in the Panjab that allow women to inherit property under customary law" (p. 121, folio not stated); the Glossary adds "Siyal Clan name of Hir's family; powerful landowners of the Panjab" (p. 188, folio not stated), "Kher ās" [text-layer?: Kheṛās] "A powerful clan of landowners in the Panjab mentioned in the Hir-Ranjha myth" (pp. 183–184, folios not stated), and, under satti, "Some sections of Indian society, such as the Siyal Jats of the Panjab, permitted a widow to remarry, and she was not forced into satti" (p. 189, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Waris Shah's time, "the eighteenth century" as the book dates his Hir at p. 120, and "now"; author's argument): Waris Shah "is said to have recited it with great emotion among his speech communities in the Panjab"; "there is now substantial recorded material of Waris Shah's Hir available", and "Many renowned folk artists in the Panjab, such as Reshma, Zahida Parvin, Nazir Begum, Sabiha Khanum, Tufail Niazi, and Iqbal Bahu, have sung it" (p. 123, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Panjabi wedding", "Panjabi ritual"; refers to: "traditionally", period not stated; author's argument, endnote): Hir's lament as she is carried off in the palanquin "is traditionally sung at almost every Panjabi wedding at the time of the bride's departure to her husband's home" (p. 124, folio not stated); an endnote adds that "The ḍolī (palanquin) has a strong emotional context in Panjabi ritual, as a complex of emotions are associated with it" (p. 206, folio not stated). "Panjabi" here is a cultural adjective, not a place name.
- (book's word: "the Siraiki-speaking area of the Panjab", "the Panjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument, glossary definition): the Sohni myth "is sung in Sufi poetry in all the regions of the Sind, the Siraiki-speaking area of the Panjab" (pp. 126–127, folios not stated); the Glossary calls Mahival "The hero in the Sohni-Mahival myth of the Panjab" (p. 184, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; glossary definition): "dhammāl The vigorous ritual dance performed at Sufi shrines in Panjab and Sind" (p. 180, folio not stated).

## From Regula Burckhardt Qureshi, Sufi Music of India and Pakistan

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/qureshi_sufi_music_qawwali.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

**An author-only row throughout: six occurrences across two chunks, every one of them a citation or
an index headword.** The id is taken under the ruling of 18 September 2026 (a person who is an
archive row's principal figure takes that row's id even where the book names them only as an
author). **The book never calls him a saint, never names Lahore in connection with him, and never
describes a shrine, a tomb or a dargah for him: all (not stated).** This is evidence that Qureshi's
doctrine of samaʿ is built on him; it is **not** material about the shrine, and nothing here should
be written into the `data-darbar` entry as a fact about the site.

- **Saint**
  - **Names used, as printed:** "Hujwiri" (pp. 102, 103, 126; folios 82, 83, 106), "Al Hujwiri"
    (p. 103, folio 83), `HUJW R I I , ' Ali ibn Uthman al-` `[text-layer?: HUJWIRI, ʿAli ibn Uthman
    al-]` in the bibliography (p. 270, folio 250), and "Hujwiri, Ali ul-" in the index (p. 284,
    folio 264).
  - **The only date the book attaches to the man is a century:** "starting from early Sufi treatises
    (for example, eleventh-century Ibn-e-Arabi and Al Hujwiri), singers with a special competence
    served mystical assemblies (Hujwiri 1970: 417)" (p. 103, folio 83). **The archive row's
    `figure_died` 1072 is neither confirmed nor contradicted** — "eleventh-century" is consistent
    with it but is not a date. "1970" throughout is the modern edition's year, not a date for the
    man.
  - **"Data Ganj Bakhsh" occurs exactly once in the whole book, as an index cross-reference with no
    locator of its own: "Data Ganj Bakhsh, see Hujwiri, Ali ul-" (p. 283, folio 263).** This is the
    only string in the book that connects the cited author to the archive row's popular title.
  - **Lahore, a death year and a silsila are (not stated) for him anywhere in the book.** The one
    "Lahore" near him is a publisher's city, not his.
- **Significance (what he is used for)**
  - **He supplies the book's working definition of samaʿ:** samaʿ "comprises first and foremost that
    which is heard, the 'divine message which stirs the heart to seek God' (Hujwiri 1970: 404)"
    (p. 102, folio 82).
  - **He is the authority for subordinating samaʿ to spiritual hierarchy** — the book's central
    institutional claim: "Sufism achieves this primarily by placing the entire practice of sama'
    firmly within the hierarchical structure of spiritual authority (see, for example, Hujwiri 1970:
    Ch. 25)" (p. 103, folio 83).
  - **He is named among the authorities whose written principles underlie present-day Qawwali:** "The
    principles laid down between the eleventh and fourteenth centuries (discussed by Sijzi 1884,
    Hujwiri 1970, Ghazali 1979, among others; see also Rahman 1 971 `[text-layer?: Rahman 1971]`) not
    only serve as a charter for this concept, but they contribute to an amazing extent to the
    concepts in use today" (p. 126, folio 106).
  - **Full bibliography entry, as printed:** "HUJW R I I , ' Ali ibn Uthman al- 1 970 `[text-layer?:
    1970]` *The 'Kashf al-Mahjub', the Oldest Persian Treatise on Sufism by al-Hujwin* `[text-layer?:
    al-Hujwiri]`, trans. R. A. Nicholson, Gibb Memorial Series no. 17 (London: Luzac; first published
    1911)" (p. 270, folio 250). **The *Kashf al-Mahjub* is named only here, in the bibliography, and
    nowhere in the body text.**
  - Index headword: "Hujwiri, Ali ul-, 83" (p. 284, folio 264). **The locator 83 is a printed folio,
    not a PDF page.**

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`data-darbar`** rests on **six author-only citations and index headwords** and nothing else:
  `Hujwiri 1970: 404` (p. 102, folio 82); `Hujwiri 1970: Ch. 25`, `Al Hujwiri`, `Hujwiri 1970: 417`
  (p. 103, folio 83); the parenthetical list at p. 126 (folio 106); the bibliography entry at p. 270
  (folio 250); and the index at pp. 283–284 (folios 263–264). **Weigh it as a bibliographic-source
  hit, not a shrine hit.** Nothing here can be written into the `data-darbar` entry as a fact about
  the shrine. What it is evidence for is that Qureshi's doctrine of samaʿ is built on the *Kashf
  al-Mahjub* — and **the book names that work only in the bibliography, never in the body text**.
  "Eleventh-century" (p. 103, folio 83) is consistent with the row's `figure_died` 1072 but is not a
  date.

- **"Hazrat Ali" is NOT the principal figure of `data-darbar`**, whose figure is Ali ibn Usman
  al-Hujwiri. The book's own glossary settles it: "Ali son-in-law of Prophet Muhammad" (p. 262,
  folio 242). The archive's only `Ali` principal figure, `shrine-of-imam-ali-ul-haq` (Sialkot), is a
  third person.

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's place-level material on "Lahore" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore") — **the thinnest attachment in this file, and it is honest to say so: the book's Lahore is a bibliography line and four names in a discography.**
  - **Four Lahore Qawwals are named**, in "Other Recordings of Qawwal Parties": "Kausar Jabin
    (Lahore)", "Mubarak Ali-Fateh Ah `[text-layer?: Mubarak Ali-Fateh Ali]` (Lahore)", "Muhammad
    Siddiq (Lahore)", "Alaf Din Pathan (Lahore)" (p. 282, folio 262). **No shrine, no assembly, no
    date is attached to any of them: all (not stated).**
  - **Lahore as a publishing centre for this literature:** BURCKHARDT 1968 "Lahore: Sheikh Muhammad
    Ashraf"; NADVI "Lahore: Institute of Islamic Culture"; PHULVARVI "Lahore:
    Idara-e-Saqafat-e-Islami"; SHARIB "Lahore: Sheikh Muhammad Ashraf" (pp. 269, 272, 273, 275;
    folios 249, 252, 253, 255).
  - **Nothing else.** No Lahore shrine, saint, ʿurs, custodian, endowment, building or visitor is
    named anywhere in the book; Lahore is absent from chunks 001–013 entirely. The one figure in this
    id list the book touches at all, `data-darbar`, it touches as an author and without naming Lahore
    (see its subsection above).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Panjab", "Panjabi"; refers to: period not stated, ethnographic present of the author's fieldwork; author's argument): "tunes from the two principal regions of the Qawwali tradition, Uttar Pradesh and Panjab, can be distinguished mainly by their rhythmic setting"; "A predominantly quantitative emphasis characterizes tunes from Uttar Pradesh, while Panjabi tunes tend to be rhythmically organized according to 'quality' or stress"; the drum articulation of the metre, "which in Panjab is more strongly accentual than in Uttar Pradesh, corresponding with the respective drumming traditions of each region (as elaborated in Stewart 1974)" (p. 73, folio 53). The music example that follows is captioned "Panjab (Kisi ko kuchh)" against "Uttar Pradesh (Kachhjagmag)" (p. 75, folio 55), and the same song is said to exemplify "the 'Panjabi style'" (p. 58, folio 38). The book does not say whether its "Panjab" is the Pakistani or the Indian side, or both; it sets it against an Indian region (Uttar Pradesh), and the "Panjab" example was sung by "a Panjabi Qawwal, Rahmat Khan" (p. 57, folio 37) and recorded at "Qawwali Hall, 'Urs Nizamuddin" / "Auliya, 18 Apr. 1976" (p. 13, front matter, folio xi by the front-matter rule). Borderline: kept because it is a statement about the region's musical tradition in general, not a language remark.

*Sweep notes:* Hits in the `.txt` chunks for the brief's pattern (case-insensitive, widened to catch diacritic loss with `P[aue]n.?j.?[a-z]?b`): "Panjab" 3 (p. 73 ×2; p. 75 caption); "Panjabi" 6 (p. 13 CD listing "(Panjabi version) Panjabi Qawwal and Party" ×2; p. 57 "a Panjabi Qawwal, Rahmat Khan"; p. 58 "'Panjabi style'"; p. 73 "Panjabi tunes"; p. 256 glossary "ang     regional or genre style (for example, Panjabi ang)", folio 236 by rule); "Punjabi" 1 (bibliography title "A Punjabi Village in Pakistan", Eglar, date printed "i960" [text-layer?] [1960], p. 269); "Sind" 3, all bibliography titles (p. 267 BALOCH, "Musical Instruments of the Lower Indus Valley ofSind" [text-layer?] [of Sind]; p. 274 "'Music and Culture in Sind: An Ethnomusicological Perspective'" and "Sind through the Centuries"); "Pathan" 1 (performer name "Alaf Din Pathan (Lahore)", discography, p. 282); "Punjab"/"Sindh"/"Baluchistan"/"Balochistan"/"Frontier"/"Pakhtunkhwa"/"NWFP"/"Kashmir"/"Multan"/"suba"/"Sindhi"/"Baluchi"/"Kashmiri": 0. The case-insensitive "Sind" pattern also matched "simultaneous(ly)" 11 times (chunks 006, 008, 012, 013): false positives. Excluded: the p. 13 CD listing, the p. 57 performer description and the p. 256 glossary entry (person or terminology, folded into the bullet as context only where they bear on it); all bibliography and discography lines (titles and a name, no substantive text). No toponym-only province hits. Flags for a human: the single bullet's "Panjab" is unbounded in the book (no India/Pakistan side stated) and its illustrative recording was made at the ʿurs of Nizamuddin Auliya, which the takeaways place in Delhi — a reviewer may prefer to move it to `not attached`.

## From Robert Rozehnal, Islamic Sufism Unbound

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rozehnal_islamic_sufism_unbound.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's second shrine, present in eight chunks, and the only shrine outside its own silsila that
the order permits its disciples to visit. The author's forms of the name, all as printed:
"'Ali Hujwiri (d. 1072)" (p. 72, folio 62), "al-Hujwiri" (p. 111, folio 102),
"Sayyid 'Ali al-Hujwiri" (p. 123, folio 114), "Sayyid 'Ali ibn 'Uthman al-Hujwiri (d. 1074)"
(p. 171, folio 163; p. 206, folio 198; p. 263, folio not stated), "Hujwiri, 'Ali (d. 1074)" in
the index (p. 276, folio 270), and in the disciples'
address "Hazrat Data Sahab", "Data Sahab", "Data Ganj Bakhsh" (p. 207, folio 199).

- **Saint**
  - **Death year — the book prints 1072 once and 1074 four times, and the archive row records 1072.**
    "'Ali Hujwiri (d. 1072)" (p. 72, folio 62); "(d. 1074)" at p. 171 folio 163, p. 206 folio 198,
    p. 263 folio not stated, and in the index at p. 276 folio 270. **Digits survive in this text
    layer and "1074" sits inside parentheses, not glued to a word, so it is not an endnote number:
    the discrepancy is the book's own.** All are kept; see Doubts.
  - **The moniker is glossed by the book.** "As the saint's moniker, Data Ganj Bakhsh ('The Lavish
    Giver'), suggests al-Hujwiri's tomb is renowned as a place of intense power—an open and
    accessible outlet for spiritual grace." (p. 207, folio 199)
  - **Silsila — never stated in the book's own voice: (not stated).** What the book gives instead is
    an honorary affiliation: "al-Hujwiri is remembered as an honorary Chishti Sabiri, and his shrine
    is a focus of local ritual practice." (p. 207, folio 199) Wahid Bakhsh's letter puts the same
    thing carefully: "Although Hazrat Data Sahab, may God have mercy upon him, is not in the line of
    our silsila directly, he is great enough to know what you need. Moreover, he is very much
    associated with the masha'ikh of our silsila. He is very kind to us." (p. 207, folio 199)
  - **He is one of five figures contemplated daily** in muraqaba-i shaykh, with Mu'in ad-Din Chishti
    Ajmeri, Baba Farid ad-Din, Muhammad Zauqi Shah and Shah Sayyid Waris Hasan, "for fifteen to
    thirty minutes on a daily basis" (p. 206, folio 198).
  - **As author — the book's dominant register for him.** *Kashf al-mahjub* is called "the famous
    Persian treatise on Sufism written by the eleventh-century master 'Ali Hujwiri (d. 1072)" (p. 72,
    folio 62), "al-Hujwiri's famous tome titled Kashf al-mahjub [The Unveiling of the Concealed]"
    (p. 111, folio 102), and "the Persian classic by al-Hujwiri" (p. 173, folio 165). He heads a list
    of five premodern authors whose "writings … are especially popular" among today's disciples, with
    al-Ghazali (d. 1111), Ibn al-'Arabi (d. 1240), al-Suhrawardi (d. 1234) and Rumi (d. 1273)
    (p. 171, folio 163), and appears again among "Imam Ghazali, Ibn Arabi, Junaid Baghdadi, Jalal
    ad-Din Rumi, and Sayyid 'Ali al-Hujwiri" (p. 123, folio 114). **The book's title glosses for the
    same work differ: "[The Unveiling of the Secrets]" (p. 72, folio 62) against "[The Unveiling of
    the Concealed]" (p. 111, folio 102) — both are the book's own brackets.**
  - **His book's place in the order's prescribed reading order is third of four:** after Wahid
    Bakhsh's *Islamic Sufism* ("the primer and we all read that") and *A Guide for Spiritual
    Aspirants*, and before *Immense Power Potential of Pakistan* — "Then you go still deeper with
    Kashf al-mahjub [a translation of the Persian classic by al-Hujwiri]. That delves into all the
    deeper levels of Sufism" (p. 173, folio 165, senior Malaysian disciple, October 1, 2001, Kuala
    Lumpur), the whole run being "like the journey itself: from fana' … to baqa'" (p. 173,
    folio 165).
  - **Three editions of his book in the Chishti Sabiri economy, all Wahid Bakhsh's.** Ch 2 n. 74:
    "Wahid Bakhsh Sial Rabbani translated Kashf al-mahjub from its original Persian into Urdu. See
    Sayyid 'Ali ibn 'Uthman al-Hujwiri, Kashf al-mahjub, Urdu translation from Persian with
    commentary by Wahid Bakhsh Sial Rabbani (Lahore: al-Faisal Publishers, 1995). The text… has also
    recently been translated into English and published by disciples in Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia. See
    Sayyid 'Ali ibn 'Uthman al-Hujwiri, The Kashful Mahjub ('Unveiling the Veiled'): The Earliest
    Persian Treatise on Sufism, English translation with commentary by Wahid Bakhsh Sial Rabbani
    (Kuala Lumpur: A.S. Noordeen, 1997)" (p. 246, folio 239) — **an Urdu and an English version by
    the same shaykh, two years and two cities apart.** A third entry, filed under its title with **no
    author named**, is Reynold A. Nicholson's translation: "The Kashf al-Mahjub: The Oldest Persian
    Treatise on Sufism. Translated by Reynold A. Nicholson. Leiden: E. J. Brill, 1911." (p. 264,
    folio 258). It was Nicholson's translation, not the Persian, that is credited with sparking
    Shahidullah Faridi's interest in Sufism (p. 72, folio 62) — **the edition and year of the copy he
    read are (not stated)**.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Place, given once, in one clause with the death year.** "the shrine of Sayyid 'Ali ibn 'Uthman
    al-Hujwiri (d. 1074) in the old city of Lahore" (p. 206, folio 198). Elsewhere it is "the tomb
    complex of his patron saint 'Ali Hujwiri" (p. 75, folio 65), "Hujwiri's shrine" (p. 75, folio 65)
    and "al-Hujwiri's tomb" (p. 207, folio 199). **The book never uses the name "Data Darbar"**, and
    the epithet "Data Ganj Bakhsh" appears only as the saint's moniker (p. 207, folio 199), never as
    the shrine's name: (not stated). **No architecture, no dome, no building date, no custodian, no
    Auqaf presence and no visitor figure anywhere: all (not stated).**
  - **A burial inside the tomb complex, and its economy.** Shahidullah Faridi's elder brother Faruq
    Ahmad "died in Lahore on February 13, 1945 after a prolonged bout of pneumonia. The details of
    his tragic death and burial within the tomb complex of his patron saint 'Ali Hujwiri are outlined
    in Tarbiyat al-'ushshaq" (p. 75, folio 65). **Burial in the compound had a price the mourners
    could not pay:** "According to the text, there were insufficient funds to fulfill Faruq Ahmad's
    desire to be buried in the compound of Hujwiri's shrine, and an alternative location was hastily
    arranged. As the burial was about to commence, however, a stranger suddenly arrived and announced
    that he would" (p. 75, folio 65) — the quotation ends there. **The sentence is cut by a chunk
    boundary and what the stranger did is not in the notes: (not stated).** The outcome is
    nonetheless stated flatly eight
    chapters later: the brothers "converted to Islam after reading al-Hujwiri's classic text Kashf
    al-mahjub" and "to this day, Faruq Ahmed lies buried in a small, subterranean room within his
    patron saint's tomb complex" (p. 207, folio 199) — **note the spelling "Faruq Ahmed" here against
    "Faruq Ahmad" at p. 75.** A related detail: workers later moving his grave "discovered that his
    body was still fresh and had not decomposed—one of the well-known tropes of sainthood", and
    "Faruq Ahmad's tomb was indeed left undisturbed" (p. 76, folio 66). The funeral itself is located
    at Lahore: "Throughout the time of the funeral, a light rain fell from that cloud of mercy only
    above that spot in Lahore" (p. 75, folio 65).
- **Practices and events**
  - **The order's single named exception to its own rule.** "Do not go to any mazar which is not in
    our silsila" (p. 207, folio 199), and: "A notable exception is made, however, for the shrine of
    Sayyid 'Ali ibn 'Uthman al-Hujwiri (d. 1074) in the old city of Lahore" (p. 206, folio 198).
  - **A weekly pilgrimage pattern, with days.** "Disciples who live in Lahore regularly visit
    al-Hujwiri's shrine on **Thursday evenings** before attending the communal halqa-i zikr, and they
    often repeat the journey on **Sundays** as well." (p. 207, folio 199) **Who founded it:** "it was
    Shaykh Wahid Bakhsh who established this weekly pilgrimage, instigating a new ritual practice
    that Chishti Sabiris in Lahore continue today" (p. 208, folio 200).
  - **The order does NOT attend the ʿurs.** A senior Pakistani disciple: "Murids do not go to the
    mazar [of al-Hujwiri] collectively. They go privately or as a family, but not as a group. We do
    not even go for the 'urs [death anniver-sary]. We only go for one-day attendance." (p. 208,
    folio 200) **No ʿurs date, month or duration for this shrine anywhere in the book: (not
    stated).**
  - **A ritual differentiation recorded as the disciples' own.** "When we go to shaykhs other than
    our silsila, we recite Fatiha twice… But if you go to Data Sahab, you recite it only once because
    you are in his silsila. This is done out of respect." (p. 208, folio 200)
  - **What a disciple does at the tomb**, a female disciple's routine: "You should say, in your
    heart, 'I am sitting in front of Data Sahab. Data Sahab is sitting there, and I am sitting here.'
    Then you just close your eyes, and take long, slow, deep breaths… When I go to Data Sahab I sit
    there for ten to fifteen minutes of meditation and receive an answer to all my problems."
    (p. 208, folio 200, October 31, 2000, Lahore)
- **Legends and miracles (karamat)**
  - **The grant of South Asia at this tomb.** "According to tradition Mu'in ad-Din Chishti Ajmeri
    (d. 1236), the first Indian Chishti master, was granted the dominion (wilayat) of all of South
    Asia, for himself and his successors, at al-Hujwiri's tomb." (p. 207, folio 199)
  - **Data Sahab appears and speaks.** "When you visualize Data Sahab, you see Data Sahab. Many times
    he will appear to you and give you messages." (p. 208, folio 200) Murids "compare the spiritual
    energy (faizan) that radiates from the saint's tomb during muraqaba to an intense electric
    current surging through the body" (p. 208, folio 200); contemplation there is "an intimate
    conversation between the Sufi disciple and the patron saint, alive in his tomb" (p. 207,
    folio 199).
- **Significance**
  - **The other Lahore shrines, as the order sees them — and none is named.** "There are many mazars
    of unknown shaykhs in Lahore. Some of them are qalandars [a roaming sect of antinomian Sufis] and
    can only give their visitors what they have—that is, they make them qalandars as well. In our
    silsila we avoid this." (p. 207, folio 199) **No such mazar is named, so none is mapped from this
    sentence.**
  - **Index locators**, printed folios 62, 65–6, 102, 114, 163, 165, 198, 199–201 (p. 276,
    folio 270). **No `see also` is printed for him**, and he is not cross-referenced from `shrines`,
    `mazars` or `ziyarat` (pp. 280–281, folios 274–275).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

2. **al-Hujwiri's death year — the book gives two, and one matches the archive.** "(d. 1072)" once
   (p. 72, folio 62); "(d. 1074)" four times (p. 171 folio 163; p. 206 folio 198; p. 263 folio not
   stated; p. 276 folio 270). The archive row records **1072**. "1074" sits inside parentheses, not
   glued to a word, so it is not an endnote number. **A human must decide which the entry carries.**

- **`data-darbar` is attached eight times, but only once by its shrine.** Seven of the eight are
  author-only or name-in-a-list mentions (pp. 72, 111, 123, 171, 173, 246, 263, 276); the substantive
  shrine material is confined to pp. 206–208, folios 198–200. **The book never writes "Data Darbar".**

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's place-level material on "Lahore" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore", 35 rows — the heaviest attachment in this book, as the ruling intends):
  - **Lahore is the book's base.** "I spent the academic year of 1995–1996 as a graduate student
    studying Urdu in Lahore, Pakistan" (p. 21, folio 10); "In Pakistan, I was based in the cultural
    capital of Lahore" (p. 26, folio 15) — **"the cultural capital" is the book's own epithet for the
    city.** It is also the city where the photograph of the dead shaykh was circulated among
    "several friends in Lahore" (p. 21, folio 10).
  - **The city as the order's densest living node.** "frequent attendance at the zikr circles in
    Lahore" is part of the author's fieldwork (p. 141, folio 133). Two weekly halqa-i zikr run there,
    both in private homes and one of them women-led: "a senior male murid hosts a weekly halqa-i zikr
    in his home," and "A female disciple of Shaykh Wahid Bakhsh Rabbani does the same, leading a
    separate session comprised mostly of women" (p. 201, folio 193). The full eleven-item zikr
    repertoire the book prints was recorded "at a gathering on a Thursday evening in October 2000"
    in Lahore (p. 202, folio 194). **Hospitality is banned at these gatherings:** "That is why
    serving any food or even a cup of tea is strictly forbidden, because some people then might come
    just to socialize" (August 26, 2001, Lahore) (p. 203, folio 195) — **there is no langar.**
  - **Lahore as the order's print capital.** The Sufi Foundation, "founded by another senior male
    murid, published a series of translations and original writings by Wahid Bakhsh during the
    1980s"; Bazm-i Ittehad al-Muslimin was "founded in 1984 by the shaykh's nephew"; "Beginning in
    1996, Talifat-i Shaheedi, established by another murid of Shahidullah Faridi, has published four
    additional texts, three of them in English" (p. 130, folio 121). Commercial houses used: "Ziya
    al-Qur'an… currently publishes two of Wahid Bakhsh's Urdu translations of classical Persian
    malfuzat; Qausin Publishers prints two key Urdu malfuzat; and, most importantly, al-Faisal
    Publishers distributes a number of Wahid Bakhsh's works in both English and Urdu" (p. 130,
    folio 121). Also named: Sh. Muhammad Ashraf (p. 270, folio 264), Vanguard Books
    (p. 241, folio 234) and `al-Kitab` (p. 263, folio not stated) — and the silsila's books reach the
    public through "the Urdu bookstores of Karachi and Lahore" (p. 111, folio 102). Across all
    houses, "At present, some fifty different texts are produced and distributed from a number of
    publishing houses" (p. 130, folio 121).
  - **A women's madrasa.** A murid "now runs an informal religious school (madrasa) for women in
    Lahore" (p. 44, folio 34).
  - **Lahore as a seat of state and press.** "the governor of Punjab in Lahore" (p. 39, folio 29);
    "the Lahore-based English language weekly, The Friday Times" (p. 37, folio 27); the *Journal of
    the Panjab Historical Society (Lahore)* (p. 242, folio 235; p. 270, folio 264).
  - **Lahore as a distance benchmark for another shrine:** Pakpattan is "120 miles southwest of
    Lahore" (p. 35, folio 25).
  - **A death and a funeral in the city.** Faruq Ahmad "died in Lahore on February 13, 1945 after a
    prolonged bout of pneumonia" (p. 75, folio 65); "Throughout the time of the funeral, a light rain
    fell from that cloud of mercy only above that spot in Lahore" (p. 75, folio 65).
  - **The city's shrines as a class, in the order's own words.** "There are many mazars of unknown
    shaykhs in Lahore. Some of them are qalandars [a roaming sect of antinomian Sufis] and can only
    give their visitors what they have—that is, they make them qalandars as well. In our silsila we
    avoid this." (p. 207, folio 199) **This is a statement about Lahore's shrines in general and
    names none of them; it is the clearest case in the book for the place-level reading.**
  - **Interview datelines, recorded because the ruling attaches them and because they date the
    ethnography**: March 9, 2001; February 21, 2001; October 1, 2001; April 24, 2001 (pp. 44, 46, 47,
    folios 34, 36, 37); October 3, 2000; November 5, 2000; June 17, 2001; August 26, 2001 (pp. 91,
    94–96, 131, folios 81, 84–86, 122); October 22, 2000; March 16, 2001; February 26, 2001 (pp. 167,
    169, 173, folios 159, 161, 165); October 30 and November 7, 2000; April 25, 2001; October 7, 2000
    (pp. 188, 191, 193, 225, folios 180, 183, 185, 217); October 31, 2000 (p. 208, folio 200);
    October 3, 2002 (p. 132, folio 123).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: British colonial rule — the book's frame is "the British Raj", no years given in the sentence; quoted source: Katherine Ewing, *Arguing Sainthood*, 49, per n. 10): "British policy, especially in the Punjab and Sind, was to treat them in essentially the same way that landlords and tribal leaders were treated . . . They tried to maintain the traditional social structure intact, securing the loyalty of the pirs, landlords, and chiefs by reinforcing their economic positions and educating them in the British tradition." — "them" being "The hereditary saints [sajjada nishin]", who "were seen primarily in terms of their economic power" (p. 33, folio 23). Note 10 points for "the British policy toward local Sufi institutions and exemplars in the Punjab" to Ansari's *Sufi Saints and State Power: The Pirs of Sind, 1843–1947* and Gilmartin's *Empire and Islam: Punjab and the Making of Pakistan* (p. 241, folio 234; bibliographic only).
- (book's word: "the southern Punjab"; refers to: from Wahid Bakhsh's khilafat being "publicly confirmed" "In 1975" and Shahidullah Faridi's "last will and testament", no year printed for the will; author's argument): Shahidullah's will says Wahid Bakhsh "has initiated halqa zikr and guidance in Bahawalpur" and "should spread the silsila in this place only"; the author reads this: "Here Shahidullah effectively delineates the southern Punjab as Wahid Bakhsh’s personal spiritual domain (wilayat)", adding that "In the following years, however, the shaykh’s duties Chishti Sabiri master rapidly expanded in scope and scale" (sentence printed so, a word apparently missing; not repaired) (p. 90, folio 80). The will names only Bahawalpur; the extension to "the southern Punjab" is the author's.

## From Annemarie Schimmel, As Through a Veil

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_as_through_a_veil.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- **Kind of evidence — read this first.** The book never describes Data Darbar, an urs, a custodianship or
  any practice at the site. Hujwiri appears almost entirely as **the author of the *Kashf al-maḥjūb***,
  quoted or cited (usually in the compound form `Hujwiri/Nicholson`), plus one biographical half-sentence
  in the running text and one index headword. The id is taken under the 18 September ruling.

**Saint**
- He is the author of the `Kash/ al-mafJjub` [text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb], which the book calls "the
  first major treatise on Sufi theories in Persian" (p. 61, folio 51).
- The one thing the running text says of the man: he is the one "who spent his last years in Lahore"
  (p. 61, folio 51).
- Name forms as printed: "c Ali ibn c Othman al-Hujwiri" [text-layer?: ʿAlī ibn ʿUthmān al-Hujwīrī] in the
  notes (p. 236, folio 218); "Hujwiri, c Ali ibn c Uthman al-Jullabi al-" [text-layer?: ʿAlī ibn ʿUthmān
  al-Jullābī al-Hujwīrī] in the bibliography (p. 324, folio 306).
- He is placed in the book's chronology of Persian mystical literature, which "developed first in the
  Eastern Lands of the Caliphate, namely in Herat and northwest India, in the second half of the eleventh
  century" (p. 61, folio 51), and paired with a contemporary: "at approximately the same time another
  mystic, `c Abdullah-i Ansari` [text-layer?: ʿAbdullāh-i Anṣārī]" translated "Sulami's `Tabaqat a�-` /
  `�afiyya`" [text-layer?: Ṭabaqāt aṣ-Ṣūfiyya] and wrote "the first devotional booklet in Persian, the
  `Munii-` / `jiit`" [text-layer?: Munājāt] (p. 61, folio 51).
- **Epithet and death date — the two places differ, kept side by side.** In the running text at p. 61
  (folio 51) the book gives no death date, no order and no epithet: Pass 1 records explicitly that "Data
  Ganj Bakhsh" does not appear there (p. 61, folio 51). The Index of Proper Names, by contrast, heads him
  `Hujwiri, c Ali ibn <Othman Data Ganj Bakhsh (d. c. 1071) (Kash[ al-mabjiib)` [text-layer?: `Hujwīrī,
  ʿAlī ibn ʿOthmān Data Ganj Bakhsh`, work `Kashf al-maḥjūb`] (p. 355, folio 337) — so the epithet that
  gives the shrine its name, and a death date, occur once each, in the index only. The date is printed
  with the book's own hedge, `(d. c. 1071)` (p. 355, folio 337). The index attaches exactly one work to
  him, in the parenthesis after the name, and gives him no sub-entries (p. 355, folio 337).

**Shrine / Darbar**
- **(not stated) throughout.** No tomb, no shrine, no order and no silsila are given at p. 61 (folio 51);
  the bibliography entry gives "no place, tomb, date or silsila for him — only the authorship" (p. 324,
  folio 306; p. 325, folio 307); and Lahore is not named at all in the chunk containing the note on union
  (p. 276, folio 258).

**Significance — cited as author of the *Kashf al-maḥjūb***
- Transmitter of a saying of Sumnun al-Muhibb: "Sumnun al-Muhibb, quoted by c Ali ibn c Othman al-Hujwiri,
  Kashf al-mahjub, p. 137" (note 25 to chapter 1, printed on p. 236, folio 218).
- Cited for the doctrine of *tauhid*: "Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf al-mahjub, p. 2cn" [text-layer?: page
  number not recoverable]; "see also Gramlich, 'Mystical Dimensions of tauhid'" (note 81 to chapter 1,
  printed on p. 240, folio 222).
- The reference for early Persian Sufi prose in the opening run of the Persian chapter's notes:
  "Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf al-mahjub" (p. 245, folio 227).
- For two contrasted contemplative positions: the *Kashf* "discusses the two approaches to the world: some
  mystics state, 'I never saw anything without seeing God therein,' which points to an advanced stage of
  contemplation because he sees the agent only, while the statement of others, 'I never see anything
  except God,' shows that he, fully enraptured, sees only the agent" (p. 248, folio 230).
- For a prohibition: "Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf al-ma'1jiib" [text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb] "says: 'Looking
  at youth and associating with them are forbidden practices and anyone who declares this to be allowable
  is an unbeliever!'" (p. 250, folio 232).
- For the law of love: "As Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf al-mahjlib" [text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb] "says: 'It
  is a law that when the lover advances, the beloved retires. If the lover is satisfied with love alone,
  then the beloved draws near. In truth, the lover has honor only while he has no desire of union.'" —
  which "forms the topic of most Persian works on love" (p. 251, folio 233).
- Cited bare, without comment, for the imagery of wine or the tavern: "Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf
  al-ma}/jiib" [text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb] (p. 254, folio 236).
- Cited once in the note on union: `See Hujwiri/Nicholson, Kashf a/-mal1jiib [text-layer?: Kashf
  al-maḥjūb], p. 398` (p. 276, folio 258).

**Bibliography entry (no Lahore imprint — the edition cited is European)**
- One entry, its head on the last line of p. 324 (folio 306) and the rest on p. 325 (folio 307): "Kashf al
  ma/Jjub [text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb], trans. by / Reynold Alleyne Nicholson. GMS XVII . London:
  Luzac-Leiden: Brill, 1911; 3d ed . 1959, and often." (p. 324, folio 306; p. 325, folio 307)
- **Doubt:** in the notes the evidence is a bare bibliographic citation, with no saint, shrine, city or
  date attached (p. 236, folio 218; p. 240, folio 222) — flagged by Pass 1 as thin. The only Lahore
  sentence in the whole book's Hujwiri material is "who spent his last years in Lahore" (p. 61, folio 51),
  and the only appearance of "Data Ganj Bakhsh", the name the archive row carries, is the index headword
  (p. 355, folio 337). A human should decide whether an entry may rest on those two lines.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

**Seven death years in the book disagree with the archive rows.** All seven are printed headwords in
the `Index of Proper Names`, i.e. a 1982 Columbia monograph disagreeing with the sheet, not OCR
noise. The archive's `figure_died` is given only for comparison.

- `Hujwiri … (d. c. 1071)` (p. 355, folio 337) against 1072 — the book hedges with its own `c.`

- **Thin attachments — author-only, imprint-only, title-only.** `data-darbar`: Hujwiri is only an
  author, "Hujwiri/Nicholson" five times (pp. 245–254, folios 227–236), plus the single clause "who
  spent his last years in Lahore" (p. 61, folio 51); the epithet "Data Ganj Bakhsh" appears only in
  the index headword (p. 355, folio 337) and nowhere in the body.
  `rahman-baba-mausoleum-rehman-baba-shrine` is the weakest of all: the name appears **only inside a
  book title**, "Jens Enevoldsen, Selections from Rahman Baba" (p. 278, folio 260; p. 319, folio 301),
  and the index has no Rahman Baba headword. `shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori` rests in one
  chunk on a damaged word inside a title, "Kalam-i Girhoi;i" (p. 318, folio 300), and in another on
  "the Sindhi mystic Girhori" with no first name (p. 294, folio 276) — the surname match is the
  note-writer's, not the book's, though the index does print `Girhon, c Abdur Ral;lim (d . 1778)`
  (p. 353, folio 335). `shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi` rests on "Abu Hanifa and Hanuman are
  one and the same" (p. 176, folio 158) plus a bare headword (p. 354, folio 336).
  `darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz` rests on the Mahmud–Ayaz pairing alone, "his Turkish officer Ayaz"
  (p. 84, folio 74) — no Lahore, no tomb, no office; the identification is inferential.
  `mazar-e-iqbal`, `shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri`, `garh-maharaja-shorkot`,
  `mazar-of-bulleh-shah`, `shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar` and `mithankot-kot-mithan` each rest, in
  the bibliography chunk, on one printed entry with no tomb, date, place or silsila (pp. 325–341,
  folios 307–323).

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's material headed "Lahore (place-level: 35 rows)", copied into every existing entry that heading lists (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

**Kind of evidence: OVERWHELMINGLY IMPRINT-ONLY — a place of publication or a publisher's address in
the bibliography and the notes, not the book discussing Lahore.** Chunk 014 states it flatly: "in this
chunk every single 'Lahore' is a place of publication or a publisher's address — the book nowhere
discusses Lahore itself here." Chunk 010 says the toponym "occurs twice, both times in one note, and
both times only as a place of publication," and calls the attachment "place-level and weaker than
place-level." **Chunk 009's entire Lahore attachment — all 35 rows — rests on the single imprint
"(Lahore, 1932)".** **Exactly two facts below are genuinely topical** and are the only Lahore material
about the city itself: Hujwiri's last years in Lahore (p. 61, folio 51) and Madho Lal Husain's tomb
near the Shalimar gardens (pp. 170–171, folios 152–153). A shrine-entry writer can drop everything
else in this subsection as bibliographic apparatus.

**Topical (the book on Lahore itself) — two facts only**
- Lahore is named once in twenty-five pages of chunk 003, and only as the place where a book was
  finished: "The `Kash/ al-mafJjub`[text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb] of Hujwiri, who spent his last years
  in Lahore, is the first major treatise on Sufi theories in Persian" (p. 61, folio 51).
- Madho Lal Husain "is buried together with his Hindu beloved in a modest tomb near the Shalimar
  gardens in Lahore" (pp. 170–171, folios 152–153). This is chunk 007's only Lahore occurrence. The
  Shalimar gardens are named only as the landmark for that tomb; nothing else is said about them.
- Negative finding (chunk 003): no shrine, tomb, mosque, ʿurs, custodian, endowment or building in
  Lahore is mentioned, and the city is not otherwise described (not stated) (p. 61, folio 51 context).

**Index**
- `Lahore` stands as its own headword in the Index of Proper Names (p. 357, folio 339).

**Imprint-only — notes**
- Iqbal's "epic ]avidniima (Lahore, 1932), lines 727-728", note 2 to chapter 1, printed on p. 233
  (folio not stated). This single imprint is the whole of chunk 009's Lahore evidence; the chunk says
  nothing else about the city, and nothing at all about any Lahore site, saint, shrine or practice.
- "Part translation in verse by Pir Imam Shah, Lahore 191 1"[text-layer?: 1911], "fly leaf. Another
  Panjabi verse translation by Maulana Muhammad Shahuddin, Lahore, 1939 (see L. D. Barnett, Panjabi,
  p. 39)." (p. 254, folio 236). The note is note 3 of the Rumi chapter and stands two notes after "The
  literature about Jalaluddin is almost inexhaustible"; **the work being translated is not named in the
  note itself** (not stated). So chunk 010's whole Lahore holding is: two Panjabi verse translations
  were published at Lahore, one in "191 1"[text-layer?: 1911] and one in "1939", by "Pir Imam Shah" and
  "Maulana Muhammad Shahuddin" respectively (p. 254, folio 236).
- Note 77: "A fine Panjabi version with a commentary by Haji Muhammad ad­din Naqshbandi Mujaddidi from
  Bombay was published in the `Alliihwiile ki qaumi dukkiin` [text-layer?: Allāhwāle kī qaumī dukkān],
  Lahore; it preserves the rhyme letter m, followed by the genitive particle di." (p. 301, folio 283).

**Contradiction kept side by side (not reconciled)**
- Chunks 010, 013 and 014 attach imprint lines as Lahore material under ruling (a). Chunk 015 says of
  the same kind of line: "Imprint lines are refused as place-level material — see `## Doubts`"
  (pp. 342–343, folios 324–325), while still heading its bullet with all 35 ids. The two positions are
  left standing as the chunks left them.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "in the centuries after his death", i.e. after the historian Bada'uni, as the book states, with no dates given; book's historical prose): "only in the centuries after his death did mystical poets in Sind and the Punjab develop the art of utilizing simple folk tales as media for mystical instruction" (p. 154, folio 136).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, the poets being "contemporaries" of "the urban poets in India"; book's historical prose): "While the urban poets in India expressed their veneration of the Prophet in grandiose hymns to display their erudition, their contemporaries in the plains of Sind and the Punjab wrote tender little poems or instructive Golden Alphabets to instil love for him into the souls of the villagers" (p. 222, folio 204).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, endnote 80 to "Mystical Poetry in the Vernaculars"): the story of Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyya, "a son of 'Ali by another wife than Fatima", "is found in the Panjab, in Bengal, South India, and Malaysia" (p. 284, folio 266).
- (book's word: "Panjabis"; refers to: present tense, no date; the version is dated "the late eighteenth century"; author's argument): the romance of Hir Ranjha (printed "Hfr Riinjhii" [text-layer?] [Hīr Rānjhā]), "elaborated in more than a hundred versions in Panjabi, Urdu, Sindhi, and Persian, was given its final form in the late eighteenth century by Warith Shah. It is his verse which is sung, time and again, wherever Panjabis meet" (p. 171, folio 153). This is about a people, not a territory, and the book does not say where they meet.

## From Annemarie Schimmel, Mystical Dimensions of Islam

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_mystical_dimensions_of_islam.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The row's principal figure is the book's most-used single authority: named `Hujwiri`, `Hujwirl`,
`Hujwlri`, `HujwIri`, `Hujwm`, `Huj-` / `wirl`, `Hujwlri`, `Hujwirfs`, `A1I ibn c Uthman al-Hujwirl`,
`CAH ibn cUthman al-HujwIri` and, in the index, `Hujwiri, cAli ibn "-Uthman Data Ganj` / `Bakhsh`
[all text-layer?: Hujwīrī, ʿAlī ibn ʿUthmān Dātā Ganj Bakhsh], and cited far more often still by the
bare siglum `H` (pp. 22–24, 37, 45–48, 68, 73, 82, 89, 93, 100, 104, 118–122, 134, 141, 153–163,
177–186, 219–237, 321, 376–379, 480, 516, 519, 538). **`H` is Nicholson's translation of the
*Kashf al-Maḥjūb*, never a page of this book.**

- **Saint**
  - **Two readings of the death year, kept side by side.** The running text gives `Hujwiri (d. circa
    1071)` (p. 37, folio 6) — `circa` is the book's own hedge — and `He later came to Lahore, the
    capital of the Ghaznawids in India, and he died there in 1071.` (p. 119, folio 88), where `1071`
    prints cleanly in four digits; the index prints `(d. ca. 1071)` (p. 516, folio 485). **The archive
    row records 1072.**
  - Origin and formation: `One of Qushayri's colleagues, who had attended his meetings and had visited
    almost every leading Sufi of his time, was Hujwiri from Ghazna.` (p. 119, folio 88). No birth
    date, no family, no silsila and no teacher other than this attendance on Qushayrī —
    `(not stated)`.
  - Dated only by century elsewhere: `Hujwirl, in the mid-eleventh century, summed up the discussion`
    (p. 45, folio 14); `Even Hujwlri, in a book written in the mid-eleventh century—unfortu- nately no
    longer extant—in spite of all his admiration for Hallaj, felt obliged to declare him "not firmly
    settled."` (p. 104, folio 73). **That lost book's title and the ground of the verdict are
    `(not stated)`.**
  - Contemporary of Anṣārī: `A contemporary of Qushayri and Hujwiri was cAbdullah-i Ansarl (1006-89),
    whose work, like that of Hujwiri, was written in part in his Persian mother tongue.` (p. 120,
    folio 89).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The shrine is named, placed and described as a living cult, in two sentences a chapter apart.**
    `His shrine, called that of Data Ganj Bakhsh, is still a popular place of pilgrimage in Lahore.`
    (p. 119, folio 88) — `still` is Schimmel's word and dates the claim to her own time of writing,
    which the book does not otherwise fix. And: `the name of Hujwiri, who composed his famous Persian
    treatise on Sufism in this town, has already been mentioned; his tomb still provides a place of
    pilgrimage for the Panjabis.` (p. 376, folio 345) — the town is Lahore, from the same sentence.
  - **That is the whole of the shrine material.** No building date, no custodianship, no ʿurs, no
    visitor number, no Auqaf, no architecture, no quarter — all `(not stated)` (explicitly noted at
    pp. 119, 376).
  - The `INDEX OF SUBJECTS` records pilgrimage to him as a topic of the book, set beside pilgrimage
    to Mecca: `Pilgrimage: to Mecca, 32, 66, 67, 71, 89, 106, 229, 230, 264, 388, 425, 430; to Data
    Ganj Bakhsh, 88; rewards of making seventy, 231; Abu Sacld without, 242; spiritual, 276` (p. 538,
    folio 507). `88` is **the book's own index locator** — a printed folio, quoted verbatim, not
    converted. The name `Data Ganj Bakhsh` occurs **once** in that whole chunk, at this entry.
  - **Promoted here by worker A under ruling (a).** The toponym key `data darbar` (2 rows:
    **darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz**, **peer-makki**) was declined by chunk 006 on the ground that the book
    prints `Data Ganj Bakhsh` and not `Data Darbar`. That is an orthographic objection of exactly the
    class that `Ucch`→`uch sharif` and `Sehwan`→`sehwan sharif` were fired through elsewhere in this
    same book, and both rows' `location_short` names this shrine as their own landmark. The two
    pilgrimage sentences above therefore attach to those two rows as well — see the place-level run
    below. Both rows are in any case inside the Lahore 35, so nothing is lost if Rauf reverses it.
- **Significance**
  - *The innovation the book credits him with*: `Hujwirfs important innovation is that he wrote his
    Kashf al-mahjub, "Unveiling of the Hidden," in Persian and thus ushered in a new period in
    mystical literature.` (p. 119, folio 88).
  - *Schimmel's judgement on the book, running across the page break*: `A monument of early Persian
    and noteworthy for its expressiveness, the Kashf, "which belongs to the valid and famous books"
    (N 316), contains much interesting information rarely found in other sources. Although the
    author's` (p. 119, folio 88) `inclination toward systematization sometimes goes too far, it is
    singularly valuable in its approach and its balanced discussion.` (p. 120, folio 89). The praise
    `which belongs to the valid and famous books` is **Jāmī's**, cited as `N 316`, not Schimmel's.
    She repeats the charge of over-systematising at p. 141, folio 110.
  - *His book in the Delhi book trade*, in Barani's list of what it became fashionable to buy in
    Nizāmuddīn's Delhi: `Hujwiri's Kashf al-mahjub` (p. 379, folio 348).
  - *Editions, from the book's own apparatus.* Abbreviations key: `H   A1I ibn c Uthman al-Hujwirl.
    The "Kashf al-Mahjub," the Oldest Persian Treatise on Sufism by al-Hujwiri. Translated by Reynold
    A. Nicholson. Gibb Memorial Series, no. 17. 1911. Reprint. London, 1959.` (p. 22, folio xix,
    continuing onto p. 23, folio XX) — the entry straddles the page break with a stray `C`, an ʿayn
    knocked out of `c Uthman` or `al-Hujwiri`, alone on its own line between the halves (p. 22,
    folio xix). Footnote 62: `CAH ibn cUthman al-HujwIri, Kashf al-mahjub, ed. V. A. Zukovskij
    (Leningrad, 1926; reprint ed., Tehran, 1336 sh./igsy` [text-layer?: 1336 sh./195 — **the final
    digit prints `y`, which is not in the measured substitution table and is not guessed**]`)`
    (p. 119, folio 88). Bibliography: `Hujwiri, CAH ibn c Uthman al-. Kashf al-mahjub. Edited by V. A.
    Zukov-` / `skij. Leningrad, 1926. Reprint. Tehran, 1336 sh./ig57.` [text-layer?: 1957] and
    `The "Kashf al-Mahjub," the Oldest Persian Treatise on Sufism` / `by al-Hujwiri. Translated by
    Reynold A. Nicholson. Gibb Me-` / `morial Series, no. 17. 1911. Reprint. London, 1959.` (p. 480,
    folio 449). `1926`, `1911` and `1959` print cleanly.
  - *Index apparatus.* The main entry, verbatim: `Hujwiri, cAli ibn "-Uthman Data Ganj` / `Bakhsh
    (d. ca. 1071), 6, 14, 15, 17, 51,` / `62, 73, 87, 88-89, 1O3- 11D' lll> 122 >` / `126, 129, 130,
    131, 132, 146, 147, 152,` / `199, 206, 290, 345; Kashf al-mahjub, 88,` / `34°` (p. 516, folio 485).
    **Four tokens in it are unreadable as printed**: `1O3-` [text-layer?: 103], `11D'` [text-layer?:
    110], `lll>` [text-layer?: 111], `34°` [text-layer?: 340 or 348 — **unresolved**]. The book is
    indexed again by its title, `Kashf al-mahjub (Hujwlri), 88, 348` (p. 519, folio 488).
  - *His own positions, as Schimmel explicitly ascribes them to him* — kept apart from the sayings he
    merely transmits, per the notes' own voice test:
    - On the name Sufi, four derivations summed up: `Some assert that the Sufi is so called because he
      wears a woollen garment (jama-i suf), others that he is so called because he is in the first
      rank (saff-i awwal), others say it is because the Sufis claim to belong to the ashab-i Suffa
      (the people of the Bench who gathered around the Prophet's mosque). Others, again, declare that
      the name is derived from safd` [text-layer?: ṣafā] `(purity).` (`H 30`) (p. 45, folio 14).
    - On discord and love: `"Love is concord, and the lover has but one duty in the world, namely to
      keep the commandment of the beloved, and if the object of desire is one, how can discord
      arise?"` (`H 38`) (pp. 46–47, folios 15–16; the sentence is split by the page break).
    - On learning: `"Knowledge is immense and life is short: therefore it is not obligatory to learn
      all the science . . . but only so much as bears upon the religious law"` (`H 11`) (p. 48,
      folio 17).
    - On God's two approaches: `There is a difference between one who is burned by His Majesty in the
      fire of love and one who is illuminated by His Beauty in the light of contemplation.` (`H 367`)
      and `There is a difference between one who meditates upon the Divine acts and one who is amazed
      at the Divine Majesty; the one is a follower of friendship, the other is a companion of love.`
      (`H 373`) (p. 37, folio 6).
    - On the Malāmatiyya, more critical than Sulamī: `"The ostentatious men purposely act in such a
      way as to win popularity, while the malamatl purposely acts in such a way that people reject
      him. Both have their thought fixed on mankind and do not pass beyond that sphere"` (`H 6`)
      (p. 118, folio 87).
    - On the dervish garb: `Its collar is annihilation of intercourse [with men], its two sleeves are
      observance and continence, its two gussets are poverty and purity, its belt is persistence in
      contemplation, its hem is tranquillity in [God's] presence, and its fringe is settlement in the
      abode of union.` (`H 56`) (p. 134, folio 103).
    - On the forty-day retreat: its origin is `derived, as Hujwiri says, from the forty-day fast of
      Moses, when he hoped for a vision from God, as related in Sura 7:138` (p. 134, folio 103).
    - On repentance: `"The penitent is a lover of God, and the lover of God is in contemplation of
      God: in contemplation it is wrong to remember sin, for recollection of sin is a veil between God
      and the contemplator"` (`H 296`) (p. 141, folio 110); and his threefold scheme, which Schimmel
      marks as unique to him — `tauba as return from great sins to obedience; indba` [text-layer?:
      ināba] `is the return from minor sins to love, and auba the return from one's self to God`
      (`H 295`)`. This tripartition is not found elsewhere.` (p. 141, folio 110).
    - On poverty, endorsed by Schimmel in her own voice: `"Its form is destitution and indigence, but
      its essence is fortune and free choice"` (`H 19`) (p. 153, folio 122); and the long description
      of dervishhood, `Dervishhood in all its meanings is a metaphorical poverty … Then he is the Way,
      not the wayfarer, i.e., the dervish is a place over which something is passing, not a wayfarer
      following his own will.` cited as `(Hag)` [text-layer?: **an `H` siglum whose number is damaged
      — not resolved**] (p. 154, folio 123).
    - On contentment: `Hujwirl is probably right in his statement that ridd is a result of love,
      inasmuch as the lover is content with what is done by the beloved (H 180).` (p. 157, folio 126)
      — `probably right` is Schimmel's own judgement, carried as printed.
    - On sobriety: `True sobriety is defined by Hujwiri as "reaching the goal" (H 230)` (p. 160,
      folio 129).
    - On *maʿrifa*: named among the systematisers, `like Hujwm (H 267)` (p. 161, folio 130).
    - His classification of lovers, `which Ghazzali adopted as well, in slightly elaborated form:
      "Those who regard the favor and beneficence of God toward them and are led by that regard to love
      the Benefactor, and those who are so enraptured by love that they reckon all favors as a veil and
      by regarding the Benefactor are led to [consciousness of] His favors"` (`H 308`) (p. 163,
      folio 132). **The square brackets are printed in the book.**
    - His threefold *tauḥīd*: `"God's unification of God, i.e. His knowledge of His Unity; God's
      unification of His creatures, i.e. His decree that a man shall pronounce Him to be one, and the
      creation of unification in his heart; man's unification of God, i.e. their knowledge of the Unity
      of God"` (`H 278`) (p. 177, folio 146).
    - His correction of a misattributed saying, endorsed by Schimmel: `But Hujwirl has given the more
      correct version—Muhammad ibn Wasic` [text-layer?: Wāsiʿ] `said: "I never saw anything without
      seeing God therein,"` (`H 91, 330`) (p. 178, folio 147).
