    - On the *ṣalāt*: `Hujwiri has defined the various types of mystics by their attitudes toward the
      salat: some hold that it is a means of obtaining the presence of God; others regard it as a means
      of obtaining absence` (`H 301`) (p. 183, folio 152).
    - On saintship, which opens the book's whole discussion of it: `"You must know that the principle
      and foundation of Sufism and knowledge of God rest on saintship"` (`H 210`)`, says Hujwirl`
      (p. 230, folio 199). Schimmel pairs him with Jāmī: `and three centuries after him Jam! opened his
      discussions about the history of Sufism with a paragraph on saintship.` (p. 230, folio 199) —
      **the book does not say where or when Hujwīrī wrote** (not stated).
    - On the condemnation of `nazar Ha? l-ahddth` [text-layer?: naẓar ilā ʾl-aḥdāth], `the "looking
      toward the young men"`, to which he `devoted a weighty paragraph` (p. 321, folio 290): `Looking at
      youth and associating with them are forbidden practices and anyone who declares this to be
      allowable is an unbeliever. The traditions brought forward in this matter are vain and foolish. I
      have seen ignorant persons who suspected the Sufis of the crime in question and regarded them with
      abhorrence, and I observed that some have made it a religious rule. All the Sufi shaykhs, however,
      have recognized the wickedness of such practices, which the adherents of incarnation (hulull-` /
      `yat)` [text-layer?: ḥulūliyyat]`—may God curse them!—have left as a stigma on the saints of God
      and the aspirants to Sufism.` (`H 549`) (p. 321, folio 290).
  - *As a transmitter — the act of transmission is a fact about him, the words are not his*: a prayer
    of Sarī as-Saqaṭī `has been preserved by Hujwiri` (p. 162, folio 131); an unnamed predecessor's
    definition of unification is `what an earlier Sufi, quoted by Hujwirl, had said` (p. 177,
    folio 146); and `"To traverse distance is child's play: henceforth pay visits by means of thought;
    it is not worth while to visit any person, and there is no virtue in bodily presence"` (`H 235`)`,
    as one of Hujwiri's masters told his disciple.` — the master is not named, **(not stated)**
    (p. 237, folio 206).
  - *The sigla trap.* The chunk covering pp. 59–74 cites `H` three times — `(H 103)` for Junayd on
    Ibrāhīm ibn Adham (p. 68, folio 37) and `(H 100)` twice for Dhuʾn-Nūn (p. 73, folio 42) — **without
    printing the name `Hujwiri` anywhere in those sixteen pages**; the attachment there rests on the
    abbreviations key printed on PDF 22–24. A Pass-1 worker records this as a judgement a consolidator
    may reasonably reverse.
  - **The `cAli` collision was refused at every occurrence.** The book's `CA1I` / `CAH` is the
    Prophet's son-in-law — `the attribute given by the Shiites to CA1I, the wall Allah par excellence`
    (p. 230, folio 199), `Muhammad's descendants through CA1I and Fatima` (p. 113, folio 82),
    `there is no fata but CA1I` (p. 277, folio 246), `Hasan ibn CA1I the first, 374` (p. 539) — and is
    **not** the row's `Hazrat Ali ibn Usman al-Hujwiri`. The row rests on `Data Ganj Bakhsh` and on
    `Hujwiri`, both unambiguous.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **His own voice → mapped to **data-darbar**.** `H 6` (p. 118, chunk 006), `H 19` / `(Hag)` / `H 180`
  / `H 230` (chunk 008), `H 267` (p. 161), `H 308` (p. 163), `H 278` (p. 177), `H 91, 330` (p. 178)
  (chunk 009), `H 301` (p. 183, folio 152, chunk 010), `H 210` (p. 230, chunk 012), `H 549` —
  Hujwīrī's own paragraph in the first person (p. 321, folio 290, chunk 017).

- **Words he reports from someone else → refused, and parked under "Other".** `H 67` — Ḥamdūn
  al-Qaṣṣār (p. 118, chunk 006); `H 359` — Shiblī's, expressly (p. 132, chunk 007); `H 186` — `one
  of his mystical masters` (p. 160, chunk 008); `H 111` Sarī as-Saqaṭī (p. 162), `H 40` Junayd
  (p. 165), `H 173` Sumnūn (p. 171), `H 158` an unnamed `earlier Sufi` (p. 177), `H 282-83` Junayd
  (p. 177) (chunk 009); `H 293` second occurrence — Shiblī — and `H 458` — a divine address to an
  unnamed mystic (pp. 179, 186, chunk 010); `H 195` (p. 200) and `H 124` (p. 211) (chunk 011);
  `H 235` (p. 237, chunk 012); `H 233-34` (p. 238, folio 207) and `H 302` — Muhammad's experience —
  (p. 249, folio 218), on which chunk 013 **declined to map **data-darbar** at all**; `H 120` —
  Fāṭima of Nishapur (p. 458, chunk 025).

**Chunk 013's warning, and it is the most quotable false attribution in the book.** On p. 244
(folio 213) the saying `"It is better to restore one dead heart to eternal life than to restore to
life a thousand dead bodies."` is attributed by Schimmel **only** to `the saint`, with footnote 20
pointing at **Sorley's *Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit*, p. 248**. The worker: *"A page in a book about
Shāh ʿAbdu'l-Laṭīf is not the same as a saying by him; the consolidator must not promote it to his
words."* It is a genuinely attractive sentence and it will be promoted unless someone stops it.

- ****data-darbar** rests on the siglum `H` alone in one chunk.** Across pp. 59–74 the book cites `H`
  three times and **never prints the name `Hujwiri`**; the attachment there rests on the abbreviations
  key printed on PDF 22–24, outside that chunk. A Pass-1 worker set this out as a judgement a
  consolidator may reasonably reverse; I have kept it, because the key is the book's own.

- **data-darbar** — in chunk 003 it is mapped on *siglum-only* evidence: the name `Hujwiri` is never printed in pp. 59–74; what is printed is the bare `H` three times (`H 103` p. 68; `H 100` twice p. 73), and neither quotation is about Hujwīrī. *"A consolidator may reasonably drop this row from this chunk without losing any content."*

**data-darbar**'s principal figure is `Hazrat Ali ibn Usman al-Hujwiri (Data Ganj Bakhsh)`, and this
book prints the Prophet's son-in-law as `CA1I`, `CAH`, `cAli`, `CA15` and `AH`. **The collision fires
in at least twelve chunks and was refused every time**: p. 113, folio 82 (chunk 006); p. 138,
folio 107 (chunk 007); pp. 180, 186 (chunk 010); p. 200, folio 169 and footnote 77, p. 206
(chunk 011); p. 230, folio 199 (chunk 012); p. 277, folio 246 and footnote 46, p. 288 and p. 282,
folio 251 (chunk 015); p. 348, folio 317 (chunk 018); pp. 369–371 (chunk 020); five names on one
page in chunk 025 (pp. 458, 469, 475, 480–481); **six different people in chunk 026**; and repeatedly
in chunks 028 and 029. Chunk 026 states the safe rule: of its six, *"only the first —
`Hujwiri, CAH ibn c Uthman al-` (p. 480) — is data-darbar's figure."*

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's place-level material on "Lahore" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's `Lahore` — all 35 rows in that place, ruling (a)): the city is given four
  substantive statements in the whole book, and one more inside an article title.
  - **The founding statement, at the moment of the Ghaznawid conquest**: `Lahore became the first
    center of Persian-inspired Muslim culture in the Subcontinent—the name of Hujwiri, who composed
    his famous Persian treatise on Sufism in this town, has already been mentioned; his tomb still
    provides a place of pilgrimage for the Panjabis.` (p. 376, folio 345). Its setting: `The second
    wave of Muslim conquest in India, that of the Ghaznawids about the year 1000` [text-layer?:
    clean]`, brought into the Subcontinent not only scholars like al-Blruni [text-layer?: al-Bīrūnī]
    (d. 1048 [text-layer?: clean]), who made a careful study of Hindu philosophy and life, but
    theologians and poets as well.` (p. 376, folio 345).
  - **Political status**: `Lahore, the capital of the Ghaznawids in India` (p. 119, folio 88), where
    Hujwīrī `died there in 1071` and where `His shrine, called that of Data Ganj Bakhsh, is still a
    popular place of pilgrimage in Lahore.` (p. 119, folio 88).
  - **The seat of the Qadiriyya in Mogul India, and the order is said to have arrived there from
    Sind**: `It was not the "official" Chishti order to which he was attracted, but rather the
    Qadiriyya, represented in Lahore by Mian Mir, who had come there from Sind, together with his
    mystically inclined sister Bibi Jamal.` (p. 392, folio 361); and a seventeenth-century Qadiri
    biography *of Lahore* exists, written by the Mogul heir apparent — `The Sakinat al-auliyff
    [text-layer?: Sakīnat al-auliyāʾ] is a fine biography of Mian Mir and the Qadiriyya in Lahore.`
    (p. 392, folio 361). The book gives that work no date — (not stated).
  - **An annual festival, fixed by place, date and name**: `Madho Lai's anniversary, still celebrated
    in Lahore near the Shalimar Garden on the last Saturday in March, is famous as Meld Chiraghdn
    [text-layer?: Melā Chirāghān], the "Fair of Lamps."` (p. 415, folio 384). No attendance figure,
    no duration, no custodian, no founding year — all (not stated).
  - **A book trade**: of al-Būṣīrī's *Burda*, `Copies are still being sold in the shops of Lahore and
    elsewhere.` (p. 256, folio 225). No shop, no bazaar, no date for the observation — (not stated).
  - **A burial**: `a few years ago a maqam of Muhammad Iqbal (who is buried in Lahore) was erected in
    the garden of Jalaluddin Ruml's tomb in Konya` (p. 270, folio 239). `a few years ago` is the
    book's own dating and no year is given — (not stated).
  - **Residence by proximity only**: `Bullhe Shah (1680-1752) … lived near Lahore.` (p. 419,
    folio 388). The book does not say where he is buried and does not name Kasur — (not stated).
  - **Inside an article title, and fired by chunk 026 under ruling (a) because §6(ii) exempts only
    imprints and index locators**: `Massignon, Louis, and Huart, Clemens. "Les entretiens de Lahore."`
    / `Journal asiatique 209 (1926).` (p. 486, folio 455; the same article cited at p. 391, folio 360,
    footnote 23). **The entire content is the entry as printed.** The Pass-1 worker recorded being
    uncomfortable with this firing — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
  - **Nothing else in the book is said of Lahore as a place.** No mosque, no other shrine, no quarter,
    no building date, no modern condition — **(not stated)**. Every other `Lahore` in the book (some
    fifty occurrences across chunks 007, 009, 013, 014, 015, 016, 017, 018, 019, 021, 022, 023, 025,
    026) is **a publisher's imprint city in a footnote or a bibliography entry**, or the index entry
    `Lahore, 88, 225, 239, 345, 361, 384, 388` (p. 520, folio 489), and all of those were declined.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: the Mogul period, "the Mogul emperors (especially Akbar)"; author's argument): interest in Rumi's work "seems to have been common to all classes of society—the Mogul emperors (especially Akbar) were as fond of him as the simple villagers in Sind and Punjab" (p. 358, folio 327).
- (book's word: "Punjabi"; refers to: present day ("still claim"), looking back to "Nizamuddln's time" [text-layer?: Niẓāmuddīn's]; ethnographic present): in Nizamuddin's time "Sufism became a mass movement in northwestern India", and "A number of Punjabi tribes still claim to have been converted by Fariduddin Ganj-i Shakar" (p. 381, folio 350; header printed `35O` [text-layer?: 350]).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the time of "Shah Ismacll [text-layer?: Ismāʿīl] Shahid" and "Sayyid Ahmad of Bareilly", no date given for the occupation; book's historical prose): both were "known for their brave fight against the Sikh, who had occupied the whole of the Punjab and part of the northwestern frontier" (p. 403, folio 372). The book's unit here is the Punjab as occupied by "the Sikh"; carried as printed.
- (book's word: "a Panjabi village", "The Punjab"; refers to: present day, with Warith Shah's form dated "in the late eighteenth century"; ethnographic present and author's argument): "The visitor who stays in a Panjabi village will certainly find somebody to sing for him the old romance of Hir Ranjha in the classical form given it by Warith Shah"; "The Punjab and the Indus valley have a common tradition of mystical folk literature" (p. 414, folio 383; the section heading `MYSTICAL POETRY IN THE REGIONAL LANGUAGES— SINDHI, PANJABI, PASHTO` begins mid-page under an ordinary header).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the two provinces"; refers to: "back to the fifteenth century", with Madho Lal Husayn as "sixteenth-century"; book's historical prose): "Reliable tradition traces the use of the native language for mystical poetry both in Sind and in the Punjab back to the fifteenth century"; Madho Lal's allusions to the stories of Hir and of Sohni Mehanwal "seems to inaugurate (or perhaps even continue from unknown beginnings) a tradition typical of the religious poetry of the two provinces: the insertion of folk tales into the context of mystical thought" (p. 415, folio 384).
- (book's word: "The Punjab"; refers to: period not stated (an eighteenth-century Panjabi poet is quoted); author's argument): "The Punjab and Sind show close similarities in the types of mystical literature that nourished there"; Panjabi and Sindhi "were excellent media to express mystical feelings, though not mystical theories"; the themes "were sung time and again in forms inherited from the Indian past—dhoras, kdfh [text-layer?: kāfī], way—not in the Persian form of ghazal", in forms "closely connected with indigenous musical modes"; the attack on the "molld" [text-layer?: mollā] was taken up "by the mystics in the northwest part of the Subcontinent", and such verses "appealed particularly to the peasants and the illiterate" (p. 416, folio 385).
- (book's word: "both provinces"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "In both provinces, a preference for the motif of spinning and weaving can be observed, a natural propensity in a cotton-growing country"; "Both provinces have also produced hymns extolling the great masters of the mystical orders, particularly cAbduDl-Qadir Gilanl [text-layer?: ʿAbduʾl-Qādir Gīlānī]"; the imagery "could easily be understood by everybody in the village" (p. 417, folio 386).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, the example Sheikh Tahir is "sixteenth-century"; author's argument): "A strange fact—more visible in Sind than in the Punjab—is the way Hindus shared in the Muslim orders. Some saints were claimed by both communities" (p. 418, folio 387).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "after 1800" and "the twentieth century"; book's historical prose): "In the Punjab, the literary heritage of Panjabi was rarely continued by Muslims after 1800, though in the twentieth century a number of mystical treatises have been written and published in Panjabi, including a part translation of Rumi's Mathnawi" (printed "Rumi'sMathnawi"); the language "was used almost exclusively by the Sikhs as their literary medium, whereas the Panjabi Muslims resorted to Urdu", "though they enjoyed and still enjoy listening to the simple mystical verses and ballads in their spoken mother tongue" (p. 427, folio 396; header printed `306` [text-layer?: 396]).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, Appendix 1): the Ahmad bila mim tradition "has been repeated in high literature as much as in popular mystical poetry, be it among the Turkish Bektashis or among the mystical bards of Sind and the Punjab" (p. 451, folio 420; header printed `42O` [text-layer?: 420]).
- (book's word: "Punjab", "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, Sayyid Muhammad Jiw Jan is "in the seventeenth century"; author's argument, Appendix 2): "In the province of Sind, as well as in Punjab, a special aspect of mystical appreciation of women can be observed that is rarely encountered in other parts of the Muslim world, i.e., the representation of the longing soul in the form of a woman"; the bridal symbolism "is used mostly in the mystically interpreted folk tales of Sind and the Punjab"; "The tragic heroines of the Sindhi and Panjabi folk tales—like Hir, Sassui, Sohni, and many others—represent the human soul in search of the beloved" (p. 465, folio 434).

## From Haroon Khalid, Walking with Nanak

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/khalid_walking_with_nanak.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- **Practices & Events**
  - Author's reconstruction: when someone suggested Tripta "should also offer a chadar (sheet) at the shrine of Ali Hajveri in Lahore, she did so" (p. 21, folio 3).
  - "Data Ganj Baksh in Lahore" is named among Sufi shrines that have "come under attack" as "a wave of Islamic purism has spread over Pakistan due to the influence of the Taliban" (p. 278, folio 252).

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's material headed "Lahore (place-level: 35 rows)", copied into every existing entry that heading lists (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

Mostly thin and incidental. "The Lahore High Court" is treated as an institution, not the Baba Shah Chiragh site; the bibliography imprint "Lahore: Punjabi History Board" is not material.

- **Shrine / Darbar** (the city's religious history as the book gives it)
  - "In Lahore, I found that Muslims and Christians, whose ancestors were once Hindus, continue to visit a Hindu temple their ancestors visited" (temple not named) (p. 32, folio 14).
  - Iqbal Qaiser's LUMS lecture "was titled SikA [OCR?] [Sikh] Architecture in Lahore": "like the Sikh religion, which picks up from both Hinduism and Islam, Sikh architecture draws inspiration from both Hindu and Muslim architecture"; domes "initially part of the Muslim buildings" were made "smaller" by Sikhs, and "Now such domes are also used in Muslim buildings in Pakistan"; Sikh shrines carry "graphic depictions of Sikh Gurus and also sometimes Hindu deities (in older buildings)" (pp. 309–310, folio not stated / folio 284).
  - Rohtas "was not the kind of fort that was found in Lahore, where only royalty stayed" (p. 249, folio 223). Today "the mighty Ravi of history flows like a stream in Lahore" (damaged) (p. 281, folio 255).
  - "The Kashmiri binders who used to reside in Lahore were known for their meticulous binding" (p. 39, folio 21); Bhai Ram Singh was Principal of "Mayo School of Arts in Lahore" (p. 117, folio 99).
- **Practices & Events** (history the book sets in Lahore)
  - Babur, attacking Punjab, destroyed "the cities of Eminabad and Lahore on the way" (p. 15, folio not stated).
  - "the Governor of Lahore, Moin-ul-Mulk also known as Meer Manu" ordered the killing of Sikhs; "The Lahore High Court" heard the Shaheed Ganj case in the British era and "Around 1957-58" (p. 77, folio 59; p. 78, folio 60).
  - Guru Arjan "was summoned to Lahore by Emperor Jahangir" (p. 234, folio 208) and died there (haveli of the Mughal Diwan near the Rang Mahal; samadhi near Lahore Fort; the Ravi — see the Arjan group) (p. 253, folio 227); Chandu Lal was "appointed as the Diwan of Lahore by the Mughal authorities" (p. 253, folio 227); Sikh tradition traces the Prithi Chand–Arjan conflict to "Arja[n]'s trip to Lahore" (p. 274, folio 248); Khusro "rebelled against his father and moved towards Lahore" (p. 255, folio 229).
  - After defeat by Aurangzeb, Dara Shikoh "decided to head west to Lahore and continue his fight"; Guru Har Rai "along with his forces, reached Lahore" and met him there; Dara "had planned to flee to Multan from Lahore"; a version has Aurangzeb follow "after he fled to Lahore" (pp. 210–211, folios 184–185).
  - "In 1931" Bhagat Singh, Rajguru and Sukhdev "were hung at Lahore jail" (p. 48, folio 30).
  - Ghulab Singh is "the first Sikh police officer in the revamped traffic police of Lahore" (p. 35, folio 17); new apartments "built recently to cater to the growing demands of urban Lahore" (p. 36, folio 18); indigenous trees "now being replaced by imported ones in the developed areas of Lahore" (p. 46, folio 28); Bedian was founded "near the city of Lahore"; "The Bedian road is the old route to Amritsar", and Prithi Chand's rival shrine would lie "on the route from Lahore to Amritsar" (p. 38, folio 20; p. 44, folio 26).
  - Lahore is listed among cities whose Sikh communities have "Only recently" been able "to exert themselves" (p. 88, folio 70); Jasdeep studies at "Punjab University, Leh i) [OCR?] [Lahore?]" (p. 88, folio 70).
  - Passing mentions only: a beggar "had once been a rich merchant from Lahore" (reconstruction, p. 19, folio not stated); the author met Iqbal Qaiser at his university "an [OCR?] [in] Lahore" (p. 41, folio 23); Saidpur the most important trading stop "After Delhi and Lahore" (reconstruction, p. 104, folio 86); the Grand Trunk Road "connected with Lahore and then Delhi" (p. 115, folio 97); the caravanserai route "Lahore, Satgaraha, Deepalpur, Harrapa, Multan and Uch Sharif" (p. 119, folio 101); Tulamba "about midway between Lahore and Multan" (p. 123, folio 105); Sajjan suspects "rich merchants from Lahore" (p. 124, folio 106); Qaiser "had returned to Lahore" (OCR "Lahowe") (p. 127, folio 109); Nanak heard of Farid "while I was in Lahore" (p. 141, folio 123); Lahore–Islamabad "about three hundred and fifty kilometres", Islamabad drivers "much more courteous than the drivers of Lahore" (p. 159, folio 141); Nanak and Mardana reached Lahore on their way home (p. 163, folio 145); "a city like Lahore, where everyone wears their religion on their [sleeve]" (damaged) (p. 174, folio 148); the author returned to Lahore (p. 179, folio 153); "the Lahore and [OCR? 'ane'] Kasur region" (p. 185, folio 159); Sialkot business travels between "Lahore, Gujranawala and Sialkot" (p. 191, folio 165); flooding "was not the case in Lahore", and Sialkot faces differ "from that of people living in Lahore and other regions of Central Punjab" (p. 193, folio 167); Karachi Hindu customs unlike "those that I was used to in Lahore" (p. 223, folio 197); an army marching "[to] the Khyber from Lahore" (reconstruction, pp. 258–259, folios 232–233); the author "from Lahore … even though I now live in Islamabad" (p. 282, folio 256); they "drove back to Lahore in the night" (p. 286, folio 260).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Pakistani Punjab"; refers to: the author's own lifetime, present day; travelogue/introduction): the author, "born and brought up in Pakistani Punjab", heard of Guru Nanak as founder of Sikhism, poet and spiritual leader, "Yet given the Muslim-dominant culture of Pakistani Punjab, there were no avenues available for me within the culture to explore his personality" (p. 15, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Pakistani Punjab", "Punjab"; refers to: present day and the author's childhood; author's argument): in "Pakistani Punjab", Punjabi "is looked down upon. It is considered to be a language of the boorish and the uneducated"; his elite private school fined any student speaking Punjabi "even in the playground"; "the educated elite of Punjab ends up disowning, in fact, ridiculing anything Punjabi, which includes the poetry of Baba Farid, Guru Nanak, Shah Hussain, Waris Shah and Bulleh Shah" (printed "Babs Farid" [OCR?] [Baba Farid]; the sentence opening at "rin system that is prevalent in Pakistani Punjab" is damaged [OCR?]) (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "Punjab government"; refers to: the day of the visit, present day, date not stated; travelogue): over the locked gate of the school housing the gurdwara at Makhdoom Pur Pahuran hung "a banner describing the new policy of the Punjab government to improve the education system in public schools" (printed "improv" [OCR?]) (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "Pakistani Punjabi", "Punjab"; refers to: present day, "the past few years"; travelogue): Boota Singh of Nena Kot ("We are not from the Tribal Areas. We are Punjabis") was "the first Pakistani Punjabi Sikh I had interacted with. Earlier, I had only encountered Pathan Sikhs who had moved to Punjab in the past few years" (p. 83, folio 65).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: present day; book's historical prose): devotees of Nanak who refused the Khalsa of Guru Gobind Singh: "There are thousands of such Sikhs living in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Punjab and Sindh in Pakistan, and they are referred to as Nanak Panthi Hindus. In their temples, along with pictures of Hindu deities, one also finds a picture of Guru Nanak" (p. 98, folio 80).
- (book's word: "southern Punjab"; refers to: present day, "for the past few years", with a historical contrast, period not stated; reported view of Ayesha Siddiqa, author of *Military Incorporated*, name printed "Ayesha Siddiqa 15 4 Pakista" [OCR?] at p. 283): organisations "like Jamat-ud-Daywa and Laskhar-e-Jhangvi" [OCR?], of the Ahl-e-Hadith school, "have seeped into the social fabric of southern Punjab, a region that has historically rejected such strands of Islam and adopted a syncretistic version of religion. Such Sufi shrines are a symbol of that strand of Islam"; this is changing as they open seminaries "in these rural areas" and attract unemployed youth (p. 284, folio 258).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: present day, "a recent paper"; reported view of Dr. Tahir Kamran): his paper "'Evolution and Impact of "Deobandi" Islam in the Punjab'" records "a similar phenomenon": such organisations are popular with the youth of the region because "they offer an explanation for their current oppression"; their first step is "to target such shrines and their guardians, who have become immensely rich feeding off the oppressed classes" (p. 284, folio 258).
- (book's word: "The southern region of Punjab"; refers to: present day; author's argument): "The southern region of Punjab, where Uch Sharif lies, has more traces of religious and cultural syncretism compared to the northern and central region, where there is a greater prevalence of education and hence religious intolerance" (p. 277, folio 251).
- (book's word: "Punjab", "Sindh"; refers to: present day, on the drive to Uch Sharif; travelogue): "The boundary between Sindh and Punjab becomes a blur as we drive through Bahawalpur"; "This is where the cultures of Punjab and Sindh merge. Some of the roadside hotels are called Punjab Hotel, while others are Sindh Hotel"; the language, "Siraiki", is "an amalgamation of the two languages"; Iqbal Qaiser: "The Siraiki spoken in Multan is closer to Punjabi, while here it is more like Sindhi" (p. 276, folio 250).
- (book's word: "lower Punjab"; refers to: the day of the visit to Inderjit, present day, date not stated; travelogue): Geo news on the television was "covering the floods in Sindh and lower Punjab" (p. 306, folio 280).
- (book's word: "Punjabi"; refers to: present day; travelogue): on a mound, shrubs of "karir (capparis deciduas), another ancient Punjabi species of plant that is no longer found anywhere except in abandoned places" (p. 92, folio 74).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the end of the eighteenth century" at p. 34, "the nineteenth century" at p. 87 — both as printed; book's historical prose): "When Maharaja Ranjit Singh became the sovereign of Punjab at the end of the eighteenth century he spent large amounts of money on the renovation and expansion of many gurdwaras", and "attached vast tracts of land to these gurdwaras, so that the revenues from the lands could facilitate their functioning" (p. 34, folio 16); he is called "the Sikh ruler of Punjab, Maharaja Ranjit Singh in the nineteenth century" (p. 87, folio 69).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the time of Guru Hargobind and Emperor Jahangir; book's historical prose, reporting "some claims"): "some claims have asserted that Hargobind [printed "Hargobe" [OCR?]] joined his employment, serving him in the Punjab" (p. 234, folio 208).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the time the mantle of Guruship was bestowed upon Harkrishan" (printed "By the me" [OCR?]); book's historical prose): "the Sikh community had expanded beyond the confines of the Punjab into places as far as Kabul and Ghaznvi [OCR?]", making central control by the Guru "increasingly difficult" (p. 181, folio 155).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; book's historical prose, reporting historians): the Guruship becoming feudal is called ironic "because Sikhism is seen as a reaction against the feudal land gt [OCR?] … ot [OCR?] [of] Punjab bv [OCR?] [by] a lor [OCR?] [lot] ot [OCR?] [of] historians" — the noun after "feudal land" is lost (p. 182, folio 156).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: Nanak's lifetime, "the time the Mughal marauder Babur attacked Punjab"; introduction): Nanak's Babur Bani is "a long poem that Nanak wrote at the time the Mughal marauder Babur attacked Punjab, destroying the cities of Eminabad and Lahore on the way" (p. 15, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: the time of Nanak's parents and of the young Nanak; author's reconstruction): "The jungles of Punjab were infested with thugs and dacoits who attacked innocent travellers" (p. 22, folio 4); the young Mardana's questions to Nanak include "the jungles of Punjab and the bandits residing in them" (p. 110, folio 92).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: Nanak and Mardana's time; author's reconstruction): Mirasis travelled city to city singing of local chieftains, and landlords showered them with gifts; Mardana "knew by heart several folk stories of Hindu deities and Muslim saints that he would sing during different festivals of Punjab" (p. 121, folio 103); as a younger man "he would roam the cities and villages of Punjab, singing folk songs and legends", the most popular being Puran Bhagat's (p. 184, folio 158).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: the time of Sheikh Ibrahim, Farid's twelfth successor, "three hundred years since the death of the great Sufi" as printed; author's reconstruction): Baba Farid's poetry "was still sung by qawwals at the shrine and by fakirs in the cities of Punjab" (p. 142, folio 124).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the Punjabi"; refers to: the Sikh force's taking of Hassan Abdal under Hari Singh, date not stated; author's reconstruction in the novelised chapter "The Legend"): the Sikh soldiers "had arrived from the Punjab"; "For almost a thousand year[s] invaders had crossed into the Punjab from the West. Therefore in [the imagination?] [OCR?] of the Punjabi the West was a fearful place where giants resided" (p. 258, folio 232); Hari Singh wanted the tribesmen to know "that now it was the time of Punjab to create havoc in the Khyber" (p. 262, folio 236).

## From Harish Dhillon, Janamsakhis

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/dhillon_janamsakhis.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's material headed "Lahore (place-level: 35 rows)", copied into every existing entry that heading lists (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

The book's "Lahore". The Duni Chand story names no present-day site in Lahore.
- "Lahore had by now taken on the position of a pre-eminent rown [text-layer?] [town] of the Lodh.i [text-layer?] [Lodhi] Empire, north of Delhi" (p. 124, folio 138) — refers to Nanak's second udasi. "Daulat Khan Lodh.i [text-layer?] [Lodhi]" "had risen in rank to become the governor of Lahore and the adjoining areas" (p. 124, folio 138), and was away in Delhi (p. 125, folio 139).
- Ghori "was not able to conquer Lahore" when he captured Punjab "in 1192" (p. 136, folio 151).
- In Dhillon's retelling ("The Story of the Needle"): the evening "sacsang [text-layer?] [satsang]" with "a large turnout" (p. 125, folio 139); "Duni Chand, the amil (revenue collector) of Lahore", whose haveli "was at the centre ofthe [text-layer?] [of the] town, it was set in a large compound of its own" (pp. 125–127, folios 139–141); "a sec [text-layer?] [set] of seven red pennants (flags)": "Each flag stands for a lakh of rupees" (p. 128, folio not stated); the needle to be returned "when we meet next in the other world" (p. 129, folio 143); Duni Chand and his wife became disciples and he gave away his "seven lakh rupees" (p. 134, folio 148).
- Dialogue: "my fields near Lahore, which are only a few hundred kos away" (p. 80, folio 88). Point of departure: "From Lahore, Nanak made his way to Sialkot" (p. 136, folio 151).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- Book's word "Punjab" / "the Punjab region"; refers to the invasions and Muslim rule before Nanak's birth, as the book frames them (Ghaznavis, Ghoris, Tughlaqs "after the beginning of the eleventh century", through Taimur "in 1398"): "Since Punjab in the north was the gateway to India, it bore the brunt of all these invasions" (p. 15, folio 17); the conquerors, "once they had settled down in the Punjab region", turned to "either converting [printed "convening" [text-layer?]] the kafirs or non-believers or destroying them" (p. 16, folio 18); "For over three hundred years, Islam and Hinduism existed side by side in the Punjab region in a state of constant conflict" (p. 16, folio 18).
- Book's word "Punjab"; refers to 1192, Ghori's conquest: "Shahabuddin Muhammad Ghori captured Punjab in 1192" (p. 136, folio 151).
- Book's word "Punjab"; refers to the Puratan Janamsakhi's fifth udasi "(1523-1524)": "This lasted about one year and covered the towns and regions within Punjab." (p. 55, folio 61).
- Book's word "Punjab"; refers to the third udasi "(1514-1516)" of the "more modern accounts": to Tashkent "and then back to Punjab via the Kashmir Valley" (p. 55, folio 61).
- Book's word "Punjab"; refers to Nanak's time, the Haridwar episode dated "1504" and the Prayag episode (year not stated): Nanak faces "west, in the direction of Punjab" (p. 79, folio 87); "Punjab is very far away" (p. 80, folio 88); "He is Nanak, the great Guru from Punjab" (p. 102, folio 113).
- Book's word "the Punjab"; refers to the return to Talwandi "in I 506 [text-layer?] [1506]": "there were very few in the Punjab who did not know of him and his teachings" (p. 123, folio 137).
- Book's words "Punjab" / "the Punjab" / "south-west Punjab"; refers to the second udasi (no year given): Nanak "crossed through Rajpurana and the south-west Punjab till he came to Multan" (p. 164, folio 180); "Nanak was by now a well-known figure in Punjab" (p. 167, folio 183); the Multan Sufis' "patronizing tolerance" "As long as he was preaching elsewhere in the Punjab" (p. 168, folio 184).
- Book's word "the Punjab"; refers to the close of the fourth udasi, around Babur's "third incursion into India" (no year given): "a south-eastern route co [text-layer?] [to] the Punjab" (p. 224, folio 246).

## From Haroon Khalid, A White Trail

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/khalid_a_white_trail.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's "Data Darbar", "the shrine of the eleventh century Sufi, Ali Hujwiri", "near the walled city of Lahore" (p. 207, folio not stated).

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - "Jind Kaur, ordered the construction of a library at the shrine of the eleventh century Sufi, Ali Hujwiri and gifted all the Qurans she had in her possession to it" (p. 207, folio not stated).
- **Practices & Events**
  - Named among the famous Muslim shrines at which Ghulam Ali's dandiya group has played (p. 63, folio not stated); among the Sufi shrines Nadeem has visited "on the occasion of urs" (p. 219, folio not stated).
- **Significance**
  - "The shrine near the walled city of Lahore goes by the name of Data Darbar and is one of the most popular Sufi Shrines in the city, revered by people from different religions" (p. 207, folio not stated).

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's material headed "Lahore (place-level: 35 rows)", copied into every existing entry that heading lists (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

The book's "Lahore", "the city", "this city", "the walled city of Lahore" (11 of 13 chunks). Place-level, ruling (a): the author's home city and the base of most of his fieldwork, so the material is mostly about its minority communities, not its shrines. Only two archive sites are named in it — "Jain Mandir at Anarkali" and "Krishna Mandir on the Ravi road", both attacked after 1992. Fort-area and Anarkali material is in the separate Lahore Fort and Anarkali groups below; the Shiva statue in the Lahore Museum is under the Shiva group.

- **The book's Lahore and its fieldwork** (author's own account)
  - Contents list the Lahore chapters: "Shri Valmiki's birthday at Lahore", "Shri Krishna Janamasthami at Lahore", "Palm Sunday at Sacred Heart Cathedral, Lahore", "Good Friday at the Sacred Heart Cathedral, Lahore", "Easter Sunday at the Cathedral Church of Resurrection, Lahore", "Navroz in Lahore", "Hazrat Bab's birthday in Lahore" (p. 5, folio not stated), "Commemoration of Guru Arjan's martyrdom at Gurdwara Dera Sahib, Lahore" and "Commemoration of Maharaja Ranjit Singh's death anniversary at Lahore" (p. 6, folio not stated).
  - From 2008 the author "would visit an obscure monument around Lahore and write about it"; he found "a Hindu temple, lying in the middle of a congested locality in the heart of Lahore" (temple not named) (p. 9, folio not stated).
  - Two counts, kept side by side: "the five religious communities living in and around Lahore (Hindus, Christians, Sikhs, Baha'is and Zoroastrians)" (p. 9, folio not stated) and "the four religious minorities living in Lahore (Hindu, Sikh, Christian and Baha'i)" (p. 10, folio not stated).
  - The CAP "Minority Project", on "religious minorities in Lahore", "officially began in September 2010" (p. 10, folio not stated).
  - Afterword: "Based as I am in Lahore, much of my fieldwork took place in the city itself and its vicinity. This is bound to have had its limitations."; "My research with the Hindu community primarily remained confined to the city of Lahore. All the structured interviews that I have used are of people from here." (p. 233, folio not stated). Zoroastrians: "I limited myself to the members of the community based in Lahore", "only three structured interviews", plus Parsis met "during the Navroz celebration"; his account of Lahore–Karachi differences was "provided to me by my sources in Lahore". Baha'is: "Lahore and Islamabad being two of the important cities as far as Baha'i representation is concerned"; "I interacted primarily with the Baha'is in Lahore" (p. 234, folio not stated).
- **After 6 December 1992 and the wars** (book's historical prose and informants)
  - After "6th of December 1992", "Sitla Mandir at Shah Alami, Lahore, Jain Mandir at Anarkali, Lahore and Bhadra Kali Mandir at Thokar Niaz Baig, Lahore were a few prominent temples that were attacked" (pp. 21–22, folio not stated); "Functional temples like Krishna Mandir on the Ravi road and Valmiki Mandir at Neela Gumbad, both in Lahore, were also burned down" (p. 22, folio not stated).
  - Shami Lal, "Another Hindu from Lahore", shut down his business "for several months after 1992" (p. 22, folio not stated).
  - "A lot of prominent Hindus from Lahore went into hiding during the wars" of 1965 and 1971 (p. 74, folio not stated).
- **Partition memories** (informants unless marked)
  - "Lal was a teenager when partition riots spread in Lahore"; his grandfather "refused to leave his ancestral home" (p. 22, folio not stated).
  - According to Bishna Ram, at partition friends and relatives told them "that Lahore was no longer safe for us" and that they should move to India (p. 67, folio not stated). Shazia Waleed's father was "a prominent Hindu businessman from Lahore who continued living here despite partition" (p. 77, folio not stated).
  - According to Dr. Ammar Farooqi, a 73-year-old Protestant poet: "We Christians were living here before Lahore became a part of Pakistan" (p. 128, folio not stated).
  - According to Mary, an 83-year-old widow, her family "were un-touchable Hindus" who lived "near Shahjamal on the canal that now divides Lahore into two parts. Its source lies somewhere in India"; near partition "we would frequently sight mutilated bodies floating in the water", and her mother made the children wear a cross to show "to anyone who would come asking for our religion" (p. 132, folio not stated). (The locality name is the whole basis for `shah-jamal`; the shrine is not mentioned.)
  - According to Farah Desai, an 82-year-old Zoroastrian: "At the time of partition, there were quite a few Parsis ... living in Lahore and Amristar", "all well to do"; most later emigrated, "settling in the States or Europe"; "There are only a handful of us left here" (p. 136, folio not stated). Sadaf, born "after partition", grew up in "a Lahore that was different from the multi-religious city that Farah grew up in"; by then the sari "had become the symbol of an Indian woman and wasn't encouraged in Lahore any more" (pp. 138–139, folio not stated).
  - Book's historical prose: before the partition riots "Tara Singh, a prominent Sikh leader at that time, took out his kirpan on the stairs of the Punjab Assembly in Lahore and said that the time to take revenge for the Gurus had come", "a result of a resolution presented in the Punjab Assembly for the creation of Pakistan" (p. 199, folio not stated); "Sir Zaffarullah Khan ... drafted the Lahore Resolution which the Muslim League presented in Lahore in March 1940" (pp. 211–212, folio not stated).
  - Alfred Woolner's black statue outside the old campus of the University of Punjab is "one of the few statues of pre-partition Lahore that has survived", "defying the iconoclastic streak of the country which emerged post 1947" (p. 100, folio not stated).
- **Hindus and Balmikis**
  - Prem Gupta, "a 40 year old Hindu activist from Lahore", head of the Hindu Sudhar Sabha (p. 46, folio not stated); in his Muslim-dominated neighbourhood "theirs are the only Hindu houses. Unlike the majority of the Hindus, living in Lahore, they have refused to take up dual names" (p. 48, folio not stated); "Hindu festivals are celebrated with much pomp in these areas [Khyber Pakhtunkhwa]; more so than in Lahore"; Gupta: "If you want to see the lights of Diwali, go to Peshawar. You will find nothing in Lahore" (p. 49, folio not stated).
  - The Neela Gumbad Valmiki temple "serves a small community of Balmiki Hindus in Lahore, numbering only a few hundred, most of whom are Christians on record" (p. 66, folio not stated); "On religious festivals like Holi, Diwali and Shri Valmiki's birthday, the Neela Gumbad Mandir becomes an island of religious syncretism in Lahore" (p. 70, folio not stated).
  - Pilgrimage from Lahore: pilgrims came to Katas in 2010 from cities "including Lahore" (p. 47, folio not stated); in 2011 the Neela Gumbad priest brought "a delegation of three hundred people from Lahore and neighbouring regions" to Ram Thamman (pp. 63–64, folio not stated).
  - Book's historical prose: River Parsuni "is the old name of river Ravi, flowing next to the city of Lahore"; "Lahore and Kasur are said to be named after Luv and Kush" (p. 71, folio not stated).
- **Christians**
  - Untouchability: "Even in prominent cities like Lahore, several Muslims refuse to eat with Christians and consider utensils used by them to be impure"; "A lot of low end hotels and restaurants not only in rural areas but even in metropolitan cities like Lahore have separate utensils for Christians" (p. 90, folio not stated). Colonel Chaudary, "a high-caste Christian who resides in Lahore", thirty years "in the Pakistani army": Muslim labourers in his house refused his cook's food "because of the impurity associated with eating at a Christian's house"; Dr. Yousuf John, "a politician and a social worker based in Lahore", was told at school not to put his mouth to "the only tap in the school" — "I was the only Christian student in the school, you see" (school's location not stated) (p. 91, folio not stated).
  - According to Javed Aftab, "a 52 year old Catholic": "Before 9/11 a huge procession used to leave from the Don Bosco School (another Church school) on the Railway road" on foot to the Sacred Heart Cathedral, "about twenty kilometres from there"; "All the Catholic Churches in the city would merge into that one procession"; "Every year, there used to be religious conferences around the city", "About three thousand people", stopped at the government's request "Following the threats of rising terrorism" (pp. 114–115, folio not stated).
  - Nasreen, 54, "a resident of Shahdara in Lahore", is a regular Maryabad pilgrim (p. 97, folio not stated); "the Bishop Sahib comes from Lahore" on the festival's second day (p. 86, folio not stated). Easter breakfast at the Cathedral Church of Resurrection is "halwa puri and channa, typical Lahori nashta" (p. 134, folio not stated). "Power outages in the city have become worse", so the Church keeps a generator on standby (p. 117, folio not stated).
  - "a school principal in Lahore was accused of blasphemy" over a multiple-choice question listing his own name beside the Prophet of Islam as role-model options (p. 104, folio not stated).
- **Sikhs**
  - "it is now rare that one runs into a Sikh in this city, a city which was once ruled by a Sikh and the seat of their political strength" (p. 196, folio not stated).
  - Guru Ram Das "was also born in present day Pakistan, in the city of Lahore"; "Guru Arjan spent a lot of time in the same city, where he was assassinated and his remains interred"; Guru Hargobind "also spent a lot of time here" (p. 222, folio not stated).
  - According to Kartar, at a restaurant "in Model Town", "a Lahore locality" — "This is not a low end place like Shah Alami" — he was told "they cannot serve me" because "they did not have 'our' utensils" (p. 186, folio not stated).
  - The "Dyal Singh Memorial Trust, a pre-partition trust based in Lahore", runs the book stall at Tambu Sahib (p. 224, folio not stated).
  - In 1799 Ranjit Singh "was greeted enthusiastically by a population sick of the lawlessness of that time"; he built "a mosque at a mohalla called Gul Begum in Lahore"; Fakir Azizudin's "family still lives in Lahore in their ancestral house, located inside the walled city" (p. 207, folio not stated).
- **Parsis (Zoroastrians)** (informants)
  - The city's Zoroastrian community is "microscopic" (p. 136, folio not stated); "There are only thirty five Parsis left in Lahore", according to Farah (p. 141, folio not stated).
  - Daria Boatwala, who moved to Lahore "sometime in the eighties": "Lahore is much more monolithic and I would say religiously conservative"; she still wears dresses to work "but there are a few people who object to it"; many think Parsis "worship fire, which is not true"; "In the beginning, I loved the fact that Lahore was a quiet, peaceful place with lots of greenery"; of Navroz, "Nothing really happens here as you can see" (p. 142, folio not stated). Tambola "is the only game they play in Lahore now" at Navroz (p. 143, folio not stated).
  - "Because of no priest there is no way to perform a traditional Parsi marriage in Lahore. The same applies for the rites after death"; there is no Tower of Silence in Lahore; "the Parsi community buries its dead in a graveyard next to the Minar-e-Pakistan facing the ancient walled city of Lahore" (p. 144, folio not stated).
  - According to Sadaf, "Rettigan Road" is "a prominent locality in colonial Lahore, now taken over by small businesses and shops and no longer the clean spacious area that it once was" (p. 140, folio not stated).
- **Baha'is and Ahmadis**
  - "The LSA at Lahore was established before partition"; it meets at the Baha'i Centre "near the Ganga Ram Hospital" (p. 149, folio not stated); about "forty odd people" attend "out of the one hundred and fifty Baha'is registered with the LSA" (p. 147, folio not stated).
  - Usman Nawaz, "a 62 year old man living in Shahdara, Lahore", worked eight years at the "Sohrab Cycle Factory, located across the road from his home in Shahdrah", and was "eventually forced to resign because of the hostile attitude of his colleagues"; a Muslim worker threw a stone and injured him "because he was a Baha'i"; management "were afraid to take any action" (pp. 152–153, folio not stated).
  - "On 28th May 2010, two houses of worship of the Ahmadiyya community in Lahore were attacked, resulting in the death of 94 people and all political leaders refrained from visiting the site or sympathizing with the community" (p. 152, folio not stated).
  - "In February 2012, the lawyers' community of the Lahore High Court un-officially banned the sale and consumption of Shezan drinks in the premises of the court" because Shezan "is owned by an Ahmadiyya family" (p. 211, folio not stated).
- **Institutions and cityscape** (reportage and book's historical prose)
  - The National College of Arts, "established in Lahore in 1857", "considered to be the most prestigious art college in the country" (p. 47, folio not stated); the NCA "situated next to the Museum", established during the colonial era, faces "the University of Punjab (old campus)" (p. 100, folio not stated).
  - "the Forman Christian College Lahore, one of the oldest colleges set up by Christian missionaries" (p. 48, folio not stated); FC College, "One of the leading colleges in the city of Lahore", was "one of the first English-medium institutes to be set up here, during the latter half of the nineteenth century" (p. 102, folio not stated).
  - On Sunday "1st April 2012" at seven am, "Mall Road, one of the busiest boulevards in the city is deserted"; Sunday cricket in all the grounds, "a usual Sunday feature"; "A drive along the road is a journey through the colonial era": the Lahore High Court, "combining European and Eastern architectural techniques", near the Panorama Market; facing it "the Lahore Cathedral built in the late nineteenth century is a model of the Gothic architectural tradition" (p. 100, folio not stated). (The keys "mall" and "lahore high court" are what attach `shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh`; the book never mentions the shrine.)
  - Masjid Shuhad, "a small white structure with an open courtyard", at the Regale Chowk junction, on Fridays "teeming with pilgrims", mats spread on the road for "hundreds of men" (pp. 100–101, folio not stated).
  - "The Roshani gate, one of the thirteen gateways that lead into the ancient walled city of Lahore, is between the Alamgiri Gate and the Royal Mosque"; lamps "were lit all along the passageway ... hence the name Roshani gate, or the gate of lights"; "Behind it is the Heera-Mandi bazaar, the area reserved for the prostitutes of the city before they dispersed all over Lahore, after a crackdown on them during the Islamist government of General Zia-ul-Haq"; "the musicians remain. This is still one of the largest markets for traditional instruments in the city"; it is "also famous for the food street that runs parallel to the wall of the Badshahi Masjid" (p. 205, folio not stated).
  - The River Ravi, which flowed next to the fort, "has changed its course, moving a few kilometres away" (p. 195, folio not stated).
  - Sabar Elahi, "a 62 year old entrepreneur from Shahdara, Lahore", runs a hotel "on the main road, right after the Ravi Bridge crossing" (p. 99, folio not stated). "One hardly finds these bottles [older-logo Pepsi] in Lahore anymore" (p. 221, folio not stated).
- **Incidental** (distance and transit only)
  - The Okara cyclists "will spend the night in Lahore, about one hundred and twenty kilometres from Okara" (p. 85, folio not stated); walkers come to Maryabad "from cities as far as Lahore (a distance of about 70 kms.)" (p. 88, folio not stated); the Sahiwal civil court is "about one hundred and fifty kilometres from Lahore" (p. 117, folio not stated).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Southern Punjab"; refers to: the present, c. the book's writing; book's historical prose): of Pakistan's non-Muslims other than the Ahmadiyya, "the majority are Hindus living in the 'faraway' 'peripheral' regions of Southern Punjab, inner Sindh and Baluchistan" (p. 12, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the cities of Punjab"; refers to: the months after the Babri Mosque demolition, "6th of December 1992"; book's historical prose): "Well-known Hindus living in the cities of Punjab had to hide for several months after the incident" (p. 22, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "urban Punjab"; refers to: the present, c. 2010; author's argument): "The religious distinctions between Hindus and Christians have become blurred in urban Punjab" (p. 28, folio not stated).
- (book's words: "Central Punjab", "Southern Punjab"; refers to: "historically", the partition, and "after 1947" to the present; author's argument): "Compared to Central Punjab, Southern Punjab has been historically tolerant towards other non-Muslim faiths, which is why a significant Hindu population continues to live here"; "violence here during the partition never scaled the heights it did in the other regions, therefore the resentments between communities that seeped in after 1947 were not that serious" (p. 31, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: the present, c. 2010–2011; author's argument): "A lot of Hindus in Punjab do this, passing off as Muslims or Christians by taking up non-Hindu names. This is a survival technique in a hostile environment" (p. 39, folio not stated). Minorities in KPK are visible in society, "which is hardly the case in Punjab, which is much more monolithic" (p. 49, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Pakistani Punjab"; refers to: after partition to the present; author's argument): without Hussain's family Ram Thamman "would have also been taken over by the refugees and converted into a household, like the hundreds of temples and Gurdwaras all over Pakistani Punjab" (p. 58, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Governor of Punjab"; refers to: the present, c. 2011, and "recent years", no date given; book's historical prose): "the Governor of Punjab Salman Taseer was gunned down" (p. 48, folio not stated); the Aasia Bibi case "received international coverage after the former Governor of Punjab, Salman Taseer came out in support of this woman and was later killed for his pains" (p. 90, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the book's present, c. 2011, with "the census of 1998" cited; author's argument): "unlike the Hindus, the Christian community has a formidable presence in the Punjab"; Hindus "are a smaller community in the Punjab and can therefore be ignored" by political parties and leaders. Most Hindus are scattered in other provinces, "with only a few in Punjab. The Christians on the other hand have an overwhelming majority in the Punjab (among the minorities)" (p. 87, folio not stated).
- (book's words: "the Punjab", "the province"; refers to: the present, c. the time of writing; author's argument): Shanti Nagar's Christian landlords make it "a unique case study in the social fabric of the Punjab. Whereas in most of the villages across the province, Muslims are the dominant landowners, while Christians are confined to menial jobs associated with the untouchable caste" (p. 123, folio not stated); "In a regular village where Christians are on the lower rung ... they would be treated as untouchables" (p. 124, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: the attacks of 2005 and 2009, as the book dates them; author's argument): the attacks at Sangla Hill (2005) and at Gojra and Bahmniwala, Kasur (2009), "all of which happen to be located in Punjab"; "The story of a few burnt pages from the Quran found in local mosques seems to happen often" (p. 125, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the present of Daria Boatwala's account, after she moved to Lahore "sometime in the eighties"; informant: Daria Boatwala): "here in the Punjab, the situation is much different. There are hardly any Zoroastrians here and I have never come across any Hindu. The only Christians that you see are those who are cleaners and sweepers" (p. 142, folio not stated).
- (book's words: "the Punjab", "central Punjab", "the province"; refers to: period not stated ("an ancient festival"), then "After the creation of Pakistan" to the present, c. 2011; book's historical prose and author's argument): "Lohri like Vaisakhi or Basant, is an ancient festival of the Punjab that commemorates the change of season", celebrated "on the 13th of January, which is generally the last night of the month of Poh". Traditionally it means a village bonfire, dry fruit, folk songs, children collecting wood, and kheer cooled overnight (pp. 192–193, folio not stated). After the creation of Pakistan "this indigenous festival of the Punjab died down"; "the Sikh and the Hindu minorities of central Punjab also stopped this celebration" (p. 193, folio not stated). It was "earlier celebrated on a large scale throughout the province. It is ironical though that this Punjabi tradition shunned by Punjab is now being revived by a group of Pathan men residing in Punjab" (p. 194, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the older days", period not stated more exactly; book's historical prose): "yellow was commonly used for buildings in Punjab during the older days" (p. 197, folio not stated).
- (book's words: "South Punjab", "Southern Punjab"; refers to: the present, c. 2011; book's historical prose): Nanak Panthi Sikhs "belong to the areas of South Punjab and Sindh", with temples "in Sindh and Southern Punjab" (p. 197, folio not stated); the Dera Sahib langar workers are "low-caste Nanak Panthis from Southern Punjab" (p. 201, folio not stated).
- (book's words: "the heart of Punjab", "Punjab"; refers to: the recent past to the present, no year stated; book's prose about Pathan Sikh elders, and author's argument): "Moving from tribal areas to the heart of Punjab, an overwhelming majority of them could not even speak the language. They remained confined to their little enclaves" (p. 203, folio not stated). Nankana's Sikh interviewees "remember this internal migration from KPK to Punjab", which gives "a good picture of the different socio-economic conditions in both of these provinces" (p. 235, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "West Punjab"; refers to: the book's present, the November 2011 festival at Nankana; reportage): the Janamasthan kirtan singers "have covered their heads with a loose turban, a popular style in the villages of West Punjab" (p. 230, folio not stated). The book uses "West Punjab" here for a present-day style; it does not define the term.
- (book's words: "Southern Punjab", "Central Punjab", "Punjab"; refers to: the book's present, the author's fieldwork period, no year stated; author's argument, Afterword): "The majority of the Hindu community is based in Sindh and Southern Punjab, where they have a proportionate representation from the lower and the upper castes" (p. 233, folio not stated). Hindus experience "ghettoization" in "Central Punjab where I conducted my research"; "My perception is that the conditions under which members of the Hindu community live in Central Punjab are the harshest compared to the other regions" (p. 233, folio not stated). "Almost all the interviews I conducted were of Hindus belonging to the lower castes because there are hardly any high-caste Hindus in this region", so his observations "from Central Punjab" cannot be "construed as being reflective of Hindu communities in areas where there is a considerable population" (pp. 233–234, folio not stated). "The majority of the Christian minority is based in Punjab", so his Christian material "can be generalized to be reflective of the larger Christian community in the country" (p. 234, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the first half of the 11th century", before partition; the book does not state the unit's extent; book's historical prose): "Al-Beruni came to India during the first half of the 11th century, after the Muslim invader Shah Mahmood Ghaznvi attacked Punjab" (p. 44, folio not stated).
- (book's words: "Punjab", "the land of Punjab", "Sher-e-Punjab"; refers to: the reign of "Maharaja Ranjit Singh (1780-1839)", with "1799" entry to Lahore and "1801" coronation, before partition; the book does not state the unit's extent; book's historical prose and author's argument): "Maharaja Ranjit Singh (1780-1839), the erstwhile ruler of Punjab" (p. 42, folio not stated); "the Sikh ruler of Punjab" (pp. 159, 196, folio not stated); "the Sikh king who ruled Punjab before the British took over" (p. 206, folio not stated); "crowned as the Ruler of Punjab" in 1801 (p. 207, folio not stated). "In the land of Punjab which was for over a millenium [as printed] ruled by Afghans, Mughals and other non-Punjabis, Ranjit Singh was the first Punjabi to rule Punjab"; he renovated "many Gurdwaras all over Punjab" and brought stability "in Punjab which lasted a good forty years" (p. 208, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: British rule, before partition: "the middle of the nineteenth century", "almost a century after the colonization of Bengal", "almost immediately after his [Ranjit Singh's] death"; no year given; the book does not state the unit's extent; book's historical prose): the British "arrival, which in Punjab was in the middle of the nineteenth century"; "the nineteenth century, which is also known as the Sikh period" (p. 57, folio not stated). "Punjab was colonized almost a century after the colonization of Bengal by the British, as a result of which English language is not as institutionalized here" (p. 105, folio not stated). "As a result of sectarian divisions between the Sikh communities and the takeover of Punjab by the British, a large number of important Sikh Gurdwaras were being administered by Hindu mahants" (p. 189, folio not stated). "Punjab became a British colony almost immediately after his death eventually leading to its division at the time of Partition"; Dalip Singh was "the last Maharaja of Punjab, before the British took over" (p. 209, folio not stated).

## From Haroon Khalid, In Search of Shiva

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/khalid_in_search_of_shiva.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's "the most important shrine of Lahore, Data Darbar". One sentence (ch. 7). The row is also in the Lahore place-level group; "Data Darbar" is not named anywhere else in the book's material.

- **Practices & Events**
  - "On 1 July 2010 three bomb blasts rocked the most important shrine of Lahore, Data Darbar, resulting in the death of five people and injuring more than hundred and seventy-five" (book's historical prose) (p. 13, ch. 7, folio not stated).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- "Data Darbar" is never named in chunk 004; no `data-darbar` material beyond the Lahore head.

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's material headed "Lahore (place-level: 35 rows)", copied into every existing entry that heading lists (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

Place-level under ruling (a): the book's "Lahore", "the city", "the walled city", in seven of the nine chunks (not 002 or 009). **Judgement call:** much of this is thin, so the incidental items are grouped last. No archive site is named in this material. The "historic Hindu temple in Lahore" (ch. 4) and the "famous Sufi shrine in Lahore" where drugs were banned (ch. 5) are unnamed and are **not** assigned to any row. Bare route mentions ("a journey of about four hours" from Lahore, "about an hour from Lahore") are not counted here.

- **Shrines and the state** (book's historical prose)
  - "Recently the Punjab government banned the consumption of drugs at a famous Sufi shrine in Lahore to control the law and order situation there, but in doing so it permanently altered the culture of the shrine, developed over thousands of years" (shrine not named) (p. 11, ch. 5, folio not stated).
  - The author once petitioned "with a group of prominent architects from the city to save the destruction of a historic Hindu temple in Lahore that was being encroached upon by the local populace". One architect asked him "if it was un-Islamic to save a Hindu temple", citing the conquest of Mecca and the Prophet's destruction of the idols. The temple is not named (p. 10, ch. 4, folio not stated).
- **The walled city and the Eid Milad-Un-Nabi procession** (book's historical prose)
  - "In 1935, a group of about a hundred people left the premises of the historical Wazir Khan Mosque, constructed in the seventeenth century by Wazir Khan, Mughal Emperor Shahjahan's governor of Lahore, singing praises in honour of the Prophet of Islam" and walked "around the alleys of the walled city" (p. 13, ch. 7, folio not stated).
  - The mosque's prayer leader, "Maulana Syed Dedar Shah", is quoted: "If the Hindus can arrange festivals and hold processions for their fake gods then can't we ... do the same for our Prophet [PBUH]". "Most of his followers rejected his idea"; "The Deobands, who by now had established themselves as a formidable religious force in the city, opposed the idea" (p. 13, ch. 7, folio not stated).
  - "Soon after a man called Karam Elahi, also a resident of Lahore, organized his own little procession", which merged into the Imam's. "Inayatullah Qadri, another religious scholar, started holding an annual Mehafal-e-Millad" and would "join the group from Wazir Khan" (this Inayatullah Qadri is not the archive's Shah Inayat Qadiri). The event "crossed the boundaries of the walled city and spread to neighbouring villages and towns" (p. 13, ch. 7, folio not stated).
  - The procession "had to be moved outside Delhi Gate, where a permanent platform was constructed"; "The junction where the procession converged every year got the name of Millad Chowk" (p. 13, ch. 7, folio not stated).
  - "the walled city of Lahore, of which Bhatti gate is one of the twelve entry points". The shrine of cats ("Billiyon Wala Mazaar", not an archive row) is "next to Bhatti gate"; the author: "The fact that the shrine is located in the heart of Lahore, a major metropolitan city, is probably why this practice has diminished much faster" (p. 12, ch. 6, folio not stated). (See also the Bhatti gate group.)
- **Old villages, graveyards and DHA** (reportage and book's historical prose)
  - DHA: "the neatest and most expensive residential area of Lahore, the Defense Housing Authority (DHA)"; "Recently constructed, the DHA sprawls across a vast tract of land, eating into the small historic villages in the area"; the villages, "Congested and inflicted with poverty", "have been trapped within a wall", having sold "their abundant land for peanuts to the housing authority" (p. 12, ch. 6, folio not stated). In the DHA village "Amar Sadhu" stand Gurdwara Bibi Kaulan (see the Hargobind group) and the shrine of "Baba Mastan Shah" (not an archive row) (p. 12, ch. 6, folio not stated).
  - "this historical graveyard situated in Sultan Pura, the heart of Lahore", seat of the shrine of Ghore Shah (not an archive row): on a Sunday morning families gathered at graves and children played cricket, and junkies "often end up finding a permanent abode in this land of the dead" (p. 12, ch. 6, folio not stated).
  - "the historic village of Hanjarwal, a village which had been subsumed by the ever-growing metropolis of Lahore. It had seen its share of violence in the past" (p. 7, ch. 1, folio not stated).
  - "In Lahore, I met a man who claimed to have spent forty days inside a grave" (in a passage on chila) (p. 11, ch. 5, folio not stated).
- **Ahmad Shah Abdali's siege** (book's historical prose, ch. 9)
  - The chapter opens: "a small shrine in the outskirts of Lahore raised by the villagers there tell a different story" (p. 15, ch. 9, folio not stated).
  - "Before Ahmad Shah could reach Lahore, the prize possession of Punjab, Meer Manu met him at a place near the city of Gujrat". On the later raid, "Ahmad Shah this time had every intention of capturing Lahore" and bypassed the governor; "the forces of Ahmad Shah Abdali reached Lahore, and laid a siege on it. For months the citizens were trapped within the walls of their city". His forces "would attack villages and towns in the outskirt of Lahore" to replenish stock, and "an informant from within the city" told him of the Sikh village of Maraka "a few kilometres from Lahore". The year is given as "the 1751 attack by Ahmad Shah Abdali" (p. 15, ch. 9, folio not stated).
- **Religion, protest and violence in the present** (reportage)
  - "A few days earlier, a group of lawyers along with members of religious parties had attacked the US consulate in Lahore, taking down its flag and burning it" (p. 7, ch. 1, folio not stated).
  - At Hanjarwal on the national protest day against *Innocence of Muslims*: "Temporary gallows had been erected on the road", "A mob of hundreds" amid "the smoke of burning tyres", and "An effigy, dressed in a shirt with the US flag on it, was being dragged to the gallows" for "a public hanging" (p. 7, ch. 1, folio not stated).
  - "Just a couple of days prior to our visit to Chiniot, an angry mob of Muslims in Lahore had burned down 170 Christian houses". The rage was "ignited by an argument between two friends, a Christian and a Muslim, while they were enjoying their evening drinks"; "the 'blasphemous' Christian was already in police custody" before the mob burned "the Christian colony" (p. 16, ch. 10, folio not stated).
  - Raiwind is "a small village in the outskirts of Lahore", "about an hour's journey away"; during the annual congregation, cars "head towards Raiwind, causing a major traffic jam at one of the junctions I regularly use" (p. 10, ch. 4, folio not stated).
- **Thin / incidental** (the judgement-call items; weigh accordingly)
  - Ch. 1 park: on the protest holiday (the author first heard of it "on Thursday, 20 September"), he jogs in "a quintessential middle-class park, located in a middle-class locality, in the heart of Lahore"; the walkers "come from the surrounding houses, newly built by their children who are working as managers or administrators in big factories and offices". Talk on the track "revolves around religion and politics": the men "usually talk about the corruption of politicians" and the women "prefer to discuss spiritual matters"; some carry "rosary beads", others play "an Arabic recitation of the Holy Book, along with its Urdu translation, a public display of their religiosity" (p. 7, ch. 1, folio not stated).
  - Ch. 4 informants' Lahore: Mussadiq's house is "located in the heart of Defence Housing Authority (DHA, Lahore) considered to be the elitist locality in the city"; his friend Akhtar is "the son of a maulvi of a mosque in DHA"; Mussadiq works in his father's "car spare-parts shop on McLeod Road. This is a multi-million rupee business" (p. 10, ch. 4, folio not stated).
  - Ch. 5: Iqbal Qaiser "was working on an encyclopedia of Lahore, the first of its kind. Divided into five different parts, each about 1,000 pages", drawn from land revenue records and government archives on "several villages in and around the district of Lahore", until "administrative changes at the Punjab Cultural Institute put an untimely end to his project"; "Till 1972, Chunian district was part of Lahore" (p. 11, ch. 5, folio not stated).
  - Ch. 6: Bhera woodwork, "specimens of which are also present in the Lahore museum" (p. 12, ch. 6, folio not stated).
  - Ch. 8 school poster (borderline): the author "was working at an elite school in Lahore, one that has more than a hundred branches all over the country" and saw a poster "captioned 'Muslim heroes'" with "Muhammad bin Qasim, Mahmud Ghazni and Ahmad Shah Abdali" (p. 14, ch. 8, folio not stated).
  - Ch. 10: a friend from Mumbai visiting Pakistan said: "I was surprised to see that hardly any women in Lahore were wearing a burqa" (p. 16, ch. 10, folio not stated).
  - Ch. 10 LUMS ("Lahore University of Management Sciences"; institution- rather than place-level): "The School of Social Sciences and Humanities has played a major role in protecting the secular culture of the university from the onslaught of fundamentalist Islamists"; "there have been rumours about the religious right slowly dominating the administration of the university"; "The number of students at the university who have a beard or wear an abaya has increased considerably"; Dr Sadaf Ahmad receives emails from students who say "Our faith is not strong enough at this stage" to study Anthropology of Religion (p. 16, ch. 10, folio not stated).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- **Scope of the book** (book's words: "central Punjab", "southern Punjab"; period: the author's research travels, "spread over a few years", years not stated; author's own account): "Since this was mostly a self-funded project I could only limit my research around my hometown of Lahore. Therefore only parts of central Punjab are included and southern Punjab and Sindh are excluded, where there are many such shrines. These shrines also represent similar characteristics as discussed in this book" (p. 6, Introduction, folio not stated).
- **Indus Valley comparison** (book's words: "present day Punjab, Pakistan"; period: the Indus Valley past compared with the present; author's argument): the Indus Valley as "an agriculture-intensive society … (similar to present day Punjab, Pakistan)", where the fertility cult has "particular significance" (p. 8, ch. 2, folio not stated).
- **Canal colonies** (book's words: "Punjab", "the five rivers of Punjab"; period: "during the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries", British rule; book's historical prose): "Jungli, which literally means 'people of the jungle', is a popular term used to refer to the indigenous Punjabis, as opposed to those who settled here after the construction of the canal colonies by the British during the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries"; "the British connected the five rivers of Punjab with each other through canals, turning several acres of jungle land into agricultural land"; "thousands of new villages, referred to as chaks were established, and farming families from all over Punjab were invited to settle"; former inhabitants "were primarily nomads" unable "to prove ownership over land"; "Their land was snatched away and allotted to these migrants" (p. 8, ch. 2, folio not stated).
- **Partition migration** (book's words: "the division of Punjab between India and Pakistan"; period: "the riots of 1947" and after; author's argument): "the wave of the second migration that took place after the division of Punjab between India and Pakistan"; locals "generally tend to be more embracive of practices 'non-Muslims'" than migrants; "I have seen several gurdwaras and temples being taken over and destroyed by the migrants"; where the population is largely indigenous "such 'non-Muslim' religious spaces are better looked after", with "exceptions" (p. 8, ch. 2, folio not stated). The generalisation is said of "the locals all over the country", framed by Punjab's division.
- **Tableeghi reform, early twentieth century** (book's words: "undivided rural Punjab", "Punjab"; period: early twentieth century, the Arya Samaj / Shuddhi era; book's historical prose): Muhammad Ilyas, founder of the Tableeghi Jamaat, "worked in undivided rural Punjab, exhorting Muslims to 'purify' their religious practices which he argued were corrupted due to the influence of the dominant Hindu culture"; Muslim reformists like Ilyas "founded reformist movements of their own, aggravating the communal tension in Punjab" (p. 10, ch. 4, folio not stated).
- **Tableeghi reach, present** (book's words: "southern Punjab", "the major cities of Punjab"; period: the present of the author's visits; author's argument): Tableeghi preachers "focus on southern Punjab and rural Sindh where syncretistic religion held sway"; "The Tableeghi Jamaat has managed to permeate the social fabric of the major cities of Punjab"; so "shrines similar to the ones at Kamalia and Apal Muri are likely to face a major onslaught due to their 'impure' religious practices, in coming years" (p. 10, ch. 4, folio not stated).
- **Rural social life** (book's words: "rural Punjab"; period: the present of the author's visit; author's reportage and inference): visitors at the Apal Muri banyan left mobile numbers, "probably hoping to initiate a secret love affair in this highly monitored social life of rural Punjab" (p. 9, ch. 3, folio not stated).
- **Folk culture: rivers and love legends** (book's words: "the folk culture of Punjab", "Punjabi culture", "love legends of Punjab"; period not stated / "For centuries"; which side of the border not stated; book's prose and the author speaking):
  - "The Chenab, in the folk culture of Punjab, is known as the river of love" (Sohni and Mahiwal; Takth Hazara of Ranjha) (p. 9, ch. 3, folio not stated).
  - "Isn't Heer Ranjha part of Punjabi culture? It is the most celebrated folk story here. For centuries it has been sung and dramatized" (the author, in conversation) (p. 16, ch. 10, folio not stated).
